## Chapter 4: The Documented Lied Breakthrough 1814-1817

There is a date that Schubert scholars treat almost the way physicists treat a discovery: October 19, 1814. That is the day the seventeen-year-old Schubert finished "Gretchen am Spinnrade," and it is the day, by wide agreement, that the modern German art-song was born. The Symphony No. 1 of the previous chapter showed a gifted student writing skilled imitations of Haydn and Mozart. "Gretchen am Spinnrade," written a year later, shows something with no real precedent: a song in which voice, piano, and poem fuse into a single living thing that did not exist in music before. This chapter anchors on that song and on the explosion of song-writing it opened, the "miracle year" of 1815 and the years of unhappy schoolteaching that surrounded it.

If "Erlkönig" (Chapter 2) was the loud announcement, "Gretchen" is the quiet revolution. It is gentler, sadder, and in some ways more astonishing, because it does so much with so little: one girl, one spinning wheel, one mounting wave of memory and longing. Where "Erlkönig" dramatizes an external story, "Gretchen" dramatizes an interior state, and the interior song is the one Schubert would spend his life perfecting. This is the song to understand if you want to understand what Schubert added to music.

### 4.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote

Vienna, October 1814. Schubert is seventeen, in his first months as an assistant teacher in his father's school, a job he took partly to avoid conscription into the army and partly because it was the future his father had planned. He spends his days drilling small children in the alphabet and his evenings and stolen hours composing at a pace that defies belief. He has been reading Goethe, the great enthusiasm of his circle of young friends, and he turns to the scene in _Faust_ where Gretchen, the innocent girl Faust has seduced, sits alone at her spinning wheel and pours out her longing and dread.

The poem is short, a handful of stanzas, and it has a refrain: "My peace is gone, my heart is heavy; I shall never, never find peace again." Schubert sets it not as a pretty strophic song but as a single rising arc of obsession, with the piano spinning the wheel underneath from the first bar to the last. And he does something at the climax that no one had done before, which we will come to in the Listening Window. The result is a song that a seventeen-year-old, in a schoolroom-adjacent garret, simply invented, with almost nothing in the prior literature to build on. His friend Josef von Spaun later wrote that the friends knew at once that something new had appeared.

The context makes the achievement stranger, not less strange. This was not the work of a mature artist in a studio. It was written by a teenager doing a job he hated, in a household with no money, in stolen time. And it opened a floodgate: over the next fifteen months, through 1815, Schubert wrote more than 140 songs, on some documented days finishing several in an afternoon. "Gretchen am Spinnrade" is where the flood begins.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen

  1. **The spinning wheel in the piano.** From the first bar, the pianist's right hand plays a continuous, circling figure, fast, even, slightly whirring, that is unmistakably the turning of a spinning wheel; underneath, the left hand taps a steady recurring note that is the wheel's treadle, the foot-pedal that drives it. This figure runs through almost the entire song. As in "Erlkönig," the piano _is_ the central image. Notice that it never quite settles; the wheel keeps turning, the anxiety keeps circling.

  2. **The voice rising in waves.** Gretchen's vocal line is built as a series of mounting waves. She states the refrain ("My peace is gone"), then each new stanza of memory and longing climbs a little higher and grows a little more intense, before the refrain returns to reset her. Listen for this wave-shape: rise, intensify, fall back to the refrain, rise again higher. The form mirrors the psychology of obsession, the mind circling back to the same wound, each time more raw.

  3. **The moment the wheel stops.** Near the song's climax, at the memory of Faust's kiss, the spinning figure in the piano falters and stops, for just a moment, the wheel literally stops turning. This is the single most famous stroke in the song, and one of the most famous in all of Lieder. Listen for it: the constant whirring breaks off, there is a catch, and then the wheel slowly, haltingly starts up again. We will return to why this is so extraordinary.

#### One-sentence background

"Gretchen am Spinnrade," D 118, is a through-composed song for solo voice and piano composed on October 19, 1814, when Schubert was seventeen, setting the spinning-wheel monologue from Goethe's _Faust_ Part I in which the seduced and abandoned Gretchen pours out her longing; it runs about three and a half to four minutes, is built over a continuous spinning-wheel figure in the piano, and is widely regarded as the first fully mature Schubert Lied and a founding work of the modern art-song; the **Primary Recording** for this chapter is Elly Ameling with the pianist Dalton Baldwin (recorded for Philips in the 1970s as part of their Schubert song series), chosen for the soprano's purity of line and the clarity with which the spinning figure and its famous stop come through.

#### Search Hints

EN: "Schubert Gretchen am Spinnrade Elly Ameling Dalton Baldwin" or "Schubert Gretchen am Spinnrade D 118" ZH: "舒伯特 纺车旁的格雷琴 阿美玲 鲍德温" 或 "舒伯特 纺车旁的玛格丽特 D118" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

**Alternative recordings** within the recording-hallucination safeguard (≥10 years old OR ≥3 reviews):

* Elisabeth Schwarzkopf with the pianist Edwin Fischer or Gerald Moore (EMI, mid-twentieth century), a classic, intensely characterized reading from the older school; the word-coloring is more pointed, the manner more dramatic.
* Janet Baker with the pianist Gerald Moore (EMI, 1970s), a warm mezzo reading of great emotional weight; the lower voice darkens Gretchen's longing.
* Barbara Bonney with the pianist Geoffrey Parsons (Teldec, 1990s), a clear, youthful-sounding modern reading that emphasizes Gretchen's innocence.

### 4.2 The Listening Window

This section is for your second listen. Timestamps below refer to the Ameling / Baldwin recording named in §4.1, in which the song runs about three and a half minutes. If your recording differs, use the description; the famous "stop" is the surest landmark.

**0:00. The wheel starts, then Gretchen's refrain.** The piano begins immediately with the spinning figure, the whirring right hand, the treadle-tap in the left. After a bar or two, Gretchen enters with the refrain: "Meine Ruh ist hin, mein Herz ist schwer", my peace is gone, my heart is heavy. The vocal line is restrained, almost numb, the statement of a settled grief. The wheel turns under it. The key is D minor, the same key as "Erlkönig," dark and unsettled.

**About 0:30. First wave: memory begins.** Gretchen moves from the refrain into the first stanza of memory: she thinks of him, of how the world is a grave without him, how her poor head and senses are unsettled. The line begins to climb and warm slightly. The wheel keeps turning. Listen for the first small rise in intensity.

**About 0:55. The refrain returns.** "My peace is gone" comes back, resetting her. This return is the song's recurring anchor; each time she circles back to it after a wave of feeling. Notice that it is the same music as the opening, but it now carries the weight of what has come between.

**About 1:10. Second wave: his image.** Now she pictures him, his fine walk, his noble figure, the smile of his mouth, the power of his eyes. The vocal line climbs higher than before; the harmony warms toward major as the memory turns from grief to desire. The wheel quickens slightly with her pulse. The waves are getting higher.

**About 1:45. The refrain, more troubled.** The refrain returns again, but the surrounding harmony is less settled now; the obsession is tightening.

**About 2:05. Third wave: toward the climax.** She thinks of his hand-clasp, and then, "und ach, sein Kuss", and ah, his kiss. The line surges upward in the song's biggest wave, the most intense music yet, the memory of physical contact driving the voice to its peak.

**About 2:25. The wheel stops.** At the word _Kuss_ (kiss), the spinning figure in the piano breaks off. The wheel stops. There is a moment of held silence and a high, exposed note in the voice. This is the famous stroke. Gretchen, overwhelmed by the memory of the kiss, has literally stopped spinning, her body has forgotten its task. Then, slowly and haltingly, the piano starts the wheel again, a few hesitant turns, gathering, until the spinning resumes its steady whir. The interruption lasts only seconds, but it is the emotional center of the song: the one moment the relentless mechanism of her grief is broken by the force of the memory.

**About 2:45. The wheel rebuilds; the final wave.** The spinning re-establishes itself and the voice climbs again toward the kiss-memory, even more intensely, "his kiss," she sings, longing now to be lost in it, "und kü", and to kiss him, "as I would, his kisses should be my death." This is the highest, most abandoned point of the song.

**About 3:10. The refrain, and the unresolved end.** The song returns one last time to the refrain, "my peace is gone, my heart is heavy", and the wheel turns on. Crucially, the song does not resolve into peace; it ends with the wheel still turning and the grief unhealed, trailing off rather than concluding. Listen for the lack of a settled, consoling ending. The wheel will keep turning after the music stops; the obsession has no exit.

### 4.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### The piano-figure as psychological image

"Gretchen am Spinnrade" is the purest demonstration of Schubert's central invention: the piano figure that is simultaneously a physical object and a state of mind. The spinning wheel is literally a spinning wheel, you can hear the wheel and the treadle. But it is also Gretchen's circling, obsessive grief, the mind that returns and returns to the same wound and cannot stop. The genius is that these two meanings are the same sound. Schubert does not first depict the wheel and then, separately, depict the emotion; the wheel-figure _is_ the emotion, because obsession feels like a wheel that will not stop turning.

This fusion is what the older strophic song could not do. A pre-Schubert setting of this poem would have given Gretchen a pretty tune, repeated for each stanza, with chords underneath. It could depict her sadness in general. It could not make you feel the specific texture of obsessive grief, the relentlessness, the circling, the way a single memory keeps ambushing the mind. Schubert's spinning figure does exactly that, and it does it continuously, in real time, under everything she sings. When you listen to Schubert's songs across this volume, this is the first and most important thing to track: find the piano figure, identify the physical thing it depicts, and then feel for the emotional state that is the same sound. The brook in _Die schöne Müllerin_ , the frozen trudge of _Winterreise_ , the rustling leaves of "Wanderers Nachtlied", all are descendants of Gretchen's wheel.

#### The stopped wheel: word, body, and music as one

The moment the wheel stops at the word _Kuss_ is worth dwelling on because it shows the new art-song operating at full power. Three things happen at once, and they are inseparable. The _word_ is "kiss," the memory of the most intense physical contact. The _body_ is Gretchen's: overwhelmed, she stops spinning, her hands forget their work. And the _music_ is the piano: the continuous figure breaks off, leaving the voice exposed on a high note in sudden silence. Word, body, and music fuse into a single dramatic event. You do not need the German, or even the staging, to feel it; the breaking of the relentless figure tells you that something has overwhelmed her.

And then the slow, halting restart of the wheel is just as fine. Gretchen gathers herself; her hands remember; the treadle taps again; the wheel turns. But it costs her an effort, and you hear the effort in the music's hesitation. The depiction of a person pulling herself back from being overwhelmed, in a few seconds of faltering piano figuration, is the kind of psychological precision that simply did not exist in song before Schubert. This is the moment to point to when someone asks what Schubert added to music. He added the interior life, rendered in real time, through the partnership of voice and piano.

#### Through-composed, but with a refrain: form following feeling

"Gretchen" is through-composed in the sense that the music develops continuously rather than repeating a fixed strophe, but it is not formless. It is organized by the refrain, "my peace is gone", which returns several times, and by the waves of rising intensity between the refrains. The form is the shape of obsession: a stable, numb refrain, then a wave of memory that climbs and intensifies, then collapse back to the refrain, then a higher wave, and so on, until the highest wave breaks at the kiss and the wheel stops.

This is a different formal solution from "Erlkönig," which was a continuous escalation with no refrain. The two breakthrough songs show Schubert, at seventeen and eighteen, already commanding two distinct dramatic architectures: the relentless single arc ("Erlkönig") and the wave-and-refrain ("Gretchen"). In the next chapters you will watch him add a third resource, the strophic and modified-strophic forms in which the same music returns for each verse but is subtly altered to track the changing meaning. Schubert's mastery of song is, in large part, his mastery of _choosing_ the right form for each poem, and of bending the form to the poem's psychology. "Gretchen am Spinnrade" is the first complete demonstration of that mastery.

#### The harmonic warming and the unresolved end

Two harmonic details reward attention. First, listen again to how the harmony _warms_ during the waves of memory, especially when Gretchen pictures Faust's figure and smile (around 1:10). The song is in dark D minor, but as desire enters the memory, Schubert slides toward warmer, major-tinged harmony, the music blushing as she remembers. Then, as the refrain returns and grief reasserts itself, the harmony cools back to minor. This major-minor shading, brightening for desire, darkening for grief, is an early instance of one of Schubert's lifelong fingerprints, the expressive switch between major and minor, which Chapter 18 will collect across the catalogue (Threading B).

Second, the ending refuses resolution. A conventional song would close with a sense of arrival, a clear cadence, a settling. "Gretchen" ends with the wheel still turning and the refrain unhealed; the music does not resolve so much as trail off, leaving Gretchen exactly where she began, in inescapable grief. This open, unresolved ending is itself expressive: there is no exit from her obsession, so the music gives her none. Schubert understood, at seventeen, that how a song _ends_ is a dramatic choice, and that sometimes the truthful ending is no ending at all.

### 4.4 The Stories

#### The miracle year, 1815

"Gretchen am Spinnrade" was finished in October 1814; the flood it opened crested the following year. In 1815 Schubert wrote, by the standard count, more than 140 songs, along with two symphonies, two Masses, several stage works, and a great deal else, all while working full-time as a schoolteacher. The numbers are so extreme that they invite disbelief, but the dated manuscripts support them: on some documented days he completed several songs, sketching, setting, and finishing at a speed that no other major composer's documented output approaches. "Erlkönig," "Heidenröslein," "Der Wanderer," and dozens of other enduring songs date from this single year.

What was happening? Partly it was sheer youthful fertility meeting a poet's gift; Schubert had found, in the German lyric poetry his friends were devouring, an inexhaustible supply of texts that ignited him. Partly it was the new device, the piano-as-image, the through-composed and modified-strophic forms, which once discovered could be applied to poem after poem. And partly, one suspects, it was an escape: the songs were composed in the cracks of a hated job, and the act of composing was the part of his life that was wholly his. The miracle year is not a tidy story with a cause; it is a documented phenomenon, a teenager pouring out masterpieces while teaching the alphabet, and the honest thing is to register its strangeness rather than explain it away.

#### The unhappy schoolteacher

The biographical frame of these years is drudgery. Schubert took the assistant-teacher post in his father's school around 1814, at seventeen, and held it, miserably, until about 1817, when he finally broke away to try to live as a freelance composer. He was, by the accounts that survive, not a good or a willing teacher; his heart was entirely in composition, and the daily grind of the schoolroom was an obstacle to be endured and stolen from. The decision to leave teaching, around 1817, was a decision to give up security for art, a precarious freelance life supported by friends and occasional commissions, with no salaried position and no patron. It set the pattern for the rest of his short life: Schubert never held a regular musical post, never had a wealthy patron in the way Haydn or even Beethoven did, and lived on publication fees, teaching, and the generosity of his circle.

This precariousness is part of the "unknown Schubert" half-truth the book keeps returning to. He was not unknown; he was published and admired in Vienna. But he never achieved the financial security or the public, continental fame that a salaried position or a major patron could have brought. He fell between the old patronage system, which was dying, and the new world of the freelance Romantic artist living off the public, which was not yet able to support a song-composer. The schoolteacher years are where this lifelong precariousness begins.

#### The circle of friends

The other thing that formed in these years was the circle. Around the young Schubert gathered a remarkable group of friends, the poet and law student Johann Mayrhofer, the poet and bon vivant Franz von Schober, the painter Moritz von Schwind, the singer Johann Michael Vogl, the civil servant and early champion Josef von Spaun, and others. They were young, educated, artistic, and, in Metternich's censored Vienna, turned inward toward private cultivation. They read poetry together, argued about art, drank, and, above all, gathered to hear Schubert's music in private rooms, evenings that came to be called Schubertiades. The circle was Schubert's real audience, his support system, sometimes literally his source of food and lodging, and his first publishers of opinion. Much of his music was written for them and first heard by them. We will meet the Schubertiade circle again in the chamber-music chapters (especially Chapter 12), where its in-a-room intimacy is the social context for the _Trout_ Quintet and the quartets.

#### Recording history briefly

The recommended starting point is Elly Ameling's pure-toned, beautifully shaped reading; for a more sharply word-pointed older-school approach, Elisabeth Schwarzkopf, and for a warmer, weightier mezzo, Janet Baker. The work-specific point: the song lives or dies on the partnership of voice and piano, and the spinning figure and its famous stop must be _shaped_ , not merely played, so the accompanist matters as much as the singer. (The full Lieder lineage, and the place of the complete-songs projects, is in Chapter 20.)

#### Cross-piece links you will hear later

"Gretchen am Spinnrade" connects forward across the whole volume.

To "Erlkönig" (Chapter 2), its companion breakthrough, with which it shares the founding device, the piano figure that is the song's central image, and the dark key of D minor. The two songs, written within a year of each other by a teenager, between them establish almost everything Schubert's song-writing would become.

To the Goethe settings (Chapter 5), where "Gretchen" sits as the supreme example of Schubert setting Goethe, and where the poignant non-relationship with Goethe is taken up in full.

To _Die schöne Müllerin_ and _Winterreise_ (Chapters 6 and 7), the great cycles, where the piano-as-image (the brook, the winter wind) and the journeying or obsessive psychology are expanded from a single song into vast narrative spans. Gretchen at her wheel is, in a sense, the first of Schubert's lonely, longing protagonists, the line runs straight from her to the miller and the winter wanderer.

#### The 1814-1817 context (Thread A note)

These years, 1814 to 1817, are the hinge of Schubert's early life: from Stadtkonvikt student to schoolteacher to fledgling freelance composer. He is between seventeen and twenty. He is composing at superhuman speed; he is teaching children and hating it; he is forming the friendships that will sustain and define him; and he is, around 1817, making the fateful decision to give up the schoolroom for the uncertain life of a composer. The first maturity that follows, 1817 to 1822, will bring the _Trout_ Quintet and the _Wanderer_ Fantasy (Chapters 12 and 9) before the 1822 illness changes everything. But it all begins here, in the schoolroom-and-garret years, with a continuous figure in the piano and a girl at a spinning wheel whose peace is gone. Chapter 5 stays with the Lieder and takes up Schubert's lifelong engagement with the poetry of Goethe, of which "Gretchen" and "Erlkönig" are the towering early monuments.

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find the Elly Ameling / Dalton Baldwin recording (or one of the alternatives in §4.1). Listen to "Gretchen am Spinnrade" and attend to these four things.

  1. **The spinning figure as obsession (from 0:00).** Fix the continuous whirring right hand and the treadle-tap in the left in your ear. Then try to hear it two ways at once: as a literal spinning wheel, and as the circling, relentless motion of obsessive grief. The chapter's central claim is that these are the same sound. Test it against your own ear.

  2. **The rising waves and the resetting refrain.** Track the structure: the numb refrain ("my peace is gone"), then a wave of memory that climbs and intensifies, then a fall back to the refrain, then a higher wave. Notice how the harmony warms toward major as desire enters the memory and cools back to minor as grief returns.

  3. **The stopped wheel at "Kuss" (about 2:25).** This is the moment to listen for above all. The spinning breaks off; the voice hangs exposed; then the wheel restarts slowly and haltingly. Listen for the effort of the restart, the depiction of a person pulling herself back from being overwhelmed. This is the song's emotional center and one of the supreme strokes in all of Lieder.

  4. **The unresolved ending (from about 3:10).** Listen to how the song closes, or rather refuses to close: the refrain returns, the wheel turns on, and the music trails off without a settled, consoling cadence. Sit with the lack of resolution and ask what it tells you about Gretchen's state.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** (the pairing): "Erlkönig," D 328 (Chapter 2), back to back with "Gretchen." The two breakthrough songs, written within a year of each other, share the founding device (the piano figure as central image) and the key of D minor, but solve different dramatic problems: "Erlkönig" an external story told as relentless escalation, "Gretchen" an interior state told as waves of obsession. Hearing them as a pair is the best short course there is in what the teenage Schubert invented.

**Listen** (the range): "Heidenröslein," D 257, also from 1815, a simple, charming, strophic Goethe setting that sounds like a folk song. Heard against "Gretchen," it shows how wide Schubert's response could be even within a single year and a single poet: the same composer who wrote the obsessive, through-composed "Gretchen" wrote the artless little "Heidenröslein," and meant both. Chapter 5 takes up "Heidenröslein" in detail.

**Read** : Susan Youens has written extensively on Schubert's Goethe settings; her work is the scholarly source-of-record for the Lieder and is cited throughout Part III of this volume. For an accessible entry, Graham Johnson's commentary on "Gretchen am Spinnrade" in the Hyperion Schubert Edition notes sets the song in the context of the 1814 breakthrough and the _Faust_ source.

**Read** (the source): the Gretchen scenes from Goethe's _Faust_ Part I, in any good translation. Understanding where Gretchen is in the drama, an innocent girl seduced and abandoned, sitting alone at her wheel, heading toward catastrophe, deepens the song immeasurably. Schubert set only the spinning-wheel monologue, but the whole tragedy is behind it, and hearing the song with the drama in mind is one of the clearest demonstrations of how much a great Lied can carry.

* * *

## Chapter 5: Schubert and Goethe — the Documented Settings

You have now heard the two towering Goethe settings of Schubert's youth, "Erlkönig" and "Gretchen am Spinnrade," and you have heard, in passing, that Goethe seems never to have acknowledged them. This chapter widens the frame to the whole Schubert-Goethe relationship, which is the central relationship of Schubert's song-writing and one of the most poignant non-meetings in musical history. Schubert set around seventy of Goethe's poems across his life; Goethe, the supreme literary figure of the age, the man Schubert and his friends revered above all others, appears to have registered none of it.

The chapter anchors on four shorter Goethe songs that show the astonishing range of Schubert's response to a single poet: the folk-simple "Heidenröslein," the hushed twin "Wanderers Nachtlied" settings, and the joyous "Der Musensohn." Set beside "Erlkönig" and "Gretchen," they prove that "a Schubert Goethe setting" is not one thing but a whole spectrum, from artless strophic song to harmonic adventure to galloping drama. Learning to hear that range, and to hear _why_ Schubert chose a different formal solution for each poem, is the work of this chapter and the key skill it adds.

### 5.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote

Weimar, 1816. In Vienna, the nineteen-year-old Schubert and his devoted friend Josef von Spaun have prepared something special: a beautifully copied manuscript album of sixteen of Schubert's settings of Goethe's poems, including "Erlkönig" and "Gretchen am Spinnrade," the two greatest songs yet written to the great man's words. Spaun writes a respectful covering letter and sends the album to Goethe in Weimar, hoping the poet will recognize the young composer's gift and perhaps lend his name to a publication. It is an act of homage and hope: the unknown teenager offering his best work to the god of German letters.

Goethe, as far as the record shows, did not reply. The album was returned, or simply went unanswered; the documents are not fully clear, but no word of encouragement, no acknowledgment, reached Vienna. Goethe was then in his late sixties, the most famous writer in Europe, and his taste in song was firmly conservative: he believed a musical setting should serve the poem modestly, staying out of its way, and his model was the plain strophic settings of his friend Carl Friedrich Zelter. Schubert's settings did the opposite. They claimed equal partnership with the words; the piano painted scenes, the music dramatized and through-composed and adventured. To Goethe's ear, if he listened at all, this was very likely exactly the wrong kind of song, music that put itself forward instead of serving.

The contrast with Beethoven sharpens the poignancy. Goethe met Beethoven in person, at the spa town of Teplitz in 1812, and however awkward the encounter (Beethoven was already half-deaf and notoriously prickly), Goethe knew exactly who and what Beethoven was. Schubert he never met, and apparently never noticed, even with the greatest settings his poems would ever receive lying on his desk. Late in life Goethe did hear "Erlkönig" performed (by the singer Wilhelmine Schröder-Devrient, in the early 1830s, after Schubert's death) and is reported to have been moved, but by then it was far too late for the composer to know it. The non-relationship is one of the documented sadnesses of Schubert's career, and it sits behind every Goethe setting in this chapter.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen

Because this chapter anchors on four short songs rather than one large work, the "things to notice" are framed across the set:

  1. **"Heidenröslein": how simple a Schubert song can be.** This is a strophic folk-song setting, the same gentle tune for all three verses, light and almost childlike. After the drama of "Erlkönig" and "Gretchen," its plainness may surprise you. Notice that Schubert can write a song that sounds like an anonymous folk tune, and that the simplicity is a deliberate choice, not a limitation. The dark undertow of the poem (a story of plucking and loss) sits just beneath the cheerful surface.

  2. **"Wanderers Nachtlied II" ("Über allen Gipfeln"): how still a Schubert song can be.** This tiny setting of Goethe's most famous short lyric is almost motionless: a few hushed chords, a quiet vocal line, a long silence near the end. Notice the stillness, and notice one harmonic moment near the close where the music shifts to a distant, glowing place before settling. This is the Schubertian harmonic move (Threading B) in miniature.

  3. **"Der Musensohn": how joyous and fleet a Schubert song can be.** This is fast, dancing, exhilarated, the song of the "son of the Muses" wandering and making music wherever he goes. Notice how the music keeps shifting between two keys, swinging brightly from one to another and back, a restless, delighted motion that depicts the wandering musician's high spirits. This is the same harmonic-shift device as in "Wanderers Nachtlied," used for joy instead of stillness.

#### One-sentence background

This chapter anchors on four Goethe settings spanning Schubert's range: "Heidenröslein," D 257 (1815), a strophic folk-song setting of Goethe's "little wild rose" poem; "Wanderers Nachtlied I," D 224 (1815), and "Wanderers Nachtlied II" ("Über allen Gipfeln"), D 768 (1823), two settings of Goethe's "wanderer's night-song" poems, the second among the most concentrated short songs ever written; and "Der Musensohn," D 764 (1822), a fast, key-shifting song of the wandering musician; together with "Erlkönig" and "Gretchen" (recalled from Chapters 2 and 4) they show the spectrum of Schubert's Goethe settings; the **Primary Recording** for this chapter is Dietrich Fischer-Dieskau with the pianist Gerald Moore (from the Deutsche Grammophon complete Schubert Lieder project), chosen for the consistency of artist across the set and the clarity of the word-music relationship.

#### Search Hints

EN: "Schubert Goethe Lieder Fischer-Dieskau Moore Heidenröslein Wanderers Nachtlied Der Musensohn" or "Schubert Heidenröslein D 257" ZH: "舒伯特 歌德歌曲 费舍尔-迪斯考 摩尔 野玫瑰 流浪者夜歌 缪斯之子" 或 "舒伯特 野玫瑰 D257" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

**Alternative recordings** within the recording-hallucination safeguard (≥10 years old OR ≥3 reviews):

* For "Heidenröslein" and the lighter songs, Elly Ameling (with Dalton Baldwin or Jörg Demus, Philips) brings a fresh, folk-like directness.
* For "Wanderers Nachtlied II," Hans Hotter's deep-voiced, intensely inward reading (with Gerald Moore, EMI) is a classic; the stillness gains weight from the dark voice.
* For "Der Musensohn," Fritz Wunderlich's tenor reading (Deutsche Grammophon, 1960s) is justly famous for its lightness and joy; the song suits a bright, agile voice.
* Graham Johnson's Hyperion Schubert Edition includes all of these with full commentary, sung by a range of artists.

### 5.2 The Listening Window

Because this chapter covers four short songs, the Listening Window treats each in turn. Timings are approximate and refer to the Fischer-Dieskau / Moore readings; these are short songs, so navigate by the song rather than the second.

**"Heidenröslein" (about 2 minutes).** The piano gives a brief, lilting introduction; the voice enters with a simple, rounded tune. The song has three verses, all sung to the same music (strophic form), telling Goethe's little parable: a boy sees a wild rose, the rose warns it will prick him, he plucks it anyway, it pricks, and the song ends. Listen for the deliberate folk-song quality, the regular phrases, the dance-like lilt, the way each verse repeats the tune. And listen, underneath the charm, for the faint darkness: the poem is, on one reading, about thoughtless plucking and the rose's helpless suffering, a small allegory of seduction and loss, and Schubert's setting keeps the surface light while letting the story's edge show through in the slightly insistent ending of each verse.

**"Wanderers Nachtlied I" (about 2 minutes).** This first "night-song" sets a prayer-like poem ("Der du von dem Himmel bist", you who are from heaven). The setting is hushed, slow, and reverent, the vocal line simple and the harmony warm. It is a quiet song of longing for peace and rest. Listen for the contemplative stillness and the gentle, hymn-like quality; it prepares the ear for its more famous companion.

**"Wanderers Nachtlied II" ("Über allen Gipfeln," about 2 minutes).** This is the great one, a setting of Goethe's eight-line lyric about evening stillness: over all the hilltops is peace; in the treetops you feel hardly a breath; the little birds are silent in the wood; wait, soon you too will rest. Schubert's setting is almost motionless. A few quiet, spread chords; a vocal line that barely rises; a sense of held breath. Listen especially for the moment near "Warte nur" (wait only) where the harmony shifts to a distant, glowing key for a few seconds before returning, the music opening a window onto something far away, the "rest" the poem promises, before the hushed close. And listen for the silence Schubert places near the end, a pause that lets the stillness become total. In two minutes the song depicts the descent of evening peace and, behind it, the larger rest the poem quietly invokes.

**"Der Musensohn" (about 2 minutes).** The mood reverses completely: fast, dancing, exhilarated. The "son of the Muses" wanders through the world making music, and everything responds, the fields, the girls, the dance. The piano dances in a quick triple meter; the voice is fleet and high-spirited. The defining feature is the constant shifting between two keys: each verse swings brightly from one key up to another and back, a restless, delighted motion that never settles, depicting the musician's perpetual happy wandering. Listen for that swing, the way the music keeps lifting to a new bright place and dropping back, like a dancer never standing still. It is the same harmonic-shift device as in "Wanderers Nachtlied II," but where there it created a window onto stillness, here it creates ceaseless joyful motion.

### 5.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### The spectrum of a single relationship: one poet, many forms

The four songs of this chapter, set beside the two from Chapters 2 and 4, make a single large point: Schubert had no formula for "a Goethe setting." Each poem got the form its content demanded. "Erlkönig" is through-composed melodrama. "Gretchen" is wave-and-refrain obsession. "Heidenröslein" is artless strophic folk song. "Wanderers Nachtlied II" is hushed near-stillness. "Der Musensohn" is fleet key-shifting joy. The same composer, the same poet, five completely different musical worlds.

This is the chapter's central listening skill: hearing the _match_ between poem and form. When you encounter any Schubert song from here on, ask first what kind of poem it is, a narrative? an interior monologue? a folk lyric? a prayer? a dance?, and then notice the formal solution Schubert chose for it. Is it strophic (same music each verse), modified-strophic (the same music but altered to track changes), or through-composed (continuously new)? The choice is never arbitrary. "Heidenröslein" is strophic because the poem is a folk parable that wants the innocence of repetition; "Erlkönig" is through-composed because the poem escalates and repetition would be absurd; "Wanderers Nachtlied II" is a single arc of stillness because the poem is one held moment. Schubert's genius as a song-composer is, in large part, this unerring sense of form-to-poem fit, and once you start listening for it, the whole repertoire becomes legible.

#### Strophic, modified-strophic, through-composed

It is worth fixing the three formal categories clearly, because they organize all of Part III.

_Strophic_ form sets every verse of the poem to the same music. "Heidenröslein" is the pure case. The strength of strophic form is the folk-song innocence and memorability of repetition; its risk is monotony if the poem's verses change in feeling. Schubert often used strophic form for poems that genuinely want it, folk lyrics, songs of stable mood, and the apparent simplicity conceals real art (the tune must be good enough to bear repetition and right for every verse at once).

_Modified-strophic_ form repeats the music for each verse but alters it, shifting to minor for a darker verse, changing the accompaniment, extending a phrase, when the poem's feeling shifts. This is Schubert's most characteristic middle path, and you will meet it constantly in _Die schöne Müllerin_ and _Winterreise_ : a song that feels like a strophic folk song but bends, verse by verse, to follow the changing emotional weather. It combines the memorability of repetition with the responsiveness of through-composition.

_Through-composed_ form writes continuously new music as the poem unfolds, with little or no repetition. "Erlkönig" is the extreme case. Through-composition suits narrative and escalation; its cost is the loss of the unifying power of a returning tune. Schubert reserved it for poems whose drama demanded it.

Most of Schubert's six hundred songs fall somewhere on this spectrum, and a great deal of the pleasure of listening closely is hearing where each song sits and why. The four anchors of this chapter sample the spectrum's range; the cycles of the next two chapters work it in depth.

#### The harmonic move by thirds: "Wanderers Nachtlied II" and "Der Musensohn"

Two of this chapter's songs put a Schubert fingerprint on clear display: the harmonic shift to a key a third away, what we are calling (Threading B, launched in Chapter 2) the _harmonic move by thirds_ or mediant shift. In conventional Classical harmony, music moves most often between closely related keys, a key and its nearest neighbors. Schubert loved instead to slide to a key a third away, a more distant, more colored relationship that produces a characteristic glow or shadow, the sense of the music stepping sideways into a different light.

In "Wanderers Nachtlied II," this shift, near "Warte nur," opens a window: for a few seconds the music glows in a distant key, and the effect is of peace opening out beyond the immediate stillness, a glimpse of the larger rest the poem promises. In "Der Musensohn," the same kind of shift, the constant swing between two keys a third apart, creates not stillness but ceaseless joyful motion, the wandering musician never settling. The identical harmonic device produces opposite emotional effects depending on context: in one song a held breath, in the other a dance that cannot stop. This is worth dwelling on, because it shows that a Schubert fingerprint is not a mannerism applied for its own sake but a flexible expressive resource. Chapter 18 collects the mediant shift across the catalogue, in the late sonatas, in the String Quintet, where it operates on a huge scale; here, in two tiny songs, you can hear it cleanly and learn to recognize its color.

#### What the strophic simplicity hides: "Heidenröslein"

A word in defense of the simplest song, because listeners trained on the drama of "Erlkönig" sometimes dismiss the little strophic songs as slight. "Heidenröslein" sounds like an anonymous folk tune, so natural and inevitable that it seems it must always have existed; in fact it is Schubert's invention, and that naturalness is the achievement. To write a melody that sounds like a folk song, simple, rounded, instantly memorable, perfectly matched to a light triple lilt, is harder than it looks; most attempts sound contrived. Schubert could do it at will, and the apparent artlessness is a high art.

And the song is not as innocent as its surface. Goethe's poem, on one widely held reading, is a veiled story of seduction and abandonment: the boy who plucks the wild rose despite its protest, and the rose that must "suffer it," carry an undertone of thoughtless male conquest and female helplessness, the same theme, in folk-allegory miniature, as the Gretchen tragedy. Schubert keeps the music light and the surface cheerful, which makes the undertow more unsettling, not less: the catastrophe is sung to a pretty tune, as folk songs so often sing terrible things. Listening for the gap between a song's bright surface and its dark content is one of the subtler pleasures of Schubert, and "Heidenröslein" is the first clear case. The cheerful strophic setting is doing something more complicated than it admits.

### 5.4 The Stories

#### Goethe's conservative ear

Why did Goethe not respond? The fullest answer lies in his theory of song, which was sincere and considered, not mere obtuseness. Goethe believed that in a song the poem must rule and the music must serve. The ideal setting, to his mind, was strophic and simple, a melody that carried the words clearly and did not impose its own drama, leaving the poem's meaning intact and foregrounded. His friend and musical advisor Carl Friedrich Zelter wrote exactly this kind of setting, and Goethe trusted Zelter's judgment in all musical matters. When Schubert's settings arrived, full of pictorial piano writing, through-composed drama, and harmonic adventure, they violated Goethe's whole aesthetic: to him, the music was likely usurping the poem, putting itself forward, "interpreting" where it should have served.

There is a real argument buried here, and it is worth taking seriously rather than simply siding with Schubert. Goethe was not wrong that a powerful musical setting changes a poem, fixes one reading of it, colors it forever with the composer's emotion. After Schubert, it is nearly impossible to read "Erlkönig" without hearing the galloping triplets, or "Gretchen" without the spinning wheel; the song has, in a sense, captured the poem. Whether that is a gift or a theft is a genuine question, and Goethe, jealous for his own words, felt it as something closer to theft. The modern listener, who tends to value exactly the dramatic, interpretive setting Goethe distrusted, should at least understand that the poet's silence came from a coherent position, not mere blindness. (The Conversation Seeds return to this.)

#### "Wait, soon you too will rest"

A small, much-told coda gives "Wanderers Nachtlied II" an almost unbearable resonance. Goethe wrote the eight-line poem in 1780, reportedly pencilling it on the wall of a wooden hunting hut on a hill called the Kickelhahn, near Ilmenau, one evening as the light failed, over all the hilltops is peace. In 1831, an old man of over eighty, the year before his own death, Goethe is said to have returned to that hut, found the lines still on the wall, read them aloud, and wept at "warte nur, balde ruhest du auch", wait, soon you too will rest. The line, written by a young man as an image of evening peace, had become, for the dying poet, a literal promise of the rest of death. Schubert had set it in 1823, in two of the most concentrated minutes in all of song, and he too was within a few years of his own early death. Neither knew the other; both heard, in the same eight lines, the descent of a final stillness. The song is one of the supreme small things in the literature, and the layered approach of death, Goethe's, Schubert's, the poem's, gives it a depth no analysis fully reaches.

#### The seventy settings

Across his life Schubert set around seventy of Goethe's poems, some more than once. The early outpouring of 1815-1816, "Gretchen," "Erlkönig," "Heidenröslein," "Wanderers Nachtlied I," "Rastlose Liebe," "Der Wanderer" (a different poem from the later "Wanderers Nachtlied," and the source of the _Wanderer_ Fantasy in Chapter 9), and many more, is the core, but Goethe settings recur throughout the career, including "Der Musensohn" and "Wanderers Nachtlied II" of the early 1820s and the late "Gesänge des Harfners" and "Mignon" songs (settings of the songs sung by characters in Goethe's novel _Wilhelm Meister_). Taken together they are the spine of Schubert's Lied output and, with the two great Müller cycles (Chapters 6 and 7), the foundation of his claim to be the central figure in the history of the German song. No composer set Goethe more, or better; the irony that Goethe never knew it is the chapter's recurring note.

#### Recording history briefly

For this chapter, hearing one artist across the four anchors, Dietrich Fischer-Dieskau in the recommended readings, lets you concentrate on the songs' differences rather than the singers'. Individual songs also have their own famous readings worth seeking out, Fritz Wunderlich's radiant tenor "Der Musensohn" above all; and the female Goethe songs ("Gretchen," the Mignon settings) belong to the great sopranos and mezzos. The complete-songs projects and the indispensable annotated Hyperion edition are described in Chapter 20.

#### Cross-piece links you will hear later

The Goethe settings connect across the volume.

To _Die schöne Müllerin_ and _Winterreise_ (Chapters 6 and 7), where Schubert turns from Goethe to the younger poet Wilhelm Müller and from individual songs to the song cycle. The formal range you have heard here, strophic, modified-strophic, through-composed, is the toolkit he brings to the cycles; the modified-strophic form especially, glimpsed here, becomes the cycles' workhorse.

To the _Wanderer_ Fantasy (Chapter 9), built on Schubert's 1816 song "Der Wanderer" (a setting of a poem by Schmidt von Lübeck, not Goethe, despite the shared "wanderer" theme), an early instance of the song-instrumental interpenetration thread.

#### The 1815-1823 context (Thread A note)

The four anchor songs span Schubert's first maturity. "Heidenröslein" and "Wanderers Nachtlied I" come from the 1815 miracle year, the schoolteacher-and-garret time of Chapter 4. "Der Musensohn" (1822) and "Wanderers Nachtlied II" (1823) come from the cusp of the late period, around the time of the 1822 illness that Chapter 6 will take up, and from the same years as the _Wanderer_ Fantasy and the first great cycle. The Schubert who set "Wanderers Nachtlied II" in 1823 was a different and graver artist from the teenager who set "Heidenröslein" in 1815, and you can hear the difference: the early songs have the freshness of discovery, the later ones the concentration and harmonic depth of maturity. Goethe's silence spanned all of it. The next chapter turns to the first of the two works that, more than any others, secure Schubert's place as the supreme song-composer: the cycle _Die schöne Müllerin_ , written in the shadow of the 1822 illness.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"Goethe believed a song's music should serve the poem modestly and stay out of its way; he preferred plain strophic settings like Zelter's, and probably heard Schubert's dramatic, piano-painted, through-composed settings as music 'usurping' the poem. The chapter takes his position seriously, noting that 'after Schubert, it is nearly impossible to read "Erlkönig" without hearing the galloping triplets', the song has 'captured' the poem. Do you think a great musical setting is a gift to a poem or a kind of theft? When you read a poem you know in a famous setting, can you still hear it as the poet's, or only as the composer's?"_

* _"The same harmonic device, the shift to a key a third away, creates near-motionless stillness in 'Wanderers Nachtlied II' (a window onto peace) and ceaseless joyful motion in 'Der Musensohn' (a dance that never settles). Listen to both and try to hear the same 'sidestep' producing opposite feelings. What does it tell you that a single technical fingerprint can mean such different things? How much does context, the poem, the tempo, the surrounding music, determine what a musical gesture 'means'?"_

* _"'Heidenröslein' sets a dark story, on one reading a parable of seduction and abandonment, to a cheerful, folk-simple tune, keeping the surface light while the catastrophe plays out underneath. The chapter calls this gap between bright surface and dark content 'one of the subtler pleasures of Schubert.' Can you hear the darkness under the charm? Why might a composer, or a folk tradition, choose to sing terrible things to pretty tunes? What does that gap do that a 'sad tune for a sad poem' could not?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find the Fischer-Dieskau / Moore readings (or the alternatives in §5.1). Listen across the four songs and attend to these things.

  1. **The form-to-poem match across the set.** Hear the four songs (plus "Erlkönig" and "Gretchen" from memory) as a demonstration that Schubert had no single formula for Goethe. For each, name the kind of poem (folk parable, prayer, held moment, dance) and the formal solution (strophic, through-composed, single arc). Feel how right each match is.

  2. **The mediant shift in "Wanderers Nachtlied II" (near "Warte nur").** Listen for the few seconds where the harmony glows in a distant key before returning, opening a window onto the "rest" the poem promises. Then hear the same kind of key-shift drive the perpetual motion of "Der Musensohn." Same device, opposite effect.

  3. **The folk-simplicity of "Heidenröslein" as deliberate art.** Listen to the tune as something Schubert _made_ to sound anonymous and inevitable, not as a real folk song. Then listen under the charm for the story's dark edge in the insistent ending of each verse.

  4. **The stillness and the silence in "Wanderers Nachtlied II."** Attend to how little the song moves, the few spread chords, the barely rising line, and especially to the pause near the end that lets the stillness become total. In two minutes the song enacts the descent of evening peace and, behind it, a larger rest.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** (the range in one sitting): "Erlkönig," "Gretchen am Spinnrade," "Heidenröslein," "Wanderers Nachtlied II," and "Der Musensohn," in that order. Five Goethe settings by one composer, spanning melodrama, obsession, folk innocence, stillness, and joy. Heard together they are the best possible demonstration of Schubert's range as a song-composer and of the form-to-poem matching this chapter is about.

**Listen** (the Mignon and Harper songs): Schubert's settings of the songs from Goethe's novel _Wilhelm Meister_ , "Kennst du das Land," "Nur wer die Sehnsucht kennt," and the three Harper's songs ("Wer sich der Einsamkeit ergibt," and the others). These are darker, later, more harmonically searching Goethe settings, and they show another whole region of the relationship. Fischer-Dieskau (Harper) and various sopranos and mezzos (Mignon) have recorded them.

**Read** : Susan Youens's scholarship on Schubert's Goethe settings is the source-of-record; her studies of the songs combine close poetic reading with musical analysis aimed at serious listeners. For song-by-song commentary, Graham Johnson's notes in the Hyperion Schubert Edition cover every Goethe setting with context on the poem, the date, and the music.

**Read** (the Goethe relationship): the relevant pages of any of the major biographies, Newbould, Gibbs, or Byrne Bodley, on the 1816 album sent to Weimar and Goethe's non-response. The documents are collected in Deutsch's _Schubert: A Documentary Biography_. Reading the actual covering letter Spaun wrote, and registering the silence that followed, is a good way to feel the human reality behind the music: a teenager offering his best to his hero, and hearing nothing back.

## Chapter 6: Die schöne Müllerin, D 795 (1823) — The First Great Song Cycle

Until now you have met Schubert's songs one at a time. This chapter introduces the form that crowns his song-writing and that he, more than anyone, established: the song cycle, a sequence of songs that together tell a story or trace an emotional journey, conceived as a single large work. _Die schöne Müllerin_ ("The Fair Maid of the Mill"), twenty songs on poems by Wilhelm Müller, composed in 1823, is the first of Schubert's two great cycles and the first towering example of the form. It is a complete tragedy in song: a young man falls in love, is rejected, and drowns himself, and the whole journey unfolds across an hour of music for one singer and one pianist.

The cycle also introduces a new listening skill, the skill of hearing a _narrative across songs_ , of holding twenty separate pieces in mind as chapters of one story, hearing how a tune or a key or a figure returns transformed, how the emotional arc bends from sunny hope to black despair. And it deepens the piano-as-character idea you have been building since "Erlkönig": in _Die schöne Müllerin_ the piano becomes a brook, and the brook becomes the cycle's second protagonist, companion, confidant, and finally the young man's grave and cradle. This is one of the supreme achievements of Schubert's art, and one of the most approachable; its surface is folk-simple and its tunes are immediately lovable, even as its depths are tragic.

### 6.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote

Vienna, 1823. Schubert is twenty-six, and it is the worst year of his life. Late in 1822 he fell seriously ill; the nature of the illness is disputed (Chapter 19 takes up the dispute, the syphilis-or-other question, in full), but its effects were severe and frightening, and in 1823 he spent time in hospital, lost his hair for a period and wore a wig, and confronted, for the first time, the real possibility of an early death. It is in this dark year, while ill and shaken, that Schubert encountered a cycle of poems by Wilhelm Müller, a young German poet, telling the story of a wandering miller's apprentice who loves and loses and drowns himself, and set twenty of them as _Die schöne Müllerin_.

There is something almost unbearable in the conjunction: a young man facing his own mortality sets a cycle about a young man who chooses death by water. But the cycle is not, on its surface, a sick man's work. Its prevailing tone, especially in the first half, is fresh, springlike, full of the energy of youth and first love; the tragedy darkens slowly and only takes full hold near the end. Whatever the illness contributed, what Schubert made is a work of astonishing vitality and tenderness, and its turn toward death at the close is all the more devastating for the brightness it grows out of.

The poems themselves have a complicated origin worth knowing. Müller wrote them partly as a kind of literary game among friends, a sequence of role-played monologues, and they carry a faint irony, a slight knowingness about the conventions of Romantic love-and-death poetry, that the original framing (a prologue and epilogue, which Schubert omitted) made explicit. Schubert cut the ironic frame and set the inner monologues straight, taking the young miller's feelings entirely seriously. The result transforms Müller's clever sequence into a genuine tragedy. This is itself a lesson in what music does to words: Schubert's settings are so sincere, so fully inhabited, that the irony evaporates and only the human suffering remains.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen

  1. **The brook in the piano.** From the first song, the piano plays flowing, rippling water-figures, the brook beside which the whole story unfolds. The young miller follows the brook to the mill, confides in it, and finally drowns in it; the brook even "speaks" in the final two songs. Listen for the water in the piano throughout, and notice how its character changes, sparkling and eager early on, dark and slow at the end. The brook is the cycle's second character.

  2. **The folk-song surface over a tragic structure.** Most of the songs sound like folk songs: simple, strophic or modified-strophic, immediately singable. The first song, "Das Wandern" ("Wandering"), is practically a hiking song. This folk-simplicity is deliberate and it is the cycle's great expressive strategy: the tragedy is sung in the voice of an ordinary, unsophisticated young man, and the gap between the simple tunes and the terrible story gives the cycle its particular ache. Listen for how plain and lovable the melodies are, and hold that against where the story goes.

  3. **The slow darkening across the hour.** The cycle has an emotional arc you can feel even on a first hearing: bright, hopeful, energetic songs at the start (wandering, arriving, falling in love); a central turn as a rival, the hunter, appears and the maid's affection cools; and a descent into jealousy, despair, and finally a strange, terrible peace as the brook cradles the dead miller. Listen for the long curve from sunlight to darkness.

#### One-sentence background

_Die schöne Müllerin_ ("The Fair Maid of the Mill"), D 795, is a cycle of twenty songs for voice and piano composed in 1823, setting poems by Wilhelm Müller (1794-1827), telling the story of a young miller's apprentice who follows a brook to a mill, falls in love with the miller's daughter, loses her to a hunter, and drowns himself in the brook, which sings him a final lullaby; it runs about sixty-five minutes, is built around the piano's ever-present brook-figures, and is the first of Schubert's two great song cycles; the **Primary Recording** for this chapter is Dietrich Fischer-Dieskau with the pianist Gerald Moore (Deutsche Grammophon, the 1970s studio cycle), chosen for the baritone's narrative command and Moore's vivid brook; for a tenor alternative closer to the young miller's voice-type, Fritz Wunderlich with Hubert Giesen is the classic tenor reading.

#### Search Hints

EN: "Schubert Die schöne Müllerin Fischer-Dieskau Moore" or "Schubert Die schöne Müllerin Wunderlich" ZH: "舒伯特 美丽的磨坊女 费舍尔-迪斯考 摩尔" 或 "舒伯特 美丽的磨坊少女 旺德利希" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

**Alternative recordings** within the recording-hallucination safeguard (≥10 years old OR ≥3 reviews):

* Fritz Wunderlich with the pianist Hubert Giesen, Deutsche Grammophon, the great tenor reading, radiant and youthful; many listeners feel the bright tenor voice suits the young miller best, and Wunderlich's early death (he died at thirty-five in 1966) gives the recording its own poignancy.
* Ian Bostridge with the pianist Graham Johnson (Hyperion) or Mitsuko Uchida (EMI), an intensely word-conscious modern-tenor reading; Bostridge is the performer-scholar whose _Winterreise_ book Chapter 7 will draw on.
* Werner Güra with the pianist Jan Schultsz (Harmonia Mundi, 2000s), a lyric-tenor reading praised for its naturalness and its beautifully characterized brook.

### 6.2 The Listening Window

The cycle is an hour long, so this Listening Window cannot walk through all twenty songs moment by moment; instead it focuses, as the syllabus specifies, on five key songs that mark the cycle's arc. Listen to the whole cycle in order at least once; then return to these five. Timings refer to a typical reading; navigate by song.

**No. 1, "Das Wandern" ("Wandering").** The cycle opens with a cheerful, energetic walking song. The piano sets a brisk, rolling figure (the turning of the mill-wheel and the flowing of the water at once); the voice sings a strophic folk tune in praise of wandering, "to wander is the miller's joy." Five verses, all to the same buoyant music. Notice the sheer freshness and optimism; this is youth setting out, and nothing in the music yet hints at the tragedy to come. The strophic repetition is part of the point: a hiking song _should_ repeat, stride after stride.

**No. 2, "Wohin?" ("Where to?").** The young miller follows a brook, asking where it leads. The piano now is pure flowing water, a continuous rippling figure under a gentle, questioning vocal line. This is the song where the brook becomes a character: the miller addresses it, wonders at it, lets it lead him. Listen for the murmuring water in the piano and for the trusting, slightly hypnotized quality of the voice, the young man following the brook the way one follows a fate. The brook is leading him to the mill, and to love, and ultimately to death, though he, and we, do not yet know it.

**No. 16, "Die liebe Farbe" ("The Beloved Color").** Now we are deep in the tragedy. The maid has chosen the hunter, whose color is green; the miller, in grief, fixates on green, the color of his rival, the color of the hunt, and now the color of the grave he imagines. The song is slow and obsessive, built over a single repeated note in the piano (a soft, insistent tolling that does not stop), like a bell or a fixed idea. The vocal line is restrained, almost numb. Listen for that relentless repeated note, the obsession made audible, and for the bitter irony of the refrain about green being his favorite color. This is the brook-figure's relative: a single repeated piano element that _is_ a state of mind, descended directly from Gretchen's wheel.

**No. 19, "Der Müller und der Bach" ("The Miller and the Brook").** This is the cycle's emotional climax: a dialogue between the despairing miller and the brook. The miller sings first, in minor, of a broken heart and the wish to die; the brook answers, in major, consoling, promising that love's pain will pass and rest will come. Listen for the alternation: the miller's grief in the minor mode, the brook's gentle consolation in major, back and forth. The brook is now fully a speaking character, and a tender one, offering the only comfort left. The harmonic shift between the miller's minor and the brook's major is one of the most touching uses of the major-minor switch in all of Schubert.

**No. 20, "Des Baches Wiegenlied" ("The Brook's Lullaby").** The final song. The miller is dead, drowned in the brook, and the brook sings him to sleep, a lullaby over his watery grave. The song is slow, hushed, in a serene major, almost unbearably gentle, the murderer-comforter cradling the body. The piano's water is now smooth and slow, a cradle rocking. The voice (the brook's voice) sings of rest, of the cool blue chamber, of sleep until the sea drinks up the brook. Listen for the terrible peace of it: this is consolation and death fused, the most disturbing serenity. The cycle that began with a brisk walking song ends with a lullaby for a corpse, sung by the water that killed him. Sit with the final hushed chords.

### 6.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### The song cycle as a form: narrative across twenty songs

_Die schöne Müllerin_ asks you to listen in a new way: not to a single song but to a sequence as one work. The twenty songs are separate pieces, each complete, but they are ordered to tell a continuous story, and they are bound together by recurring elements, above all the brook, which runs through the whole cycle as image and character. The skill the cycle teaches is to hold the arc in mind: to hear "Das Wandern" not just as a cheerful song but as the _beginning_ of a tragedy, its optimism poignant because you know (or will know) where the story goes; to hear "Des Baches Wiegenlied" not just as a lovely lullaby but as the _end_ of an hour-long descent, its serenity terrible because of everything that led to it.

This is a different listening from the single-song attention of the previous chapters. The individual songs are still worth close hearing, but the cycle adds a layer: the meaning of each song depends on its place in the whole. The brisk wandering song means one thing in isolation and another as the start of a death-bound journey. The folk-simple love songs of the first half mean one thing on their own and another when the second half has turned them to ash. Schubert was among the first composers to think on this scale in song, to compose an hour-long dramatic structure for one voice and one piano, and _Die schöne Müllerin_ is where the form arrives fully formed. When you reach _Winterreise_ (Chapter 7), you will bring this cycle-listening skill to an even greater work.

#### The brook as the second protagonist

The single richest idea in the cycle is the brook. It begins as setting (the water the miller follows), becomes companion (the confidant he talks to), and ends as a speaking character with its own voice, consoling the miller in No. 19 and cradling his corpse in No. 20. The piano carries the brook throughout, in its ever-flowing water-figures, and the brook's changing character is one of the cycle's main expressive channels: sparkling and eager when the miller is hopeful, agitated when he is jealous, slow and dark when he despairs, and finally smooth and cradling at the end.

What makes the brook so devastating is its doubleness. It is the miller's only true friend, the one constant presence that listens, leads, and comforts. And it is his killer and his grave. The water that he follows to love is the water he drowns in; the voice that consoles him is the voice that sings over his dead body. Schubert lets the brook be both without ever resolving the contradiction, and the final lullaby is so disturbing precisely because the comfort is real and the comforter is the agent of death. This fusion, the loving murderer, the deadly friend, is the cycle's deepest stroke, and it is carried entirely by the piano's water and by the harmonic warmth of the brook's two "speaking" songs. The brook is the clearest large-scale extension of the piano-as-character principle you have been tracking since "Erlkönig": here the piano-figure does not just depict a state of mind for four minutes but sustains a whole character across an hour.

#### Folk-simplicity as tragic strategy

The cycle's tunes are folk-simple, and this is its master stroke. The young miller is an ordinary, unlettered apprentice, and Schubert gives him the music such a person might sing: plain, rounded, strophic or modified-strophic melodies, immediately memorable, with nothing of the sophisticated through-composed drama of "Erlkönig." On a first hearing the early songs can almost sound naive, and that naivety is the point. We are inside the head of a simple young man whose feelings are huge but whose expressive means are humble.

The tragic power comes from the gap between the simple means and the overwhelming content. When catastrophe arrives, the miller does not suddenly start singing complex, anguished, operatic music; he keeps singing in his plain folk idiom, now bent toward grief, and the contrast between the modest manner and the enormous feeling is heartbreaking. "Die liebe Farbe," the obsession-with-green song, is built on the simplest possible material, one repeated note, and its very simplicity makes the obsession more frightening. Schubert understood that a tragedy sung in a folk voice is more piercing than a tragedy sung in a grand one, because we believe the folk voice; it is the voice of an actual person, not a tragic hero. This restraint, the refusal to inflate the music to match the suffering, is a mark of Schubert's maturity, and it distinguishes the cycle from lesser Romantic tear-jerking. The means stay humble; the feeling is total.

#### Modified-strophic form at work

_Die schöne Müllerin_ is the place to hear modified-strophic form, the middle path defined in Chapter 5, doing its essential work. Many of the songs repeat their music verse to verse but bend it as the feeling changes: a verse that began in major slips to minor when the words darken; an accompaniment that was sparkling thins to bareness; a phrase extends or a cadence is altered to register a new pain. This lets a song keep the folk-memorability of repetition while tracking the miller's shifting state.

Listen, across the cycle, for songs that seem to be repeating a strophe but are not quite, where the second or third verse arrives changed, shadowed, or intensified. This is Schubert's most characteristic song-technique and his most subtle, because it works on the listener half-consciously: you feel the familiar tune return, but it is darker now, and the darkening tells you the story has moved without a word of explanation. The major-minor switch (Threading B) is the most frequent tool of this modification, and the cycle is full of it: the brook's consolation in major against the miller's grief in minor (No. 19), the bright love-songs that curdle, the lullaby's serene major that is the most desolate music of all. When you reach the synthesis chapter (18), the major-minor switch and the modified-strophic technique will be collected as central Schubert fingerprints; _Die schöne Müllerin_ is where you can hear them sustained across a whole hour-long structure.

### 6.4 The Stories

#### Wilhelm Müller and the poems

The poet was Wilhelm Müller (1794-1827), a young German writer who would also supply the poems for _Winterreise_. Müller is one of literary history's odder cases: a minor poet of real but modest gift, now remembered almost entirely because Schubert set two of his cycles and thereby immortalized him. (He is sometimes called, half-affectionately, "Müller the miller-poet," and there is an irony in his very name, _Müller_ means "miller," so the "fair maid of the mill" is the fair maid of the poet's own name.) Müller died in 1827, at thirty-two, apparently never knowing Schubert's settings; the two men never met. Like the Goethe non-relationship of the previous chapter, the Müller story has its melancholy: the poet who gave Schubert the words for his two greatest cycles died young, a year before the composer, with no idea that his modest verses had been turned into immortal music.

The poems began as a literary entertainment. Müller and a circle of friends in Berlin around 1816-1817 created a kind of role-play, a "Liederspiel," in which the miller story was acted out in verse, and Müller later published the cycle with an ironic prologue and epilogue framing the whole thing as a slightly knowing performance. Schubert cut the frame. He set only the miller's inner monologues, straight, without irony, and in doing so transformed a clever literary game into a sincere tragedy. This editorial choice is itself a profound act of interpretation, and it is worth registering: the cycle as we know it is Schubert's shaping of Müller's material, not simply Müller set to music. Where Goethe feared that music would usurp his poems, Müller's poems were arguably _raised_ by Schubert's settings, made into something deeper than they were on the page. The relationship between the two cycles' words and music is one of the great questions in the Lieder literature, and Susan Youens's study of _Die schöne Müllerin_ (1992), the source-of-record for this chapter, treats it in depth.

#### The 1823 illness behind the cycle

The cycle's composition in 1823, the year of Schubert's frightening illness and hospitalization, is one of the most discussed facts of his biography, and it must be handled carefully. It is tempting to read the cycle as a direct expression of the sick composer's confrontation with death, and there is surely _some_ truth in the conjunction; a man who has just faced his mortality, setting a cycle about a young man who chooses death, cannot have been unmoved by the parallel. But the cycle is not autobiography, and its prevailing vitality, especially in the first half, warns against reading it as a sickbed lament. Schubert chose Müller's pre-existing poems; he did not write his own story. The honest position is that the illness forms the biographical backdrop and may well have deepened Schubert's response to the death-bound material, but that the cycle is a work of art shaped by craft and by Müller's text, not a diary entry. The book's general caution about reading the life directly into the work applies here with special force: the conjunction of the illness and the cycle is real and poignant, and it is not a key that unlocks the music.

#### The "happy" first half and the trap it sets

A note on how the cycle works on a first-time listener, because it bears on how you hear it. The first several songs are genuinely happy: wandering, arriving at the mill, the first stirrings of love, the joy of being near the maid. A listener who does not know the story can spend twenty minutes in what feels like a sunny pastoral idyll. This is a trap Schubert sets deliberately. The brightness is real, and it makes the turn devastating: when the hunter appears and the maid's affection cools, the listener feels the loss the more sharply for having shared the hope. By the time you reach "Die liebe Farbe" and the final lullaby, the sunny opening songs have become almost unbearable in memory, because you now know they were the high point of a life about to end. This is one reason the cycle rewards repeated hearing: the second time through, you hear the tragedy already present in the happy songs, the shadow inside the sunlight. The cheerful "Das Wandern" is a different song once you know it opens a death-bound journey.

#### Recording history briefly

The central question for this cycle is voice-type. The young miller is a youth, and many feel the cycle suits a tenor, with Fritz Wunderlich's 1966 recording the radiant, heartbreaking classic; but it is also a baritone staple, and Dietrich Fischer-Dieskau (the recommended starting point) brings a narrative subtlety and word-coloring that treat the cycle as a story told by a mature artist inhabiting a young character. For a first encounter, hearing both a tenor (Wunderlich) and a baritone (Fischer-Dieskau) is the best way to feel how much the voice-type shapes the cycle. (The wider roster of singers and accompanists is in Chapter 20.)

#### Cross-piece links you will hear later

_Die schöne Müllerin_ connects across the volume.

To _Winterreise_ (Chapter 7), its darker twin, also on Müller poems, also a journey, but where _Die schöne Müllerin_ has a plot (love, rivalry, death) and a companion (the brook), _Winterreise_ has neither, only a lone wanderer walking into a frozen landscape with no story and no comfort. Heard as a pair, the two cycles trace Schubert's deepening from 1823 to 1827, from tragedy-with-a-plot to existential desolation. The journeying-protagonist structure launched here is darkened there.

To the chamber and piano music of the late period, where the brook-figure's relatives (continuous piano patterns that carry a state of mind) recur, and where the major-minor switch and modified-strophic thinking shape instrumental forms.

To "Gretchen am Spinnrade" (Chapter 4), the ancestor of the whole cycle: Gretchen's wheel is the prototype of the brook, a piano-figure that is both a physical object and a psychology, sustained under a lonely, longing protagonist. _Die schöne Müllerin_ expands the single song into an hour-long structure.

#### The 1823 context (Thread A note)

The cycle sits at the hinge of Schubert's life: the start of the late period, in the shadow of the 1822-1823 illness. Schubert was twenty-six. He had given up schoolteaching years before and was living the precarious freelance life among his circle; he had written the _Unfinished_ Symphony the previous autumn (1822, Chapter 16) and would soon write the _Wanderer_ Fantasy's contemporaries; and he had just confronted, through illness, the real possibility of an early death. _Die schöne Müllerin_ is the first major work of the period that the illness opened, and the great works of the next five years, _Winterreise_ , the late quartets, the String Quintet, the last sonatas, all lie ahead. The cycle is the threshold of Schubert's late period, and it is fitting that this threshold work should be a journey that ends in water and rest. The next chapter crosses fully into the late period with the second and greater journey, _Winterreise_ , the cycle that has no brook to comfort the wanderer and no death by water to end his pain, only the endless walking and, at the last, a ragged old man turning a hurdy-gurdy in the snow.

The chapter calls this fusion of 'the loving murderer, the deadly friend' the cycle's deepest stroke. Listen to 'Des Baches Wiegenlied' (No. 20). What does it do to you that the comfort is genuine and the comforter is the agent of death? Why might this be more disturbing than a straightforwardly grief-stricken ending?"_

Do you agree? Compare the restraint of 'Die liebe Farbe,' an obsession built on one repeated note, with the idea of a big operatic mad-scene. Which moves you more, and why?"_
