Listen to 'Das Wandern' (No. 1) before and after you know the whole story. Does the cheerful walking song sound different once you know it opens a death-bound journey? What does this tell you about how knowing an ending changes a beginning, in music and in life?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Listen to the complete cycle in order once (about sixty-five minutes); then return to these five songs, this time tracking the through-lines that bind them rather than each song on its own.

  1. **The brook across the cycle.** Track the piano's water from "Wohin?" (No. 2) to "Des Baches Wiegenlied" (No. 20). Hear how its character changes, sparkling and eager early, agitated in jealousy, slow and dark in despair, smooth and cradling at the end. The brook is one continuous character carried by the piano across an hour.

  2. **The folk-simplicity holding the tragedy ("Die liebe Farbe," No. 16).** Listen to the obsession-with-green song built over a single relentless repeated note. Notice how the very simplicity, one note, a plain tune, makes the obsession more frightening than elaborate music could. This is Schubert's restraint at its most powerful.

  3. **The miller-brook dialogue ("Der Müller und der Bach," No. 19).** Listen for the alternation between the miller's grief in minor and the brook's consolation in major, back and forth. This is the major-minor switch (Threading B) used as actual dialogue between two characters, and it is one of the most touching things in Schubert.

  4. **The terrible peace of the ending ("Des Baches Wiegenlied," No. 20).** Attend to the serene major, the slow cradling water, the unbearable gentleness of a lullaby for a corpse. Hold it against the brisk walking song that opened the cycle an hour before. The distance traveled, from a hiking song to a lullaby over a grave, is the whole tragedy in two songs.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** (the two voice-types): the cycle once in Fritz Wunderlich's tenor reading and once in Dietrich Fischer-Dieskau's baritone. Notice how the bright tenor makes the young miller more purely youthful and vulnerable, while the baritone brings more narrative weight and word-coloring. There is no single right voice; the comparison teaches you how much interpretation shapes a cycle.

**Listen** (the source songs): the cycle is so unified that pulling individual songs out diminishes them; resist the urge to listen only to the famous "Das Wandern" and "Ungeduld." The cycle's power is cumulative. But once you know the whole, returning to single songs with the arc in mind is rewarding.

**Read** : Susan Youens, _Schubert: Die schöne Müllerin_ , Cambridge University Press, 1992, the source-of-record for this chapter. Youens treats Müller's poems, the ironic frame Schubert cut, and the music in depth, and is written for serious listeners. For song-by-song notes, Graham Johnson's commentary in the Hyperion Schubert Edition covers the whole cycle.

**Read** (Müller's poems): Wilhelm Müller's cycle in a dual-language edition, including the prologue and epilogue Schubert omitted. Reading the ironic frame Schubert cut, and then hearing how his straight, sincere settings transform the material, is one of the clearest lessons there is in what music does to words, and a direct counterpart to the Goethe question of the previous chapter.

## Chapter 7: Winterreise, D 911 (1827) — the Documented Peak

You met two songs from _Winterreise_ long ago, in _Close Listening: Classical Music_ : "Der Lindenbaum" and "Der Leiermann." This chapter places them inside the whole, and the whole is the work that many listeners and scholars consider the summit not only of Schubert's song-writing but of the entire art of the Lied. _Winterreise_ ("Winter Journey"), twenty-four songs on poems by Wilhelm Müller, composed in 1827, is the second of the two great cycles, and it is a darker, stranger, and more uncompromising work than _Die schöne Müllerin_. Where the earlier cycle had a story, a setting, and a comforting brook, _Winterreise_ strips all of that away. There is no plot. There is a man, rejected in love, who walks out of a town in winter and keeps walking, into cold, into isolation, into something close to madness, with no destination and no comfort, and the cycle is the record of that walk.

This is the deepest water in the book, and the chapter is long because the cycle earns it. _Winterreise_ is where Schubert's art reaches its furthest extremity of feeling and where the listening skills you have been building, the piano as character, the form-to-poem match, the major-minor switch, the narrative across songs, all converge on a work of overwhelming power. It is also, frankly, hard: an hour and a quarter of largely bleak music with little relief. It rewards patience and repeated hearing more than almost anything in the repertoire. Take it slowly.

### 7.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote

Vienna, early 1827. Schubert is thirty, with less than two years to live, though he does not know the number. He has been composing _Winterreise_ , setting Müller's bleak poems of a winter wanderer, and one day he gathers his circle of friends, the Schubertiade group who have heard everything he has written, and tells them he will play them "a cycle of terrifying songs." He sits at the piano and sings through the first twelve. In the most often-quoted account, set down years later by his friend Josef von Spaun, the friends are baffled and dismayed by the gloom of the music; they had expected something more like the lovable _Die schöne Müllerin_ , and instead they got this unrelieved darkness. Only one song, "Der Lindenbaum," pleased them. Schubert, the account goes, said that he liked these songs more than all the rest, and that in time the friends would come to like them too.

You should treat the precise wording of this scene with the usual caution; it comes through Spaun's memoir, written long after Schubert's death, and memoirs polish their scenes. But the documented core is reliable and revealing: Schubert knew that _Winterreise_ was difficult, knew that it would not charm the way the earlier cycle did, and valued it above everything else he had written. He was right on both counts. The cycle was a hard sell in its own time and has become, in ours, the work most often named when people ask what the greatest song cycle is. Schubert's confidence in his "terrifying songs," against the dismay of the friends who loved him, is one of the documented moments when you can see an artist knowing the worth of his most uncompromising work.

The composition history adds a layer. Müller's poems came to Schubert in two batches. He set the first twelve in February 1827, believing that was the complete cycle. Then he discovered that Müller had published a longer version, with twelve more poems interleaved, and Schubert set the additional twelve in October 1827, appending them as a second part rather than re-ordering the whole. This accident of publication means the cycle's order is not a single planned sequence but two halves joined; scholars debate what difference it makes, but the cycle as Schubert left it, twelve plus twelve, is the work we have, and it holds together as a journey regardless.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen

  1. **There is no story, only a state.** Unlike _Die schöne Müllerin_ , _Winterreise_ has almost no external plot. The rejection has already happened before the first song; the cycle is not about events but about a mind walking through a frozen world and through its own desolation. Do not listen for "what happens next." Listen for the shifting weather of a single, deepening despair. The unity is psychological, not narrative.

  2. **The piano is the winter and the walking.** As always in Schubert, attend to the piano. Here it is the trudging footsteps in the snow, the icy wind, the frozen tears, the crow circling overhead, the post-horn, and at the very end the hurdy-gurdy. Listen for the relentless walking-rhythms that open and run through so many of the songs, the sound of a man putting one foot in front of another with nowhere to go.

  3. **The deceptive consolations and how the cycle refuses them.** A few songs offer a glimpse of warmth or hope, "Der Lindenbaum," with its memory of a linden tree and happier days; "Frühlingstraum," with its dream of spring and love, and the cycle always snatches them away, dropping from the warm dream back into cold reality. Listen for these false dawns and for the way the music turns from major-key consolation to minor-key truth. The cycle's deepest cruelty is that it keeps offering comfort and then withdrawing it.

#### One-sentence background

_Winterreise_ ("Winter Journey"), D 911, is a cycle of twenty-four songs for voice and piano composed in 1827 (twelve in February, twelve in October), setting poems by Wilhelm Müller, in which a young man, rejected in love, leaves a town at night and wanders through a frozen winter landscape toward isolation, despair, and a final encounter with a ragged hurdy-gurdy player in the snow; it runs about seventy to seventy-five minutes, has no external plot but a deepening psychological arc, and is widely regarded as the greatest of all song cycles; the **Primary Recording** for this chapter is Dietrich Fischer-Dieskau with the pianist Gerald Moore (Deutsche Grammophon, the 1971-72 studio recording, one of several Fischer-Dieskau made), chosen as the central reference reading of the modern _Winterreise_ tradition.

#### Search Hints

EN: "Schubert Winterreise Fischer-Dieskau Gerald Moore" or "Schubert Winterreise D 911" ZH: "舒伯特 冬之旅 费舍尔-迪斯考 杰拉尔德·摩尔" 或 "舒伯特 冬之旅 D911" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

**Alternative recordings** within the recording-hallucination safeguard (≥10 years old OR ≥3 reviews):

* Hans Hotter with the pianist Gerald Moore (EMI, 1954), the great bass-baritone reading, dark, grave, and elemental; many consider it the most overwhelming _Winterreise_ on record, the heavy voice making the wanderer's exhaustion almost physical.
* Ian Bostridge with the pianist Leif Ove Andsnes (EMI, 2004) or Julius Drake; Bostridge is the performer-scholar whose book _Schubert's Winter Journey: Anatomy of an Obsession_ (2015) is a source for this chapter, and his readings are intensely word-conscious and dramatically vivid.
* Matthias Goerne with the pianist Alfred Brendel, Decca, 2003, a modern-baritone reading of great inwardness, with one of the supreme Schubert pianists as partner.
* Thomas Quasthoff with the pianist Charles Spencer or Daniel Barenboim, a warm, humane reading praised for its directness.

### 7.2 The Listening Window

The cycle is over seventy minutes and unrelievedly serious; listen to the whole in order at least once before returning to these seven key songs. Do not try to absorb it all at once. Timings refer to the Fischer-Dieskau / Moore reading; navigate by song.

**No. 1, "Gute Nacht" ("Good Night").** The cycle opens with the wanderer leaving. The piano sets a steady, trudging walking-rhythm, even, plodding footsteps in the snow, that establishes the cycle's fundamental motion. The voice enters low and weary: "A stranger I arrived, a stranger I depart." He recounts, with terrible calm, that love brought him here and now he must leave in winter, in the dark, alone; he writes "Good night" on the gate so the girl will know he thought of her. Listen for the trudging footsteps that never stop, and for one crucial moment near the end: the song has been in minor throughout, and as he sends his final gentle "good night," the music shifts briefly to major, a heartbreaking softening, the one tender gesture before the long cold journey. Then it slips back. This major-minor shift in the first song sets the cycle's central harmonic device.

**No. 5, "Der Lindenbaum" ("The Linden Tree").** The song you met in _Close Listening: Classical Music_ , now in context. By the well before the gate stands a linden tree, under which the wanderer dreamed many sweet dreams in happier days; now, passing it in the night, he hears its branches rustling as if calling him to come and find rest (the rest, we understand, of death). The song opens with the piano's famous rustling-leaves figure and a warm, major-key, almost folk-song melody, one of the most beloved tunes Schubert ever wrote, and the only song that pleased the friends at that first playthrough. But listen for how the warmth is undercut: the memory of happiness is in the past, the tree's call is a call to death, and the song darkens to minor as the cold wind blows the wanderer's hat off and he walks on, refusing (for now) the tree's invitation. In context, the loveliest tune in the cycle is also one of its saddest, a memory of a warmth that is gone and a temptation toward death the wanderer is not yet ready to accept.

**No. 11, "Frühlingstraum" ("Dream of Spring").** The cruelest song in the cycle, structurally. It begins with a light, lilting, major-key melody of almost unbearable sweetness: the wanderer dreams of May, of flowers, of a girl who loves him. Then, abruptly, the music shatters, harsh, fast, minor, as crowing cocks and screaming ravens wake him to the cold reality: it is winter, the windowpanes are painted with frost-flowers (the only "flowers" here), and the girl is lost. The song alternates between the sweet dream and the bitter waking three times, each time dropping from warm major fantasy to cold minor truth. Listen for these violent drops, the dream and the waking set side by side. This is the deceptive-consolation device at its most explicit and most painful: the cycle shows you happiness precisely in order to take it away.

**No. 15, "Die Krähe" ("The Crow").** A crow has been following the wanderer since he left the town, circling above him. He addresses it almost tenderly, as a strange companion, asking if it means to seize him as prey, or whether it will at least stay faithful to him "to the grave." The piano circles high and thin, the crow wheeling overhead in the white sky. Listen for the eerie, weightless quality, the high circling figure, the wanderer's grim near-affection for the bird that is waiting for him to die. The crow is a dreadful parody of the brook-companion from _Die schöne Müllerin_ : the wanderer's only "friend" on the journey is a carrion bird waiting for his corpse.

**No. 20, "Der Wegweiser" ("The Signpost").** The wanderer asks why he avoids the roads other travelers take and seeks out hidden paths through the snow; he has done no wrong, so why does he flee human company? Then he sees a signpost pointing down a road "from which no one has ever returned." The song is built over an obsessive, trudging piano figure; near the end, on the words about the road of no return, the music settles into a slow, fixed repetition, the same notes over and over, as the wanderer recognizes his destination: death. Listen for the trudging obsession and for the terrible calm of the ending, the recognition, without panic, of where the journey leads.

**No. 21, "Das Wirtshaus" ("The Inn").** The wanderer comes to a graveyard and, with grim irony, treats it as an inn, a place of rest for the weary traveler. The music is slow, solemn, hymn-like, almost a funeral chorale, and for once in a warm major. He asks the "inn" (the grave) for a room; but it is full, every "bed" taken, and he is turned away. Even death will not yet receive him. Listen for the chorale-like solemnity and the bitter disappointment of the close: the one rest he longs for is refused him, and he must walk on. This is perhaps the cycle's bleakest stroke, the wanderer denied even death.

**No. 24, "Der Leiermann" ("The Hurdy-Gurdy Man").** The final song, which you met in _Close Listening: Classical Music_ , now as the cycle's terminal image. At the edge of the village stands a ragged old hurdy-gurdy player, barefoot on the ice, turning his instrument with frozen fingers, his begging-plate always empty, ignored by everyone, growled at by dogs, and yet he plays on, never stopping. The music is stripped almost to nothing: a bare, droning open fifth in the piano (imitating the hurdy-gurdy's drone) and a thin, monotonous little tune that repeats, the same hollow figure over and over. The wanderer watches the old man and asks, in the last lines, whether he should go with him, whether the strange old musician will play his songs to the wanderer's words. Listen for the emptiness, the drone, the hollow repeating tune, the way the music has been emptied of almost everything. After seventy minutes of journeying, the cycle ends not in death, not in resolution, but in this frozen, ambiguous encounter: the wanderer face to face with a figure who may be madness, may be death, may be art itself (the artist who plays on though no one listens), may be the wanderer's own future. The cycle does not tell you. It simply stops, on the bare drone, leaving the question open. This is one of the most haunting endings in all of music.

### 7.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### A cycle with no plot: psychological unity

The first thing to understand about _Winterreise_ is that it abandons narrative. _Die schöne Müllerin_ had a story you could summarize: boy meets girl, loses her to a rival, drowns himself. _Winterreise_ cannot be summarized that way, because almost nothing _happens_. The central event, the rejection, is already over when the cycle begins; the twenty-four songs do not advance a plot but deepen a state. The wanderer walks; he sees things (a linden tree, a crow, a signpost, an inn, frozen tears, a stormy morning, will-o'-the-wisps, a hurdy-gurdy man); each thing becomes an occasion for an inner song; and across the sequence his despair darkens and his grip on ordinary reality loosens. The unity is not "and then, and then" but the slow intensification of a single condition.

This is a radical thing for a song cycle to do, and it asks a different kind of listening. You cannot follow _Winterreise_ by tracking events, because there are almost none. You follow it by attending to the gradual change of weather in one soul: the early songs still have some heat of grief and anger; the middle songs grow stranger and more hallucinatory; the late songs empty out toward numbness, and finally toward the bare drone of the hurdy-gurdy. The cycle's order, even given the accident of its two-batch composition, traces this deepening: from the relatively "active" despair of leaving, through the visions and false hopes of the middle, to the frozen, near-catatonic stillness of the end. Schubert found a way to make a seventy-minute structure out of a state rather than a story, and that is one of the cycle's claims to greatness: it expanded what a song cycle, and arguably what music, could be about.

#### The piano as winter: walking, weather, and emptiness

Everything you have learned about the Schubert piano comes to its fullest test here. In _Winterreise_ the piano is the entire physical and atmospheric world of the journey, and tracking it is the surest way into the cycle.

It is, above all, the _walking_. So many songs open with a steady, even, plodding rhythm, the footsteps in the snow, and the cumulative effect of all this trudging across the cycle is one of its deepest sources of power: you feel, in your body, the endless putting of one foot before another with nowhere to arrive. It is the _weather_ : the icy wind that blows the wanderer's hat off in "Der Lindenbaum," the frozen tears, the storm. It is the _creatures and objects_ : the high circling crow, the rustling leaves, the post-horn that raises a hope the wanderer knows is false, the will-o'-the-wisps. And at the end, in "Der Leiermann," it is _emptiness itself_ : the bare drone of the hurdy-gurdy, the hollow repeating tune, the music emptied of harmony, of richness, of almost everything, the sound of a world frozen to its bones.

The trajectory of the piano-writing across the cycle is itself an expressive arc. The early songs have full, active, agitated piano parts; the cycle gradually thins, simplifies, empties, until at the end almost nothing is left but a drone and a thread of tune. The piano, like the wanderer, is freezing to death. This emptying-out is one of the most sophisticated long-range strategies in Schubert, and you can only hear it by listening to the whole cycle and feeling the texture drain away. The piano-as-character principle you launched on "Erlkönig" reaches, in _Winterreise_ , its most extended and most devastating realization: not a character for four minutes, but a whole frozen cosmos sustained, and slowly killed, across seventy.

#### The deceptive consolation: warmth offered and withdrawn

The cruelest and most characteristic device of _Winterreise_ is the false dawn: a song, or a passage, that offers warmth, hope, or beauty, and then snatches it away. "Der Lindenbaum" offers the memory of happy days and the loveliest tune in the cycle, then darkens as the cold wind blows and the wanderer walks on. "Frühlingstraum" offers a sweet dream of spring and love, then shatters it three times with the cold reality of waking. "Die Post" (No. 13) offers the bright sound of a post-horn and a leap of hope that a letter might have come, then the recognition that no letter will ever come. "Das Wirtshaus" offers the longed-for rest of the grave, then refuses it. Over and over, the cycle shows the wanderer (and the listener) a glimpse of comfort precisely in order to take it away.

This is structurally essential, not incidental. A cycle of unrelieved gloom would numb the listener; the genius of _Winterreise_ is that it keeps reopening the wound, keeps offering and withdrawing, so that the despair stays sharp. And it is psychologically true: this is how a grieving mind actually works, ambushed by memories of happiness, by sudden irrational hopes, that make the present worse by contrast. The major-minor switch (Threading B) is the chief musical tool of these false dawns: the consolations come in warm major, the withdrawals in cold minor, and the _drop_ from one to the other, often sudden, sometimes within a single phrase, is the sound of hope dying again and again. Listen for these drops throughout; they are the cycle's recurring heartbreak, and they are the major-minor switch used for the most devastating purpose Schubert ever found for it.

#### The ending that refuses to end: "Der Leiermann"

A whole tradition of writing about Schubert circles around the final song, and rightly. After seventy minutes of journeying, the cycle does not deliver any of the endings a story would: not the wanderer's death (he does not die on stage), not redemption, not resolution, not even the full collapse into madness that the middle songs seem to promise. Instead it delivers an _encounter_ : the wanderer meets a ragged old hurdy-gurdy man, barefoot on the ice, playing his drone to no one, ignored and growled at, and yet playing on. And the cycle's last words are a question, not a statement: shall I go with you? will you turn your hurdy-gurdy to my songs?

The figure of the hurdy-gurdy man is one of the great open symbols in music, and the cycle deliberately refuses to fix his meaning. He may be death, the strange psychopomp who will lead the wanderer out of life. He may be madness, the wanderer's mind finally breaking, projecting itself onto a figure in the snow. He may be the wanderer's own future, the old beggar he will become. He may be art itself, the artist who plays on though no one listens and no coin falls, an image of Schubert's own condition. Or he may be simply a real old man, and the wanderer, in his extremity, recognizing a fellow outcast. The music gives no answer: it empties to a bare drone and a hollow tune and stops, on a question, in the cold.

This refusal is the cycle's final and greatest stroke. _Die schöne Müllerin_ resolved into the brook's lullaby, terrible but complete; _Winterreise_ refuses resolution entirely, leaving the wanderer suspended forever at the edge of the village, facing a figure who will not be named, asking a question that will not be answered. The emptiness of the music, the way Schubert strips it to a drone, is the form of this refusal: there is nothing left, no harmony to resolve, no tune to complete, only the hurdy-gurdy turning. It is the bleakest ending Schubert ever wrote, and it is bleak precisely because it withholds the consolation of an ending. The cycle does not conclude; it freezes.

### 7.4 The Stories

#### "A cycle of terrifying songs"

Return to that scene with the friends. The documented core, that Schubert played the cycle (or its first half) to his circle, that they were dismayed by the gloom, that only "Der Lindenbaum" pleased them, and that Schubert insisted he valued these songs above all his others, tells you something important about how to hear _Winterreise_ : it was meant to be difficult. Schubert did not stumble into bleakness; he chose it, knew it would alienate even his devoted friends, and stood by it. The cycle is uncompromising on purpose. This matters for the patient listener, because the modern temptation is to look for relief, to seize on "Der Lindenbaum" or "Frühlingstraum" as the "nice" songs and treat the rest as worthy gloom to be endured. But the gloom _is_ the work, and the consolations are there to be withdrawn. Schubert's confidence in his "terrifying songs," against the dismay of people who loved him and loved his earlier music, is a model of an artist trusting his most uncompromising vision, and it should encourage the listener to trust it too, to let the cycle be as dark as it is rather than mining it for comfort.

#### Müller, again, and the irony of the two cycles

The poet is again Wilhelm Müller (1794-1827), who had supplied _Die schöne Müllerin_ , and the same melancholy attends the relationship: Müller died in 1827, the very year Schubert was setting _Winterreise_ , at thirty-two, and never knew the settings; the two men never met. There is a documented poignancy in the dates: in 1827 the poet of both great cycles died, and the composer of both had one year left. Müller, a minor poet who once wrote that he hoped someday a composer would find his poems and "find the music sleeping in the words," got his wish more completely than any poet in history, and never knew it. The _Winterreise_ poems in particular have been somewhat rehabilitated by Schubert's settings; on the page they are accomplished but not extraordinary, and it is Schubert who found in them the existential depth that the music now seems to have always contained. The question of whether the depth is Müller's or Schubert's, raised already with _Die schöne Müllerin_ , presses even harder here, and Susan Youens's _Retracing a Winter's Journey_ (1991), the source-of-record for this chapter, examines it closely.

#### Bostridge and the modern obsession

A more recent layer of the cycle's story is the way it has drawn performers and writers into something like obsession. The English tenor Ian Bostridge, one of the great modern interpreters, wrote a whole book, _Schubert's Winter Journey: Anatomy of an Obsession_ (2015), built around his decades of performing the cycle, taking each of the twenty-four songs as the starting point for a wide-ranging meditation on the music, the poems, the history, and his own experience of singing them. The book is a model of the performer-scholar voice and a rich companion for the listener (it is recommended below). Its very existence testifies to something about _Winterreise_ : it is the kind of work that takes hold of those who live with it and does not let go. Performers return to it again and again across their careers; Fischer-Dieskau recorded it many times over decades, his interpretation deepening and darkening as he aged. The cycle seems to grow with the performer and with the listener, revealing more as one brings more life to it. This is part of what is meant by calling it the greatest of song cycles: it is inexhaustible.

#### Fischer-Dieskau and the modern Winterreise tradition

The recording history of _Winterreise_ is, to a remarkable degree, the history of one singer's lifelong engagement with it. Dietrich Fischer-Dieskau recorded the cycle numerous times across his career, and his interpretation became the central reference point of the modern tradition; the 1971-72 reading with Gerald Moore is the recommended primary. But he is not the only landmark: Hans Hotter's 1954 recording, the dark, weighty, elemental voice making the wanderer's exhaustion almost unbearable, is for many the most overwhelming of all. Because the cycle is so inward and so long, it tolerates, even demands, a wide range of interpretation, by tenors, baritones, and occasionally women, each finding a different wanderer; there is no single correct _Winterreise_ , and exploring several readings is one of the deepest pleasures the Lieder repertoire offers. (The full roster of singers and accompanists is in Chapter 20.)

#### Cross-piece links you will hear later

_Winterreise_ connects across the volume.

To _Die schöne Müllerin_ (Chapter 6), its twin and necessary companion. Heard as a pair, the two Müller cycles trace Schubert's deepening from 1823 to 1827: from a tragedy with a plot and a comforting brook to a plotless journey through a frozen mind with no comfort at all. The crow of _Winterreise_ (No. 15) is a deliberate dark echo of the brook-companion; the journeying protagonist is the same figure, stripped of story and hope.

To the late instrumental music, especially the late piano sonatas (Chapter 10) and the String Quintet (Chapter 14), which share _Winterreise_ 's harmonic world, the major-minor shadings, the sudden modulations to distant keys, the long stretches of bleak or visionary stillness. The Schubert who wrote the empty drone of "Der Leiermann" is the same Schubert who wrote the mysterious low trill of the D 960 sonata and the desolate central eruption of the Quintet's Adagio.

To the late Heine songs of _Schwanengesang_ (Chapter 8), which push _Winterreise_ 's harmonic extremity even further; "Der Doppelgänger," with its bare, obsessive chords, is the next step beyond "Der Leiermann."

#### The 1827 context (Thread A note)

_Winterreise_ was composed in 1827, the year before Schubert's death, and it is impossible not to feel the proximity. He was thirty; he had been living for five years with the aftermath of the 1822 illness and with the knowledge that his health was precarious; in March 1827 he was a torchbearer at Beethoven's funeral, the death of the master he had idolized from a distance his whole life. The two batches of the cycle frame that year: the first twelve songs in February, around the time of Beethoven's death; the last twelve in October, a year before his own. He would die in November 1828. It is tempting, and not entirely wrong, to hear _Winterreise_ as the work of a man walking toward his own death, and the cycle's journey-toward-an-unnamed-end does resonate terribly with the biography. But the book's caution holds: Schubert set Müller's pre-existing poems; the cycle is not autobiography, and its power does not depend on the biographical reading. What is true is that in 1827 Schubert, with one year left, poured into Müller's winter journey a depth of feeling about isolation, hopelessness, and the approach of an unnamed end that no listener can hear without thinking of where the composer himself was. The next chapter takes up the songs of his final year, 1828, the _Schwanengesang_ collection, where the Heine settings carry the harmonic extremity of _Winterreise_ into even stranger territory, and where Schubert's last completed song was, with documented poignancy, a light and cheerful one about a carrier pigeon named Longing.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"_Winterreise_ has almost no plot: the rejection is over before the first song, and the twenty-four songs deepen a single state rather than advancing a story. The chapter argues this is one of its claims to greatness, that Schubert 'found a way to make a seventy-minute structure out of a state rather than a story.' How does listening to a work with no plot differ from listening to one with a story like _Die schöne Müllerin_? What holds your attention across seventy minutes when 'what happens next' is not the question?"_

* _"The cycle's cruelest device is the deceptive consolation: 'Der Lindenbaum,' 'Frühlingstraum,' 'Die Post,' all offer warmth or hope and then withdraw it, often by dropping from major to minor. The chapter calls this 'psychologically true', the way a grieving mind is ambushed by memories of happiness that make the present worse. Listen to 'Frühlingstraum' (No. 11) and its three drops from sweet dream to cold waking. Why might offering and withdrawing comfort be more devastating than unrelieved gloom? Have you experienced this in your own grief or in art you love?"_

* _"The cycle ends not with the wanderer's death or any resolution but with the hurdy-gurdy man, a figure the music deliberately refuses to explain, who may be death, madness, the wanderer's future, art itself, or just an old beggar, and the last words are a question. The chapter calls this refusal 'the bleakest ending Schubert ever wrote' precisely because it 'withholds the consolation of an ending.' What do you make of the hurdy-gurdy man? Does the open ending frustrate you or move you, and why might an artist choose to end a seventy-minute journey on an unanswered question rather than a conclusion?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Listen to the complete cycle in order at least once (about seventy minutes); then return to these songs to follow the cycle's long arcs, the walking, the false dawns, the emptying-out, across the whole rather than song by song.

  1. **The walking-rhythms across the cycle (start with "Gute Nacht," No. 1).** Fix the trudging footsteps of the opening song, then notice the walking-rhythms that recur through so many songs. Feel the cumulative weight of all this trudging, the bodily sense of endless walking with nowhere to arrive. This is one of the cycle's deepest, least obvious sources of power.

  2. **The major-minor drops in the deceptive consolations.** In "Der Lindenbaum" (No. 5), "Frühlingstraum" (No. 11), and "Die Post" (No. 13), listen for the warm major of the consolation and the cold minor of its withdrawal, and for the _drop_ between them. This is the major-minor switch (Threading B) used for the most devastating purpose in Schubert: the sound of hope dying again and again.

  3. **The emptying-out of the piano across the cycle.** Compare the full, agitated piano of the early songs with the bareness of the late ones, culminating in the hollow drone of "Der Leiermann" (No. 24). Hear the texture drain away across seventy minutes, the piano, like the wanderer, freezing to death. This long-range thinning is one of Schubert's most sophisticated strategies.

  4. **The unanswered ending ("Der Leiermann," No. 24).** Attend to the bare drone, the hollow repeating tune, the music emptied of almost everything, and to the final question that the cycle refuses to answer. Sit with the way it freezes rather than concludes. This is one of the most haunting endings in music; let it stay open rather than reaching for an interpretation.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** (the pairing): _Die schöne Müllerin_ (Chapter 6) and _Winterreise_ back to back, or at least with the earlier cycle fresh in mind. The two Müller cycles are the twin peaks of Schubert's song-writing, and hearing the journey from the brook-comforted tragedy of 1823 to the comfortless winter journey of 1827 is one of the great experiences the repertoire offers.

**Listen** (multiple readings): at least two contrasting _Winterreise_ recordings, for instance Fischer-Dieskau's baritone and Hans Hotter's darker bass-baritone, or a modern reading like Goerne's or Bostridge's. The cycle is so inward that it changes profoundly with the interpreter; comparing readings teaches you how much of what you "hear" is the performer's reading of an inexhaustible text.

**Read** : Ian Bostridge, _Schubert's Winter Journey: Anatomy of an Obsession_ , Knopf, 2015, a song-by-song meditation by one of the great modern interpreters, ranging across music, poetry, history, and personal experience. It is the ideal companion for a listener living with the cycle. For the scholarly source-of-record, Susan Youens, _Retracing a Winter's Journey: Schubert's Winterreise_ , Cornell University Press, 1991.

**Read** (the poems): Müller's _Winterreise_ poems in a dual-language edition. The cycle rewards knowing the texts closely, the specific images (the frozen tears, the will-o'-the-wisps, the crow, the signpost, the hurdy-gurdy man) and the way Schubert sets each. Reading the poems first, then hearing how the music finds depths in them that the page only hints at, is the clearest possible demonstration of what Schubert's art added to Müller's words.

## Chapter 8: Schwanengesang, D 957 (1828) + the Late Lieder

This chapter completes the Lieder part with the songs of Schubert's last year, gathered, after his death, under the title _Schwanengesang_ ("Swan Song"). The title is the publisher's, not Schubert's, and it carries a fitting irony: the "swan song" of legend is the beautiful song a swan sings as it dies, and these are indeed among the last songs Schubert wrote, but he never conceived them as a cycle or a farewell. They are a posthumous gathering, and understanding what they are, and what they are not, is the first task of the chapter.

What the gathering contains, though, is some of the most extraordinary music Schubert ever wrote, above all the six settings of poems by Heinrich Heine, which carry the harmonic extremity of _Winterreise_ into still stranger and barer territory. "Der Doppelgänger," the darkest of them, strips song almost to its skeleton and stares into an abyss that points forward to the music of the later nineteenth century and beyond. The chapter anchors on the popular serenade "Ständchen," the terrifying "Der Doppelgänger," and Schubert's documented last completed song, "Die Taubenpost," and uses them to ask what Schubert's song-writing had become in its final months, and where it might have gone had he lived.

### 8.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote

Vienna, autumn 1828. Schubert is thirty-one and has weeks to live, though the illness that will kill him in November has not yet declared itself fully. He is, astonishingly, at the height of a creative surge: in this single year he has written or completed the String Quintet, the last three piano sonatas, the _Great_ C major Symphony, a Mass, and more. And he is writing songs, settings of three poets, Ludwig Rellstab, Heinrich Heine, and Johann Gabriel Seidl, that he does not live to see published.

After Schubert's death on November 19, 1828, the Viennese publisher Tobias Haslinger acquired a group of these final songs and, in 1829, published them together under the title _Schwanengesang_ , fourteen songs: seven on poems by Rellstab, six on poems by Heine, and a final one on a poem by Seidl. The title and the grouping were Haslinger's commercial decision, not Schubert's artistic plan. Schubert had not assembled these as a cycle the way he had assembled _Die schöne Müllerin_ and _Winterreise_ ; they are simply the songs that lay on his desk, by three different poets, gathered and given a marketable name. This is why scholars are careful to call _Schwanengesang_ a _collection_ , not a cycle: there is no story, no journey, no unifying conception, only the accident of being the last.

And yet the accident produced something precious. Because these are genuinely among Schubert's last songs, they offer a glimpse of where his art stood at the very end, and the Heine settings in particular show it pushing into new and frightening territory. Heine (1797-1856), almost exactly Schubert's contemporary, was a new kind of poet, ironic, modern, psychologically raw, and his poems drew from Schubert a new kind of song: barer, harmonically more extreme, emotionally more naked than anything in the earlier cycles. Had Schubert lived, the Heine settings suggest, his song-writing might have gone somewhere no one could have predicted. They are the last thing he had time to discover.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen

  1. **"Ständchen": the popular Schubert, perfected.** This serenade ("Leise flehen meine Lieder", softly my songs implore you) is one of Schubert's most famous and beloved tunes, a lover singing beneath a window, the piano imitating a serenading guitar. Notice the sheer melodic loveliness, and notice one subtle Schubert touch: the song shifts between minor and major (the lover's pleading minor, the warm major of hope), the major-minor switch used here tenderly rather than tragically. This is the accessible, melodious Schubert at his most perfect.

  2. **"Der Doppelgänger": song stripped to the bone.** This is the opposite pole. The piano plays a slow, bare, solemn progression of chords, repeated almost unchanged, like a fixed stare; the voice declaims rather than sings, rising to a shattering climax. There is almost no "tune," almost no movement, only a terrible fixity. Notice how little happens and how devastating the little is. This is Schubert at the furthest edge of what song could do in 1828, and it sounds like nothing before it.

  3. **"Die Taubenpost": the last song, and it is cheerful.** Schubert's last completed song is not a dark farewell but a light, charming setting (on a Seidl poem) about a faithful carrier pigeon, named, in the last line, "Longing" (_Sehnsucht_). Notice the gentle, lilting warmth, and the quiet poignancy that the very last song Schubert finished should be a sweet, almost playful one whose secret name is longing. There is no deathbed solemnity here; the swan's last song is light.

#### One-sentence background

_Schwanengesang_ ("Swan Song"), D 957, is not a cycle but a collection of fourteen late songs gathered and published after Schubert's death by Tobias Haslinger in 1829, comprising seven settings of Ludwig Rellstab, six of Heinrich Heine, and one of Johann Gabriel Seidl; the Heine settings are the most extraordinary, pushing Schubert's harmonic language to a new extremity, and the collection includes both the beloved serenade "Ständchen" (Rellstab, No. 4) and the abyssal "Der Doppelgänger" (Heine, No. 13); Schubert's last completed song, "Die Taubenpost," D 965a (Seidl), is appended to the collection; the **Primary Recording** for this chapter is Dietrich Fischer-Dieskau with the pianist Gerald Moore (Deutsche Grammophon), chosen for the baritone's command across the collection's enormous range, from serenade to abyss.

#### Search Hints

EN: "Schubert Schwanengesang Fischer-Dieskau Moore" or "Schubert Der Doppelgänger Ständchen Heine" ZH: "舒伯特 天鹅之歌 费舍尔-迪斯考 摩尔" 或 "舒伯特 魔影 小夜曲 海涅" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

**Alternative recordings** within the recording-hallucination safeguard (≥10 years old OR ≥3 reviews):

* Matthias Goerne with the pianist Alfred Brendel or Andreas Haefliger (Decca / Harmonia Mundi), a dark, inward modern-baritone reading; Goerne's "Der Doppelgänger" is especially harrowing.
* For "Ständchen" specifically, the great tenors (Fritz Wunderlich, Jonas Kaufmann) bring a warmer, more ardent serenade than a baritone; the song is often heard in a tenor reading.
* Hans Hotter with Gerald Moore (EMI) for the Heine settings, the deep voice giving "Der Doppelgänger" elemental weight.
* Graham Johnson's Hyperion Schubert Edition includes the whole collection with full commentary on the Rellstab, Heine, and Seidl groups.

### 8.2 The Listening Window

The collection's fourteen songs do not form a single arc, so this Listening Window focuses on the three anchor songs, with a note on the Heine group as a whole. Hear the Heine settings (Nos. 8-13) as a group at least once; they are the collection's heart. Timings refer to the Fischer-Dieskau / Moore readings; navigate by song.

**"Ständchen" (Rellstab, No. 4; about 4 minutes).** The piano opens with a gentle, plucked figure imitating a guitar, the serenader's instrument under the beloved's window at night. The voice enters with the famous melody: "Leise flehen meine Lieder durch die Nacht zu dir", softly my songs implore you through the night. The song alternates between a pleading minor (the lover's longing) and a warm major (his hope that she will hear and come). Listen for this gentle major-minor alternation, tender rather than tragic, and for the piano's "guitar" answering the voice between phrases, as if the night itself were echoing the serenade. Near the end the voice rises in ardent appeal, "komm, beglücke mich", come, make me happy, and the song closes in warm major hope. This is Schubert's melodic gift at its most directly lovable, and it shows that the composer who could write the abyss of "Der Doppelgänger" could also, in the same months, write one of the most purely beautiful tunes in the repertoire.

**The Heine settings as a group (Nos. 8-13).** Before the single anchor, hear the six Heine songs together, because they form the most unified stretch of the collection. In the published order they are "Der Atlas" (No. 8, Atlas bearing the world's pain), "Ihr Bild" (No. 9, her portrait), "Das Fischermädchen" (No. 10, the fisher-maiden, the lightest), "Die Stadt" (No. 11, the town), "Am Meer" (No. 12, by the sea), and "Der Doppelgänger" (No. 13, the double). This published sequence is not the order of Heine's own poems in his _Buch der Lieder_ ; some scholars note that rearranging the songs to follow Heine's poetic order traces a loose narrative, a small reminder that, as with the collection as a whole, the ordering we inherit is the publisher's rather than the poet's. What unites them, in any order, is a new harmonic and emotional extremity: bare textures, slow tempos, sudden plunges into strange harmony, a sense of psychological rawness and modern irony that is unlike the earlier Schubert. Listen across the group for the new bareness and harmonic strangeness; these six songs are the furthest Schubert's song-writing ever reached.

**"Der Doppelgänger" (Heine, No. 13; about 4-5 minutes).** The greatest and most frightening of the Heine settings, and one of the most extraordinary songs ever written. The poem: a man stands at night before the house where his lost beloved once lived; he sees another man standing there, wringing his hands in anguish, and recognizes, with horror, that the figure is himself, his own double (_Doppelgänger_), a ghost of his own pain. The setting is stripped almost to nothing. The piano plays a slow, solemn, bare progression of a few chords, repeated again and again with almost no change, a fixed, tolling, gravestone-like pattern (it is, in fact, built on an old technique of repeating a bass pattern, which gives it an archaic, ritual quality). Over this fixity the voice does not so much sing as declaim, low and quiet at first, describing the still night and the empty house. Then, as the man recognizes his double, the voice rises, slowly and terribly, to a shattering climax on the realization, "du Doppelgänger, du bleicher Geselle", you double, you pale companion, before sinking back into the bare, cold close. Listen for the near-total stillness, the repeated chords that will not change, and the way the entire emotional charge is concentrated into the slow rise to the climax and the chilling drop afterward. There is almost no music here, in the conventional sense of tune and movement, and that emptiness is the point: the song depicts a man frozen before his own ghost, and the music freezes with him. "Der Doppelgänger" sounds forward into the music of the next century; it is the most modern thing Schubert wrote.

**"Die Taubenpost" (Seidl, No. 14; about 4 minutes).** Schubert's last completed song, appended to the collection. After the abyss of the Heine settings, it is a shock of gentleness: a light, lilting, warm song about a carrier pigeon (_Taubenpost_ , "pigeon post") that the singer keeps, a faithful messenger that carries his messages to his beloved and never tires. The music is sweet, flowing, almost playful, in a warm major. Only in the last line is the pigeon named: its name is _Sehnsucht_ , Longing. Listen for the gentle warmth and for the quiet sting of that final word, the faithful pigeon is longing itself, and the song's lightness is the lightness of a man who carries an inexhaustible yearning so familiarly that he can sing of it with a smile. That this was the last song Schubert finished, light, warm, and secretly named Longing, is one of the most touching facts in his biography.

### 8.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### A collection, not a cycle: why the distinction matters

The first thing to be clear about is that _Schwanengesang_ is not a song cycle in the sense that _Die schöne Müllerin_ and _Winterreise_ are. Those two were conceived by Schubert as unified works: a single poet, a single narrative or journey, a deliberate order, a beginning and an end. _Schwanengesang_ is none of these. It is fourteen songs by three different poets, gathered after Schubert's death by a publisher and given a saleable title. There is no story connecting "Ständchen" to "Der Doppelgänger" to "Die Taubenpost"; they happen to be late, and they happen to have been published together.

This matters for how you listen. You should not look for an arc across all fourteen songs the way you did in the cycles; there is none to find, and forcing one distorts the music. What you _can_ do is hear the internal groupings: the seven Rellstab songs (varied, including the famous "Ständchen" and the agitated "Aufenthalt" and "Der Atlas"), the six Heine songs (the unified, extreme heart of the collection), and the single Seidl song appended at the end. The Heine group is the one stretch that holds together as a unit, and it rewards being heard as a group. The honest way to approach _Schwanengesang_ is as a precious miscellany, a gathering of late masterpieces that do not add up to a single design but that, individually and in the Heine cluster, show Schubert's art at its final, astonishing pitch. The book's general respect for what things actually are, rather than what a marketable label claims they are, applies here: call it a collection, hear it as one, and let the Heine songs be the cycle-within-the-collection that they nearly are.

#### The Heine settings and the new extremity

The six Heine songs are the reason this chapter matters as much as it does, because they show Schubert's song-writing reaching a place it had never been. Heinrich Heine was a different kind of poet from Goethe or Müller: ironic, modern, emotionally raw, willing to undercut feeling with bitterness and to stare at psychological extremity without the Romantic consolations. His short lyrics are spare and sharp, and they drew from Schubert a new spareness and sharpness in response.

The new extremity shows in several ways. Harmonically, the Heine songs go further than anything before, plunging into distant keys, holding dissonances, refusing the warm resolutions of the earlier music. Texturally, they strip away: "Der Doppelgänger" is built on a handful of repeated chords, "Ihr Bild" on bare octaves, the music reduced to essentials. Emotionally, they reach a rawness, a sense of psychological nakedness, that is new even after _Winterreise_. And they point forward: listeners and scholars routinely hear in "Der Doppelgänger" in particular an anticipation of the harmonic world of the later nineteenth century, of Wagner, of Wolf, even of the early twentieth century. Schubert, in his last months, was discovering a new kind of song, and the Heine settings are the evidence. This is one of the great "what if" moments in music: had Schubert lived even a few more years, the path the Heine songs open suggests his song-writing might have transformed the art a second time. He had time only to sketch the direction.

#### "Der Doppelgänger" and the power of subtraction

It is worth dwelling on how "Der Doppelgänger" achieves its overwhelming effect, because it is a masterclass in expression through subtraction, and it is the furthest extension of a principle this book has been tracking: that Schubert's most powerful strokes are often his most restrained. Almost everything that normally makes a song, a memorable tune, harmonic motion, varied accompaniment, has been removed. The piano plays the same few solemn chords over and over with almost no change. The voice barely sings; it declaims. There is no "development," no contrast, almost no event, except the single slow rise to the climax and the drop afterward.

And this near-nothing is shattering. Why? Because the emptiness _is_ the meaning. The man is frozen before his own ghost, paralyzed by the recognition of his own pain made visible; and the music is frozen with him, locked into its repeating chords, unable to move, exactly as he is. The one thing that does move, the slow, inexorable rise of the voice to the cry of recognition, carries the entire emotional charge precisely because everything around it is so still: against the fixed chords, the smallest vocal movement becomes enormous. Schubert understood, at the very end of his life, that he could make the most devastating song he ever wrote by taking almost everything away, that stillness, repetition, and bareness could be more terrifying than any storm of notes. This is the opposite lesson from "Erlkönig," where everything is motion and event; the two songs are the poles of Schubert's art, and that the same composer commanded both, the galloping melodrama of eighteen and the frozen abyss of thirty-one, is the measure of how far he traveled.

#### The two faces in one collection: "Ständchen" and "Der Doppelgänger"

The deepest lesson of _Schwanengesang_ is that the two faces of Schubert, the melodist of irresistible tunes and the explorer of harmonic and psychological extremity, were one composer, working at the same time, in the same months, at the very end. "Ständchen" and "Der Doppelgänger" were written within the same span; one is among the loveliest tunes ever written, the other among the barest and most frightening songs ever written; and they are by the same hand. A listener who knows only "Ständchen" thinks Schubert is a melodist of charm; a listener who knows only "Der Doppelgänger" thinks him a prophet of modern despair; both are right and both are incomplete. The whole composer is the one who could do both, and _Schwanengesang_ , precisely because it is a miscellany rather than a unified cycle, puts the range on display more starkly than the cycles do. Hold the two songs together in your mind: the serenade under the window and the man frozen before his ghost. That double image is Schubert.

### 8.4 The Stories

#### Haslinger and the marketable title

The title _Schwanengesang_ is a small lesson in how music reaches us. Tobias Haslinger was a Viennese publisher who acquired a group of Schubert's last songs after the composer's death in November 1828 and published them the following year. The title "Swan Song", the dying swan's last beautiful song, was his, a piece of Romantic marketing that capitalized on the poignancy of these being among Schubert's final works. The grouping of fourteen songs by three poets was also Haslinger's editorial decision. None of this is sinister; it is how the music business worked, and Haslinger did the world a service by publishing the songs promptly. But it means that what we receive as "Schubert's Schwanengesang" is partly Haslinger's construction, and the modern habit of treating it as a unified third cycle alongside the two Müller cycles is a misunderstanding the careful listener should resist. The songs are Schubert's; the cycle is Haslinger's. (Some modern performers, recognizing this, reorder the songs, separate the poets, or append "Die Taubenpost" differently; there is no single authoritative version, because Schubert never made one.)

#### Heine and Schubert: the near-miss

Heinrich Heine (1797-1856) was born the same year as Schubert and outlived him by nearly three decades, becoming one of the great German poets of the century. The two never met, and Heine almost certainly never knew, when Schubert set six of his poems in 1828, that the settings existed; the songs appeared after Schubert's death and Heine's fame as a poet was only beginning to grow. There is a documented poignancy, by now familiar in this book, in the relationship: Schubert set the poems of three great or near-great poets, Goethe, Müller, Heine, and was acknowledged by none of them in his lifetime. With Heine the irony deepens, because Heine's spare, modern, ironic poems were exactly suited to the new direction Schubert's music was taking at the very end, and the meeting of the two sensibilities, the modern poet and the composer reaching toward a new musical modernity, produced songs that point decades into the future. Had the two men's lives overlapped more fruitfully, had Schubert lived to set more Heine, the history of the German song might have been different. As it is, the six Heine settings are a tantalizing fragment, the start of a collaboration that death cut off before it began.

#### "Die Taubenpost" and the light at the end

The documented fact that Schubert's last completed song was "Die Taubenpost", light, warm, charming, and secretly named Longing, is worth pausing on, because it cuts against the temptation to make Schubert's end uniformly dark. The narrative of the dying composer pouring out abyssal final masterpieces ("Der Doppelgänger," the bleak late sonatas) is real but partial. The same dying composer, in the same months, wrote one of his loveliest serenades ("Ständchen") and ended his song-writing not with a cry of despair but with a gentle, smiling little song about a faithful pigeon. The "swan song" of legend is beautiful, not necessarily tragic, and Schubert's actual last song honors the legend in the gentlest way. There is something deeply characteristic in this: even at the end, with his health failing and the abyss of the Heine songs fresh on his desk, Schubert's fundamental musical nature, melodious, warm, life-loving, even as it carried an inexhaustible longing, reasserted itself. The last thing he finished was not the void but the pigeon. The careful listener holds both: the abyss of "Der Doppelgänger" and the warmth of "Die Taubenpost," written by the same man in the same final months.

#### Recording history briefly

Because _Schwanengesang_ is a collection rather than a cycle, it is recorded both whole and in parts, and the voice-type question is less fraught than in the cycles, since there is no single protagonist: tenors bring ardor to "Ständchen," bass-baritones bring weight to "Der Doppelgänger." For a first encounter, a complete reading by Fischer-Dieskau (the recommended primary) or Matthias Goerne, heard with the understanding that it is a gathering rather than a journey, is the right approach; individual songs, "Ständchen" and "Der Doppelgänger" above all, also have strong independent lives on recital discs. (The full survey is in Chapter 20.)

#### Cross-piece links you will hear later

The late songs connect across the volume.

To _Winterreise_ (Chapter 7), whose harmonic extremity the Heine settings carry further. "Der Doppelgänger" is the next step beyond "Der Leiermann": both strip song toward emptiness, but the Heine setting goes further into harmonic strangeness and psychological nakedness. The line of Schubert's deepening, from the comforted tragedy of _Die schöne Müllerin_ through the comfortless journey of _Winterreise_ to the frozen abyss of "Der Doppelgänger," is the trajectory of his last five years.

To the late piano sonatas (Chapter 10), composed in the same months as these songs and sharing their harmonic world. The mysterious, bare, harmonically extreme moments of the late sonatas (the low trill of D 960, the eruptive center of the D 959 Andantino) are the instrumental siblings of the Heine songs; Threading B (the song-instrumental interpenetration) here works not through a borrowed tune but through a shared harmonic and emotional language, the late-Lieder world and the late-sonata world are one.

#### The 1828 context (Thread A note)

The late songs come from Schubert's final year, the same astonishing twelve months that produced the String Quintet, the last three piano sonatas, the _Great_ C major Symphony, and a Mass. It is one of the most productive single years of any composer's life, and it is the year Schubert died. He was thirty-one. His health, undermined since the 1822 illness, was failing through the autumn; the typhoid fever (or whatever it was, Chapter 19 takes up the dispute) that killed him declared itself in early November; he died on November 19, 1828. The songs of _Schwanengesang_ were on his desk, unpublished; Haslinger gathered and printed them the next year. That Schubert, dying, at the peak of a creative surge, could write in the same months the lovable "Ständchen," the abyssal "Der Doppelgänger," and the gentle, longing "Die Taubenpost" is the final testimony of the Lieder, the central achievement of his life, ending not with a single note but with the full range of what he was. With the Lieder complete, the book turns to the instrumental music. The next chapter opens Part IV with the piano sonatas, where the song-instrumental interpenetration thread becomes explicit and audible, beginning with the _Wanderer_ Fantasy built on Schubert's own song "Der Wanderer."

Can you hold them together as one artist's work? What does it tell you about Schubert, or about any artist, that the melodist of irresistible charm and the explorer of the abyss were the same person?"_

Compare this with 'Erlkönig,' which is all motion and event. Which approach do you find more powerful, the everything of 'Erlkönig' or the almost-nothing of 'Der Doppelgänger'? The chapter argues this 'cuts against the temptation to make Schubert's end uniformly dark.' Why do you think we are drawn to the story of an artist's final works as profound, tragic statements? What is gained by remembering that Schubert ended not with the void of 'Der Doppelgänger' but with a smiling song about a pigeon?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find the Fischer-Dieskau / Moore recording (or the alternatives in §8.1). Listen to the anchor songs and the Heine group, and attend to these things.

  1. **The major-minor tenderness of "Ständchen" (No. 4).** Listen for the alternation between the lover's pleading minor and the warm major of hope, the major-minor switch used gently rather than tragically, and for the piano's "guitar" echoing the voice between phrases. This is Schubert's melodic gift at its most directly lovable.

  2. **The new extremity of the Heine group (Nos. 8-13).** Hear the six Heine songs as a unit and listen for what is new even after _Winterreise_ : the harmonic strangeness, the stripped textures, the psychological rawness. These six songs are the furthest Schubert's song-writing ever reached, and they point decades into the future.

  3. **The power of subtraction in "Der Doppelgänger" (No. 13).** Attend to how little happens: the repeated solemn chords that barely change, the declaiming voice, the single slow rise to the shattering climax and the cold drop afterward. Notice how the near-total stillness makes the smallest movement enormous. This is expression through subtraction, the opposite pole from "Erlkönig."

  4. **The gentle sting of "Die Taubenpost" (No. 14).** Listen to the light, warm, lilting song about the carrier pigeon, and to the quiet poignancy of the final word, the pigeon's name, "Longing." That Schubert's last completed song was this gentle, secretly yearning one is among the most touching facts of his life.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** (the trajectory): "Der Leiermann" from _Winterreise_ (Chapter 7) and "Der Doppelgänger" back to back. Both strip song toward emptiness; hear how the Heine setting pushes further into harmonic strangeness and psychological nakedness. Together they show the furthest reach of Schubert's late song-writing, the direction his art was taking when it stopped.

**Listen** (the whole collection): all fourteen songs of _Schwanengesang_ in order once, but hear them as a gathering rather than a journey, the varied Rellstab songs, the unified Heine heart, the gentle Seidl close. Notice that they do not add up to a single arc, and let the lack of a unifying design be part of what you understand about the collection.

**Read** : Susan Youens, _Schubert's Late Lieder: Beyond the Song-Cycles_ , Cambridge University Press, 2002, the source-of-record for the late songs, with extended treatment of the Heine settings and the new direction they represent. For song-by-song commentary, Graham Johnson's notes in the Hyperion Schubert Edition cover the Rellstab, Heine, and Seidl groups in detail.

**Read** (the poems): Heine's short lyrics in a dual-language edition, especially "Der Doppelgänger," "Am Meer," "Die Stadt," and "Ihr Bild." Heine's spare, ironic, modern voice is the key to the late songs' newness; reading the poems first, then hearing how Schubert's stripped-down settings match their bareness, shows why the meeting of this poet and this composer produced music that sounds forward into the next century.

## Chapter 9: The Early and Middle Piano Sonatas 1815-1825 + the Wanderer Fantasy

With the Lieder complete, the book turns to Schubert's instrumental music, and the natural bridge is the piano, because it was at the piano that Schubert lived. He was a pianist (not a virtuoso, but a fine and sensitive player), he accompanied his own songs, and the piano was the instrument of the Schubertiade, the social music-making at the heart of his world. He wrote around twenty piano sonatas, plus the Impromptus, the Moments Musicaux, the dances, and the four-hand music, an enormous body of keyboard work, much of it neglected until the twentieth century and now recognized as central to his achievement.

This chapter covers the sonatas of the early and middle years and one towering single work, the _Wanderer_ Fantasy, and it does something important for the rest of the book: it makes the song-instrumental interpenetration thread (Threading B) audible for the first time. The _Wanderer_ Fantasy is built on a theme from Schubert's own song "Der Wanderer," and hearing how a song becomes the seed of a large piano work is the listening skill this chapter adds. From here on, you will hear Schubert's songs leaking into his instrumental music again and again, in the _Trout_ Quintet, in "Death and the Maiden," in the late sonatas, and learning to catch it begins here.

### 9.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote

Vienna, 1822. Schubert, twenty-five, writes a piano work unlike anything he had attempted before: a huge, virtuosic, four-movements-in-one _Fantasy_ in C major, fearsomely difficult, built on a single idea drawn from his own song "Der Wanderer" of 1816. The story, often repeated and probably embroidered, holds that Schubert himself, no virtuoso, struggled to play the finale's ferocious fugal passage in performance, broke down, jumped up from the piano, and exclaimed, "Let the devil play it!" Whether or not he said exactly that, the documented truth underneath is real: the _Wanderer_ Fantasy is the most technically demanding thing Schubert wrote for the piano, music that reaches for an orchestral grandeur and a virtuoso brilliance that his other piano works mostly avoid.

The Fantasy points two ways. Backward, to the song "Der Wanderer" (a setting of a poem by Georg Philipp Schmidt von Lübeck, not to be confused with the Goethe "Wanderer" songs of Chapter 5), whose theme of restless, homeless searching, "Where are you, my beloved land? ... the place where I am not, there is happiness", gives the Fantasy its emotional core and its title. Forward, to Franz Liszt, who idolized the work, played it, arranged it, and took its central idea, building an entire large structure from the transformations of a single theme, as the model for his own _Sonata in B minor_ of 1853 and for the "symphonic poem." The _Wanderer_ Fantasy is thus a hinge: a Schubert song becomes a virtuoso piano epic, which becomes a model for the next generation's grandest forms.

Alongside the Fantasy, the chapter takes up the sonatas, and here the story is one of long neglect and recent rediscovery. For much of the nineteenth and early twentieth centuries, Schubert's piano sonatas were dismissed as diffuse, over-long, un-Beethovenian, the work of a melodist who could not build a proper sonata. The twentieth century, and pianists like Artur Schnabel, Sviatoslav Richter, and Alfred Brendel, rehabilitated them, recognizing that they are not failed Beethoven sonatas but successful Schubert sonatas, built on different principles, lyrical, spacious, harmonically adventurous, and that the "diffuseness" is a deliberate expansiveness, a willingness to let music unfold in its own time. The Sonata in A minor, D 845 (1825), the first major mature sonata, is the chapter's anchor for this rediscovery.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen

  1. **The _Wanderer_ Fantasy: one idea, four sections, no breaks.** The Fantasy is in four connected sections (a fast opening, a slow variation-set, a scherzo, and a fugal finale) played without pause, and all four are built from transformations of the same basic rhythm and theme, drawn from the song. Listen for the recurring rhythmic motif (a distinctive long-short-short pattern) that runs through all four sections in different guises. This is the "cyclic" idea, one seed growing into a whole structure, that so influenced Liszt.

  2. **The sonatas: melody and space.** In the A minor Sonata, D 845, listen for the difference from a Beethoven sonata. Schubert thinks in long, singable melodies and in broad, spacious paragraphs; he is willing to repeat, to linger, to modulate to distant keys for the sheer color of it. The music unfolds at its own unhurried pace. Notice the lyrical, song-like quality even in a dramatic minor-key sonata, and the sudden, glowing shifts to remote keys.

  3. **The song behind the Fantasy.** If you listen to the song "Der Wanderer" first (recommended), you will hear its slow, brooding central theme reappear as the slow second section of the Fantasy, the moment the whole work is named for. Listen for the song's melody surfacing inside the piano work. This is Threading B made audible: a Lied becoming the heart of an instrumental piece.

#### One-sentence background

This chapter anchors on two works: the Piano Sonata in A minor, D 845 (1825, ~30 minutes), Schubert's first fully mature piano sonata, lyrical and spacious in a way that rewrote what a sonata could be; and the _Wanderer_ Fantasy in C major, D 760 (1822, ~22 minutes), a virtuosic four-movements-in-one work built cyclically on a theme from Schubert's song "Der Wanderer," D 489 (1816), and the model for Liszt's later large forms; the **Primary Recording** for this chapter is Alfred Brendel (Philips, his Schubert sonata cycle and his _Wanderer_ Fantasy), chosen for Brendel's combination of structural clarity and lyrical warmth, and for his lifelong authority in this repertoire.
