* For the song "Der Wanderer," D 489: Dietrich Fischer-Dieskau with Gerald Moore, to hear the source before the Fantasy.

### 9.2 The Listening Window

This Window treats the _Wanderer_ Fantasy in some detail (because its cyclic structure is the chapter's key lesson) and the A minor Sonata more briefly. Timings refer to the Brendel readings; navigate by section and movement.

#### The Wanderer Fantasy, D 760

**Section 1 (Allegro), 0:00.** The Fantasy opens with a powerful, rhythmically driving idea: bold chords hammered out in a distinctive long-short-short rhythm. Fix this rhythm in your ear, it is the seed of the whole work, and it will return, transformed, in every section. The opening is brilliant and orchestral in ambition, the piano made to sound like a full ensemble. Listen for the sheer scale and force, unusual for Schubert's piano music, and for the rhythmic motif driving everything.

**Section 2 (Adagio), about 6:00.** The music slows to the work's emotional heart: a set of variations on the theme from the song "Der Wanderer." This is the moment the Fantasy is named for. The theme is slow, brooding, in a dark minor, the song's image of the homeless wanderer who finds happiness only where he is not. Listen for the song-melody itself (if you know the song, you will recognize it) and for the variations that grow more elaborate and more anguished. Notice that even here, in the slow section, the long-short-short rhythm of the opening is audible, binding the slow music to the fast.

**Section 3 (Presto, scherzo), about 12:00.** A fast, dancing scherzo, energetic and brilliant, again derived from the same basic material. The mood lifts; the music dances. Listen for how the seed-rhythm is now transformed into something light and quick, the same DNA in a completely different character.

**Section 4 (Allegro, fugal finale), about 17:00.** The finale launches into a fugue (a passage where a single theme is taken up by one voice after another and woven together) built, once more, on the seed material, now turned into a triumphant, virtuosic conclusion. This is the passage Schubert reportedly could not play himself. Listen for the fugal entries (the theme entering in turn) and for the headlong drive to the brilliant close. The whole twenty-two-minute work has grown from the single rhythmic-thematic seed heard in the first bars, and the finale brings it to a blazing resolution.

#### The Piano Sonata in A minor, D 845

**First movement (Moderato), 0:00.** The sonata opens quietly, with a spare, searching theme in A minor, very different from the Fantasy's bombast. Listen for the lyrical, almost vocal quality and for the spaciousness, Schubert lets the music breathe, repeats and develops at length, and modulates to distant, glowing keys. This is the mature Schubert sonata style: dramatic but unhurried, built on song-like material expanded to large scale.

**Second movement (Andante), about 10:00.** A theme and variations on a gentle, song-like tune. Listen for the melody's warmth and for the variety of the variations, by turns decorative, stormy, and serene. The variation form suits Schubert's gifts: a lovely tune, explored from many angles.

**Third movement (Scherzo), about 20:00.** A vigorous scherzo with a gentler central trio. Listen for the rhythmic energy and the contrast between the driving scherzo and the songful trio.

**Fourth movement (Rondo, Allegro vivace), about 25:00.** A quick, restless finale that returns repeatedly to its main theme. Listen for the nervous energy and for the harmonic restlessness, the music constantly shifting ground, characteristic of Schubert's minor-key finales.

### 9.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### The cyclic principle: one seed, a whole structure

The _Wanderer_ Fantasy's great innovation, and its gift to the future, is the cyclic principle: building an entire multi-movement structure from transformations of a single basic idea. Most sonatas and symphonies of the Classical era used different themes for different movements; the movements were related in key and mood but not built from the same material. Schubert, in the Fantasy, does something new: he takes one rhythmic-thematic seed, the long-short-short motif and the "Wanderer" theme, and grows all four connected sections from it, transforming it from the driving opening to the brooding slow variations to the dancing scherzo to the fugal finale. The same DNA appears in every section, in different guises, so that the twenty-two-minute work has a deep unity beneath its variety: everything is, at root, the same idea, seen from different angles and in different moods.

This is worth understanding because it became one of the most important structural ideas in nineteenth-century music. Franz Liszt seized on it: his _Sonata in B minor_ (1853) is a single huge movement built cyclically from a handful of themes transformed across its span, and his "symphonic poems" apply the same principle to the orchestra. Through Liszt the idea spread to Wagner (the leitmotif, a theme transformed to follow a character or idea through an opera) and to the whole tradition of "thematic transformation" that dominated late-Romantic music. Schubert, in a piano work of 1822, planted the seed. When you listen to the Fantasy, you are hearing the origin of one of the central techniques of Romantic music, and the listening skill, tracking a single idea as it transforms across movements, is one you will use again in the late sonatas and, in a different form, throughout the symphonies.

#### Song into instrument: Threading B made audible

The _Wanderer_ Fantasy is the first place in the book where the song-instrumental interpenetration (Threading B) is fully explicit and audible. The slow section is a set of variations on the actual theme of Schubert's song "Der Wanderer," and the whole Fantasy takes its title and its emotional core from that song. Here is the thread stated plainly: a Lied becomes the seed of a large instrumental work.

Why did Schubert do this, and why does it matter? It matters because it reveals something fundamental about how his musical mind worked. For Schubert, song was not a separate compartment from instrumental music; the two flowed into each other. His instrumental themes are often song-like (you have heard this since the First Symphony's slow movement), and sometimes, as here, an actual song becomes the basis of an instrumental piece. The song "Der Wanderer" carried, for Schubert, a specific emotional world, the restless homelessness of its text, "the place where I am not, there is happiness", and by building the Fantasy on it, he imported that whole emotional world into the abstract instrumental form. The Fantasy is "about" the wanderer's homeless searching even without words, because its theme carries the song's meaning.

This is the listening skill to take forward. From here on, whenever you hear a Schubert instrumental work built on or alluding to one of his songs, ask what the song meant and how that meaning colors the instrumental piece. In the _Trout_ Quintet (Chapter 12), the cheerful song "Die Forelle" brings its sunny, playful world into the chamber music. In "Death and the Maiden" (Chapter 13), the song of that name brings its confrontation with death into the quartet. In the _Wanderer_ Fantasy, the song "Der Wanderer" brings its restless homelessness into the piano epic. The borrowed song is never just a tune; it is a whole world of feeling, transplanted. Chapter 18 collects all these interpenetrations; the Fantasy is where you first hear the thread clearly.

#### The Schubert sonata: lyrical, spacious, and not Beethoven

The A minor Sonata, D 845, requires a shift of expectations, because the standard against which Schubert's sonatas were long (unfairly) measured is Beethoven's, and they are built on different principles. A Beethoven sonata tends to be tight, argumentative, motivically economical: a short idea is developed, intensified, and driven to a hard-won resolution, with little wasted. A Schubert sonata is lyrical, spacious, and expansive: it is built on long, singable themes; it unfolds at a relaxed pace; it repeats and lingers and modulates to distant keys for the pleasure of the color; and it is willing to let a movement be long because the material is being savored rather than argued.

For a long time this was heard as a fault, Schubert "couldn't" write a tight Beethovenian sonata, so his sonatas were "diffuse" and "too long." The twentieth-century rehabilitation recognized that this is the wrong frame. Schubert was not failing to be Beethoven; he was succeeding at being Schubert, writing a different kind of sonata suited to a melodist's gifts. The spaciousness is not weakness but a positive value: it creates a sense of music unfolding in its own time, of vast leisurely landscapes rather than tight dramatic arguments. The long-breathed melodies, the lingering, the sudden glowing modulations to remote keys, these are the strengths of the Schubert sonata, not symptoms of an inability to do something else. Pianists like Sviatoslav Richter, who played the sonatas with a spacious grandeur that made their leisureliness feel monumental, were central to this rehabilitation.

The listening lesson is to let go of the Beethovenian expectation. When a Schubert sonata movement seems "too long" or "to wander," ask whether it is failing to argue or succeeding at unfolding, whether the length is diffuseness or expansiveness. Almost always, with Schubert, it is the latter: the music wants you to settle into its pace, to enjoy the journey rather than rush to the destination. This is a different pleasure from Beethoven's drama, and learning to take it is one of the keys to the whole instrumental Schubert, and especially to the late sonatas of the next chapter, where the expansiveness reaches its sublime extreme.

#### Harmonic adventure: the glowing remote keys

One specific feature of the Schubert sonata deserves attention because it is a fingerprint you will meet everywhere: the sudden, glowing shift to a remote key. In the A minor Sonata, listen for moments where the music, instead of moving to a closely related key as Classical convention expects, slides to a distant, unexpected key, and the effect is a sudden change of light, as if the music had stepped through a door into a different-colored room. This is the harmonic move by thirds (the mediant shift) and its relatives, the same device you heard in miniature in "Wanderers Nachtlied II" (Chapter 5), now operating on the large scale of a sonata movement.

In song, the device colored a single moment; in the sonata, it shapes whole structures, because a sonata is partly a drama of keys (moving away from home and returning), and Schubert's preference for distant, third-related keys gives his sonata-dramas a different geography from the Classical norm. Instead of the expected nearby keys, he travels to far, glowing places, and the harmonic journey of a Schubert sonata movement is one of its deepest pleasures, you are taken to unexpected harmonic regions and brought home by surprising routes. This harmonic adventurousness, more than anything, is what makes the late sonatas (Chapter 10) and the String Quintet (Chapter 14) so profound, and it is one of the central Schubert fingerprints. Begin training your ear for it here: listen for the moments when the light suddenly changes.

### 9.4 The Stories

#### "Der Wanderer" and the homeless theme

The song "Der Wanderer," D 489, composed in 1816, was one of Schubert's most popular works in his lifetime, far better known then than most of the songs we now consider his greatest. It sets a poem by Georg Philipp Schmidt von Lübeck about a wanderer who travels through the world feeling that he belongs nowhere; the climactic lines, set to the brooding theme that became the Fantasy's heart, declare that happiness lies always elsewhere: "the place where I am not, there is happiness." The theme of homelessness, of restless searching, of belonging nowhere, was deeply Schubertian and recurs across his work (the wanderer of _Winterreise_ is its darkest version), and it is no accident that he chose this song's theme to build his most ambitious piano work around. The Fantasy is, in a sense, a meditation on homelessness in purely instrumental terms, the wanderer's restless searching turned into a structure that travels through four contrasting sections before finding, in the fugal finale, a kind of hard-won arrival. Knowing the song deepens the Fantasy immeasurably; this is why hearing "Der Wanderer" first is recommended.

#### Liszt and the Fantasy's future

The _Wanderer_ Fantasy's influence on Franz Liszt is one of the clearest cases of one composer's innovation shaping another's whole output. Liszt revered the work, performed it, made an arrangement of it for piano and orchestra, and absorbed its cyclic principle so completely that it became foundational to his own music. His _Sonata in B minor_ (1853), one of the supreme piano works of the century, is a single vast movement built, like the Fantasy, from the transformation of a few themes across contrasting sections; the debt is direct and acknowledged. Through Liszt, the cyclic principle Schubert pioneered in the Fantasy spread across Romantic music. There is an irony here worth noting: Schubert's piano music was largely neglected in the decades after his death, but this one work, the _Wanderer_ Fantasy, was influential precisely because Liszt championed it, so that Schubert's impact on the next generation of piano music came significantly through a single piece. The Fantasy is the most "forward-looking" of Schubert's piano works, the one that most directly fed the future, even as the sonatas waited a century for their due.

#### The neglect and rediscovery of the sonatas

The story of the piano sonatas' reception is a cautionary tale about how reputation works. In Schubert's lifetime only three sonatas were published, and after his death the sonatas languished, overshadowed by Beethoven's and dismissed by influential critics as formless and over-long. For most of the nineteenth century and into the twentieth, a serious pianist's repertoire centered on Beethoven; Schubert's sonatas were considered minor, even by people who loved his songs. The rehabilitation came slowly, through advocates who heard what the critics had missed. The pianist Artur Schnabel programmed them; Sviatoslav Richter played them with a grandeur that revealed their scale; Alfred Brendel wrote about them as well as playing them, making the intellectual case for their greatness; and by the later twentieth century the late sonatas in particular (Chapter 10) were recognized as among the peaks of the entire piano literature. The lesson is that "neglected" does not mean "minor": a whole body of great music can wait a century for the right advocates and the right ears. The A minor Sonata, D 845, the first of the fully mature sonatas, is a good place to hear what the nineteenth century missed, a sonata that is not trying to be Beethoven and is the better for it.

#### Recording history briefly

The recommended primary is Alfred Brendel, who recorded the sonatas and the Fantasy across his long career, combining structural clarity with lyrical warmth and writing illuminatingly about them. For the _Wanderer_ Fantasy specifically, the great virtuosos (Richter, Pollini) bring the brilliance the work demands. The sonatas reward exploring several pianists, because their spaciousness invites different conceptions of pace and grandeur, a point Chapter 10 develops for the late three; the fuller piano lineage is in Chapter 20.

#### Cross-piece links you will hear later

This chapter connects forward across the instrumental music.

To the late piano sonatas (Chapter 10), D 958-960, where the mature sonata style heard here, lyrical, spacious, harmonically adventurous, reaches its sublime extreme. The A minor Sonata is the gateway to the late ones; everything in it (the song-like themes, the glowing remote keys, the expansive unfolding) is fulfilled there.

To the _Trout_ Quintet (Chapter 12) and "Death and the Maiden" (Chapter 13), where Threading B (the song-instrumental interpenetration first heard clearly in the _Wanderer_ Fantasy) recurs: a Schubert song ("Die Forelle," "Der Tod und das Mädchen") becomes the basis of a chamber-music movement. The skill you build here, hearing a song's world transplanted into instrumental form, applies directly there.

#### The 1822-1825 context (Thread A note)

The two anchor works span the years around the great divide of Schubert's life. The _Wanderer_ Fantasy comes from 1822, the same year as the _Unfinished_ Symphony (Chapter 16) and just before the 1822-1823 illness; it is a work of Schubert's confident first maturity, ambitious, virtuosic, reaching for grandeur. The A minor Sonata, D 845, comes from 1825, in the depths of the late period, after the illness, after _Die schöne Müllerin_ ; it is a graver, more inward work, the first of the great mature sonatas that would culminate three years later in the last three. Between the two works lies the shadow that fell over Schubert's life in 1822-1823, and you can hear, comparing them, the deepening: the Fantasy's brilliant ambition giving way to the sonata's spacious seriousness. The next chapter follows this deepening to its end, the last three piano sonatas, D 958, 959, and 960, all composed in September 1828 in the final weeks of Schubert's life, where the lyrical, spacious, harmonically adventurous sonata style of the A minor reaches a sublimity that places these works among the greatest in the literature, written by a dying man at the height of his powers.

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find the Brendel recordings (or the alternatives in §9.1). Listen and attend to these things.

  1. **The seed-rhythm across the _Wanderer_ Fantasy's four sections.** Fix the long-short-short motif in the opening bars, then track it through the slow variations, the scherzo, and the fugal finale. Hear how one idea is transformed into four different characters. This is the cyclic principle that shaped Romantic music.

  2. **The song "Der Wanderer" inside the Fantasy (the slow section).** If you have heard the song, listen for its brooding theme surfacing as the Fantasy's slow second section, the moment the work is named for. This is Threading B made audible: a Lied becoming the heart of an instrumental piece.

  3. **The spaciousness of the A minor Sonata, D 845.** Let go of any Beethovenian expectation of tight argument and listen instead for the lyrical, unhurried unfolding, the long song-like themes, the willingness to linger. Ask whether the length is diffuseness or expansiveness; with Schubert it is almost always the latter.

  4. **The glowing shifts to remote keys.** In the sonata especially, listen for the moments when the music slides to a distant, unexpected key and the "light" suddenly changes. This is the harmonic move by thirds (Threading B), operating on the large scale of a sonata movement, and it is one of the central pleasures of the instrumental Schubert.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** (the source song): "Der Wanderer," D 489, before the Fantasy, in a Fischer-Dieskau / Moore reading. Hearing the song's restless, homeless theme and text first makes the Fantasy's slow section land with full force and demonstrates Threading B directly.

**Listen** (the influence): Liszt's _Sonata in B minor_ (1853), in any major recording. Hearing Liszt's single-movement, cyclically constructed sonata after Schubert's Fantasy shows how directly Schubert's innovation fed the next generation's grandest piano form. The debt is unmistakable.

**Listen** (more sonatas): the Sonata in D major, D 850, or the Sonata in G major, D 894, both from the mid-1820s, to hear more of the mature sonata style before the late three. The G major in particular, with its serene, spacious first movement, is one of the loveliest.

**Read** : Charles Rosen, _The Romantic Generation_ , Harvard University Press, 1995, the source-of-record for the analytical voice in this part of the book, on Schubert's piano music and the cyclic principle. For the pianist's perspective, Alfred Brendel's essays on Schubert (collected in his volumes of musical writing) are illuminating and accessible, written by the pianist who did most to establish the sonatas' greatness in the modern era.

## Chapter 10: The Late Piano Sonatas D 958, D 959, D 960

In September 1828, with perhaps two months to live, Schubert wrote three piano sonatas. Not sketched, not begun, completed: three large, four-movement sonatas, in C minor, A major, and B-flat major, finished in a single late-summer burst. They are, by wide agreement, the summit of his piano writing and among the supreme works in the entire piano literature, and the fact that they were written in a few weeks by a dying man at the very end of his life gives them a place in musical history close to Beethoven's late quartets and Bach's final contrapuntal works: the last testament of a great composer, written in the shadow of death.

This chapter is anchor-heavy because the music earns it. It focuses on three movements that show the late style at its most characteristic and most profound: the vast first movement of the B-flat Sonata D 960, with its mysterious low trill; the hallucinatory slow movement of the A major Sonata D 959; and the driving finale of the C minor Sonata D 958. Together they reveal what the lyrical, spacious, harmonically adventurous sonata style of the previous chapter became at the very end: music of extraordinary depth, breadth, and strangeness, the late Schubert reaching the furthest places his art would go in instrumental form.

### 10.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote

The documented facts are almost unbelievable, and they need no embroidery. In September 1828, Schubert, thirty-one years old and increasingly ill, composed three full piano sonatas. He dated the completion of the fair copies; the manuscripts survive; the timeline is real. Within weeks his final illness would begin; on November 19, 1828, he would be dead. These three sonatas are, with the String Quintet and the _Schwanengesang_ songs (also of 1828), the products of the most extraordinary final year in the history of composition, a sustained outpouring of masterpieces by a man whose body was failing and who may have sensed how little time remained.

There is a temptation, which this book resists, to hear the late sonatas as conscious farewells, a dying man's deliberate last words. The truth is more uncertain and more interesting. Schubert did not know he had two months to live; people with his symptoms could and did recover, and he was making plans for the future, studying counterpoint, hoping for performances. The sonatas are not deathbed valedictions written in the certain knowledge of the end; they are the work of a composer at the absolute height of his powers who happened, as it turned out, to have almost no time left. That makes them, if anything, more moving: not a planned farewell but the natural late flowering of a genius, cut off by an early death he was not expecting. The music's depth comes from artistic maturity, not from a death sentence.

The three sonatas were not published until about a decade after Schubert's death, and even then they were long neglected, like the rest of his sonatas, dismissed as too long, too diffuse, not Beethovenian enough. Their recognition as supreme masterpieces is largely a twentieth-century achievement, the work of the pianists and writers (Schnabel, Richter, Brendel, and others) who learned to hear the late Schubert sonata on its own terms. Today the B-flat Sonata D 960 in particular is among the most revered and most-recorded works in the piano repertoire, and the three together are routinely spoken of as the piano's equivalent of Beethoven's late quartets.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen

  1. **The mysterious low trill in D 960's opening.** The B-flat Sonata begins with a serene, broad, song-like melody, one of the most beautiful openings in all music. But almost immediately, deep in the bass, comes a soft, low trill (a rapid alternation of two low notes), a strange, distant rumble that interrupts the calm, like distant thunder or a tremor underground. Listen for this trill. It returns throughout the movement, and it casts a shadow over the serenity, a sense of something dark beneath the peace. It is one of the most discussed moments in the literature, and no one agrees on what it "means."

  2. **The eruption in D 959's slow movement.** The A major Sonata's slow movement (the Andantino) begins with a quiet, desolate, almost numb melody, a song of profound loneliness. Then, in the middle, it does something shocking: it erupts into a wild, dissonant, almost violent storm, music that sounds like a breakdown, like the mind coming apart, before subsiding back into the desolate calm. Listen for this central eruption; it is one of the most extreme and disturbing passages Schubert ever wrote, utterly unlike the gentle Schubert of reputation.

  3. **The relentless drive of D 958's finale.** The C minor Sonata ends with a long, restless, galloping finale (a tarantella, a fast dance traditionally associated with a frenzy) that drives forward with a dark, obsessive energy and a constant harmonic restlessness, never settling, always pressing on. Listen for the headlong momentum and the sense of being swept along by something that cannot stop, the dark, agitated Schubert of the C-minor world.

#### One-sentence background

Schubert's last three piano sonatas, D 958 in C minor, D 959 in A major, and D 960 in B-flat major, were all composed in September 1828, in the final weeks of his life, and form a culminating trilogy regarded as among the peaks of the piano literature; this chapter anchors on the first movement of D 960 (~21 minutes with the repeat, with its famous low trill), the second-movement Andantino of D 959 (with its shocking central eruption), and the finale of D 958 (a driving C-minor tarantella); the **Primary Recording** for this chapter is Alfred Brendel (Philips, his late-Schubert sonata recordings), chosen for structural clarity and lyrical depth; for D 960 specifically, the recordings of Sviatoslav Richter (spacious and profound) and Paul Lewis (probing and clear) are essential alternatives.

**Alternative recordings** within the recording-hallucination safeguard (≥10 years old OR ≥3 reviews):

* Sviatoslav Richter (various labels, 1960s-70s), whose famously spacious, slow D 960 first movement (he takes the long exposition repeat and a very broad tempo) is a profound, controversial, unforgettable reading; the model of the "monumental" late Schubert.
* Paul Lewis (Harmonia Mundi, 2000s), a celebrated complete late-sonatas cycle, clear, probing, and structurally lucid.
* Mitsuko Uchida (Philips), poetic and refined, especially fine in D 959 and D 960.
* Radu Lupu (Decca), warm, inward, and singing, a beloved D 960.
* Maria João Pires, Imogen Cooper, and Wilhelm Kempff also have distinguished readings.

### 10.2 The Listening Window

This Window focuses on the three anchor movements. Listen to all three complete sonatas eventually, but begin with these. Timings refer to the Brendel readings; navigate by movement (and note that D 960's first movement timing varies enormously depending on whether the pianist takes the long exposition repeat).

#### Sonata in B-flat major, D 960, first movement (Molto moderato)

**0:00. The serene opening theme.** The movement begins with a broad, slow, deeply peaceful melody in B-flat major, song-like, unhurried, one of the most beautiful and serene openings ever written. Let yourself sink into its calm and its spaciousness; the tempo is slow, the music in no hurry at all.

**About 0:50. The low trill.** As the opening phrase comes to rest, a soft, low trill sounds deep in the bass, a quiet, rumbling alternation of low notes, distant and strange, followed by a silence. The serenity is interrupted by this dark intrusion from below. Listen for it: the trill is the movement's great enigma. It is quiet, it is brief, and it changes everything, casting a shadow of unease over the peace. The opening theme resumes, but you now know that something dark lies beneath.

**About 2:00 onward. The theme expands; the harmonic journey.** The movement unfolds with extraordinary breadth, the serene material expanded, varied, and carried through Schubert's characteristic journey to distant, glowing keys. Listen for the spaciousness, the willingness to let the music unfold at vast length, and for the moments when the harmony shifts to remote, luminous regions. The trill recurs at structural junctures, each time reintroducing the shadow.

**The exposition repeat (if taken).** Schubert wrote a repeat of the long opening section, with a few transitional bars (including a dramatic version of the trill) that lead back to the beginning. Some pianists take it (making the movement around twenty minutes or more), some omit it (around twelve to fifteen). The repeat is musically significant: it doubles the experience of the serene opening and makes the movement's vast scale even vaster. Richter's insistence on the repeat, at a very slow tempo, produces the monumental conception; others omit it for a more flowing shape. Listen, if your recording takes it, for how the return to the opening deepens the music's sense of time.

**The development and recapitulation.** The middle of the movement (the development) takes the material into darker, more troubled regions before the serene opening returns (the recapitulation). Listen for the contrast between the troubled center and the restored calm, and for how the trill and the shadow persist even at the peaceful close. The movement ends not in triumph but in a serene, shadowed peace, the beauty and the darkness held together.

#### Sonata in A major, D 959, second movement (Andantino)

**0:00. The desolate song.** The movement opens with a slow, bare, desolate melody, a song of profound loneliness and grief, in a minor key, sparse and numb. Listen for the emptiness and sorrow; this is one of Schubert's bleakest slow movements, kin to the late Heine songs (Chapter 8) in its harmonic and emotional extremity.

**About 3:00. The eruption.** The middle section shatters the desolate calm. The music builds into a wild, dissonant, almost chaotic storm, fragmented, harmonically extreme, near-violent, music that sounds like a psychological breakdown, like grief erupting into something close to madness. This passage is one of the most shocking in all of Schubert; it goes far beyond the gentle composer of reputation into genuine derangement and despair. Listen for the buildup, the chaos at its peak, and the way it eventually exhausts itself.

**About 5:30. The return to desolation.** After the eruption subsides, the opening desolate song returns, but it can never be the same; we have heard what lies beneath. The movement ends in the bare, lonely calm it began with, now shadowed by the storm at its center. Listen for the return and for how the eruption has changed the meaning of the calm.

#### Sonata in C minor, D 958, finale (Allegro)

**0:00. The galloping tarantella.** The finale launches into a fast, restless, galloping movement in C minor, a tarantella (a quick triple-time dance associated, in legend, with the frenzy of a spider's bite). The music drives forward with dark, obsessive energy. Listen for the headlong momentum and the rhythmic relentlessness.

**Throughout. The harmonic restlessness.** What makes this finale extraordinary is its harmonic instability: the music constantly shifts ground, modulating to unexpected keys, never settling, always pressing on into new and often distant regions. Listen for this restlessness, the sense of being swept through a vast, shifting harmonic landscape without rest. The movement is long and its energy unflagging; it is the dark, agitated Schubert (the C-minor world he associated with Beethoven and with tragedy) at full stretch.

**The close.** The finale drives to an end that resolves the C-minor energy without softening it; the darkness is mastered but not dispelled. Listen for the grim power of the conclusion.

### 10.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### The late style: spaciousness become sublime

The late sonatas take the qualities of the mature sonata style (Chapter 9), lyricism, spaciousness, harmonic adventure, and raise them to sublimity. The most striking feature, especially in D 960, is the sheer breadth, the willingness to let music unfold at vast, unhurried length. The first movement of D 960 is one of the longest and slowest sonata movements in the repertoire, and its greatness is inseparable from its expansiveness: it creates a sense of enormous, contemplative time, of music that exists in a different temporal world from the dramatic urgency of Beethoven. This is the spaciousness of the A minor Sonata (Chapter 9) carried to its limit and transfigured.

To listen to these works, you must fully embrace the lesson of the previous chapter: let go of the expectation of tight argument and dramatic compression, and settle into Schubert's vast, leisurely unfolding. The late sonatas reward, more than almost any music, the patient, contemplative listening that lets time slow down. Richter understood this when he played the D 960 first movement at an extreme breadth, taking the long repeat, stretching the movement toward and past twenty minutes: he was not distorting the music but realizing its monumental conception of time. (Other pianists take it faster and find a more flowing beauty; both are valid, and comparing them teaches you how much pace shapes meaning in this music.) The spaciousness is not a flaw to be endured but the very substance of the late style's sublimity. These are not works to listen to in a hurry; they are works to disappear into.

#### The shadow beneath the beauty: D 960's low trill

The low trill in D 960's first movement is the single most discussed detail in Schubert's piano music, and it is worth understanding why, because it crystallizes the deepest quality of the late style: the holding-together of beauty and darkness. The movement's opening melody is among the most serenely beautiful Schubert ever wrote, pure, peaceful, song-like. And almost at once, the low trill intrudes: a soft, dark, rumbling alternation of low notes, distant and strange, followed by silence. It is not loud or dramatic; it is quiet, brief, and deeply unsettling, a shadow from below, a tremor under the peace.

No one agrees on what the trill "means," and the book will not pretend to settle it (the Conversation Seeds return to the question). It has been heard as distant thunder, as the rumble of mortality under the serene surface, as a question the serenity cannot answer, as a structural device, as the dark unconscious beneath the conscious calm. What matters for listening is not to decode it but to feel what it does: it makes the beauty _shadowed_. The serenity of D 960 is not naive or untroubled; it is a serenity that knows about darkness, that has the trill rumbling beneath it, and that holds its peace anyway. This is the deepest achievement of the late Schubert: not happiness and not despair, but a beauty that contains its own shadow — a peace earned in full knowledge of what lies beneath. The trill is the audible sign of that knowledge. It is why D 960 is not merely lovely but profound: the loveliness is haunted, and the haunting is what makes it true. Learning to hear the shadow inside the beauty is the central listening skill of the late style.

#### The abyss: D 959's eruption and the late harmonic extremity

If the D 960 trill is darkness as a shadow beneath beauty, the D 959 Andantino's central eruption is darkness as open abyss. The movement begins in desolate loneliness and then, in its middle, breaks open into a wild, dissonant, fragmented storm that sounds like a psychological collapse, grief turning into something close to madness. This passage shocks every listener who knows only the "gentle" Schubert, because it is genuinely violent, genuinely deranged, harmonically extreme in a way that points decades forward.

This eruption belongs to the same late-Schubert world as the Heine songs of _Schwanengesang_ (Chapter 8), the harmonic and emotional extremity that Schubert reached in his final year, in both song and instrumental music at once. This is Threading B (the song-instrumental interpenetration) in its deepest form: not a borrowed tune, but a shared world. The bare desolation and sudden harmonic violence of the D 959 Andantino and the bare, abyssal stillness of "Der Doppelgänger" are the same composer reaching the same extremity in the same months, in different media. The late songs and the late sonatas inhabit one harmonic and emotional universe, and hearing them together (the recommended exercise below) reveals the unity of Schubert's late art across the song-instrumental divide. The eruption also shows that the late style's serenity (D 960) is not the whole story: the same dying composer who wrote the shadowed peace of D 960 wrote the open abyss of D 959, and the C-minor turbulence of D 958. The late Schubert contains serenity, abyss, and dark drive, and the three sonatas, in C minor, A major, and B-flat major, can almost be heard as a progression through these states, from the dark drive of D 958 through the desolation-and-eruption of D 959 to the shadowed peace of D 960.

#### The harmonic move by thirds at its most profound

Everything you have learned about Schubert's harmonic adventurousness, the love of distant, glowing keys, the move by thirds, the major-minor switch, comes to fruition in the late sonatas, where it operates with a depth and a structural significance it never reached before. Listen, in the D 960 first movement especially, for the moments when the music slides to a remote key and the light changes utterly, opening onto luminous, distant harmonic regions. In the late sonatas these harmonic journeys are not local color but the very substance of the music's meaning: the movement _is_ its harmonic journey, the traveling to far places and the finding of the way home.

This is the harmonic fingerprint (Threading B) at its most profound, and it is what most distinguishes the late Schubert sonata from any other music. Where Beethoven's harmonic drama is one of tension and resolution, of conflict driven to a goal, Schubert's is one of exploration and wonder, of stepping through doors into unexpected rooms of light, of harmonic landscapes traversed at leisure. The D 960 first movement is, in a sense, a vast harmonic journey through glowing distant keys, anchored by the serene opening and shadowed by the trill, and its sublimity lies in the breadth and beauty of that journey. Chapter 18 collects the harmonic move by thirds across the catalogue; the late sonatas are its deepest realization, and hearing it here, operating on the largest scale and at the greatest depth, prepares you to recognize it everywhere in Schubert.

### 10.4 The Stories

#### The most extraordinary final year

It is worth pausing on the scale of Schubert's 1828, because it is one of the wonders of musical history. In this single year, his last, Schubert completed the _Great_ C major Symphony (Chapter 17), the String Quintet in C major (Chapter 14), these three piano sonatas, the songs gathered as _Schwanengesang_ (Chapter 8), the Mass in E-flat, the Fantasy in F minor for piano four hands (Chapter 11), and more. Any one of these would secure a composer's reputation; together they constitute one of the greatest concentrated outpourings in the history of the art, and they were produced by a man of thirty-one whose health was failing. The comparison often drawn is with the final periods of Bach and Beethoven, but those were old men summing up long lives; Schubert was young, in what should have been mid-career, and the "late" works are late only because his life was cut short. This is the central poignancy of Schubert: his "late period" is the late period of a young man, and the maturity, depth, and valedictory weight we hear in the late sonatas come from artistic development compressed into thirty-one years, not from old age. Had he lived another forty years, like Verdi, what we call his late period would be his early maturity. The late sonatas are the work of a young master at the height of his powers, and their depth is the measure of how far that genius had traveled in so little time.

#### Not deathbed farewells

The book has insisted, and insists again here because the temptation is so strong, that the late sonatas are not conscious deathbed farewells. Schubert did not know he was dying when he wrote them in September 1828; his final illness began only in November. He was making plans, studying counterpoint with a teacher (he wanted to strengthen his command of fugue), hoping for performances and publications, looking forward, not back. The sonatas are forward-looking works by a composer expecting to continue, not valedictions written in the knowledge of the end.

This matters because it changes how we hear them. If they were deathbed farewells, their depth and their shadows would be explained by the composer's knowledge of his approaching death, a biographical key. But they are not, and so their depth must be explained by Schubert's artistic maturity, by what his music had become, not by his medical situation. The shadow beneath D 960's beauty, the abyss in D 959, the dark drive of D 958, these are achievements of artistic vision, not symptoms of a death sentence. This is, in the end, more moving than the deathbed reading: the dying man was not writing farewells but simply writing the greatest music he could, looking ahead, and death took him in the middle of it. The late sonatas are not a planned conclusion but an interrupted continuation, the natural late flowering of a genius who expected more time. That they happen to be among the last things he wrote is an accident of fate, not a design, and the reader should resist the powerful pull to hear them as the composer's conscious last words.

#### The long neglect and the modern rediscovery

Like the rest of Schubert's sonatas, the late three suffered the same century-long neglect described in Chapter 9: published about a decade after his death (dedicated, by the publisher, to Robert Schumann), dismissed as too long and too diffuse, rarely played. Their recognition as supreme masterpieces is a twentieth-century achievement, the work of the same advocates, Schnabel, Richter, Brendel, Kempff, who rehabilitated the sonatas generally; and by the later twentieth century D 960 in particular had become one of the most revered works in the entire piano literature, routinely compared to Beethoven's late quartets. The rehabilitation is a triumph of listening, of learning to hear the late Schubert on his own terms rather than against Beethoven's.

#### Recording history briefly

The late sonatas, and D 960 above all, are among the most-recorded works in the piano literature, and their central interpretive question is pace and scale, especially in the D 960 first movement. At one pole stands Sviatoslav Richter, whose extremely slow, repeat-observing reading conceives the movement as a vast, almost static contemplation (and, for some, too slow); at another, a more flowing approach (Brendel, the recommended primary, and Lewis, Lupu, Uchida) finds the movement's beauty in a more moving tempo. Both reveal real things about the music. Because these works are so open to different conceptions of time, they reward hearing several pianists, and the comparison of a fast and a slow D 960 first movement is one of the most instructive exercises in all of close listening: the same notes, two different experiences of time and meaning. (The fuller piano roster is in Chapter 20.)

#### Cross-piece links you will hear later

The late sonatas connect across the volume.

To the late songs of _Schwanengesang_ (Chapter 8), especially the Heine settings, which share the late sonatas' harmonic and emotional world. The abyss of the D 959 Andantino and the bareness of "Der Doppelgänger" are the same composer reaching the same extremity in the same months; the late songs and late sonatas are one universe.

To the String Quintet in C major (Chapter 14), also of 1828, which shares the late style's spaciousness, harmonic depth, and the holding-together of beauty and darkness; the Quintet's sublime Adagio is the chamber-music sibling of D 960's first movement, and its turbulent central eruption the sibling of D 959's.

To the _Great_ C major Symphony (Chapter 17), also of 1828, which carries the late style's "heavenly length" and spaciousness into the orchestra.

To Chapter 18, where the late-style holding-together of beauty and darkness, the deepest of the fingerprints, is gathered; the late sonatas are its fullest realization in the piano music.

#### The September 1828 context (Thread A note)

The three sonatas are dated September 1828, two months before Schubert's death on November 19. They sit at the very end of the most productive year of his life, alongside the String Quintet, the _Great_ C major Symphony, and the _Schwanengesang_ songs. He was thirty-one. His health had been precarious since 1822; in the autumn of 1828 it began its final decline; in early November the illness that would kill him (typhoid fever, or another infection, the dispute is Chapter 19's subject) took hold; and on November 19 he died. The late sonatas were among the last large works he completed. They are not a farewell, as the book has insisted, but the natural culmination of his piano writing, cut off, like everything else, by an early and unexpected death. The next chapter completes the piano part with the shorter works, the Impromptus and Moments Musicaux, which show another side of the late Schubert: not the vast, profound sonata but the perfect miniature, the character piece that would become one of the central forms of the Romantic century and that influenced Chopin and all who came after.

Listen for the trill. What does it do to the serenity for you? Do you want to know what it 'means,' or is the not-knowing part of its power? Do you agree that this is more moving than the farewell reading? How much does knowing (or not knowing) the circumstances of a work's creation change how you hear it? Should it?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find the Brendel recordings, and ideally a contrasting D 960 (Richter or Lewis). This pass is for the relationships the Listening Window only pointed at, how shadow and serenity, calm and eruption, fast and slow, sit together.

  1. **The serenity and the trill in D 960's opening.** Sink into the broad, slow, song-like opening melody, then listen for the soft, low trill that interrupts it and the silence after. Hear how the trill shadows the beauty. Then notice it returning at structural junctures, each time reintroducing the darkness beneath the peace.

  2. **The eruption in D 959's Andantino (about 3:00).** Listen to the desolate opening song, then to the shocking central storm, the dissonant, fragmented, near-violent breakdown, and then to the return of the desolation, now changed by what you have heard. This is one of the most extreme passages in Schubert.

  3. **The harmonic restlessness of D 958's finale.** Track the galloping C-minor tarantella and, above all, its constant shifting of harmonic ground, the music never settling, always pressing into new and distant keys. This is the dark, agitated Schubert at full stretch.

  4. **The experience of time (compare two D 960 recordings).** Listen to the D 960 first movement in a slow, repeat-observing reading (Richter) and a more flowing one (Brendel or Lewis). Notice how completely the pace changes the meaning, the same notes producing a vast static contemplation or a moving lyrical journey. This is the deepest lesson of the late style: that its sublimity lives in its handling of time.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** (the song-sonata unity): "Der Doppelgänger" from _Schwanengesang_ (Chapter 8) and the D 959 Andantino back to back. Both reach the harmonic and emotional extremity of Schubert's final year, in song and in sonata; hearing them together reveals the unity of the late style across the song-instrumental divide (Threading B at its deepest).

**Listen** (all three sonatas): the complete trilogy, D 958, D 959, D 960, in order, ideally across a few sittings. Heard together they trace a progression from the dark drive of the C minor through the desolation-and-eruption of the A major to the shadowed peace of the B-flat, the late Schubert's full emotional range in one trilogy.

**Listen** (two D 960s): a slow Richter and a flowing Lewis or Brendel, for the lesson in how pace shapes meaning. One of the most instructive comparisons in all of close listening.

**Read** : Charles Rosen, _The Romantic Generation_ , Harvard University Press, 1995, on the late sonatas and Schubert's handling of large-scale form and harmony. Alfred Brendel's essays on Schubert (in his collected musical writings) are the pianist's-eye view from the artist who did most to establish these works' greatness; his discussion of the D 960 trill and the late style is illuminating and accessible.

## Chapter 11: Impromptus, Moments Musicaux, and the Other Solo Piano

The late sonatas showed Schubert building vast structures. This chapter shows him doing the opposite: writing perfect short pieces, the Impromptus and Moments Musicaux, miniatures that say something complete in three to seven minutes. These small works are, for many listeners, the easiest doorway into Schubert's piano music, more immediately lovable than the great sonatas and just as deeply Schubertian. They are also historically important: Schubert, with Beethoven's Bagatelles and a few others, essentially invented the Romantic "character piece," the short, self-contained piano work with a single mood or image, which would become one of the central forms of the nineteenth century, the form of Chopin's Nocturnes, Schumann's miniatures, Brahms's late piano pieces, and countless others.

The chapter anchors on two of the eight Impromptus and a Moment Musical, and it adds one listening skill that completes the piano part: how to hear a miniature, how a short piece achieves completeness, how it differs from a sonata movement, and why the small form suited Schubert's gifts as well as the large one did. It also closes the solo-piano story by gesturing to the four-hand music, the social piano repertoire that was the daily bread of the Schubertiade.

### 11.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote

Vienna, 1827. Schubert, in the last full year before the year of his death, writes two sets of four pieces each, eight in all, that his publisher will issue under the title _Impromptus_. The word means "improvised," "on the spur of the moment," and it was a fashionable label of the 1820s for a short, free-standing piano piece, suggesting spontaneity and informality. The pieces are not actually improvised, of course; they are carefully made. But the title captures something true about them: they feel spontaneous, like a single thought beautifully turned, without the architecture and argument of a sonata. They are, in effect, songs without words, or character sketches, or moods caught and held.

The Impromptus, like so much of Schubert's piano music, were not fully appreciated in his time and were long overshadowed by the sonatas debate, but they have become, over the past century, among the most beloved and most-played of all his works, staples of the recital hall and of countless amateur pianists' music stands. The third of the first set, in G-flat major, with its rippling accompaniment and its long, serene melody, is one of the most popular pieces Schubert ever wrote. The Moments Musicaux (a French title, "musical moments"), a set of six even shorter pieces published in 1828 but composed across several earlier years, are their close cousins, tiny gems, including the famous third, a wistful little piece in F minor that has the lilt of a folk dance.

There is a historical resonance worth noting at the outset. These short, lyrical, single-mood piano pieces point directly toward Chopin, whose Nocturnes, Preludes, and other miniatures would refine the character piece into one of the supreme achievements of Romantic piano music (Chopin is the subject of _Close Listening: Chopin_ in this series). Schubert did not influence Chopin as directly as he influenced Liszt through the _Wanderer_ Fantasy, the two composers' paths were largely separate, but the historical kinship is real: Schubert was among the first to make the short, song-like piano piece a serious art form, and the nineteenth-century character piece grows from roots he helped plant. When you listen to the Impromptus, you are hearing the dawn of one of the century's great forms.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen

  1. **Completeness in miniature.** Each Impromptu and Moment Musical is a complete musical statement in a few minutes, no long development, no large architecture, just a single idea or mood, presented, explored a little, and rounded off. Listen for the sense of wholeness on a small scale, how a three- or five-minute piece can feel as complete as a half-hour sonata. The art of the miniature is to say one thing perfectly rather than many things at length.

  2. **Song without words.** Most of these pieces are essentially songs for the piano: a singing melody (the "voice") over an accompaniment (the "piano part"), exactly the texture of a Lied, but with no singer. Listen for the melody as something a voice could sing, and for the accompaniment supporting it, the same voice-and-piano relationship you know from the songs, now both hands of one pianist. This is Threading B in a quiet form: the song-composer's instinct shaping the piano music.

  3. **The Schubert fingerprints in small.** The harmonic move by thirds, the major-minor switch, the glowing modulation, all the late-Schubert fingerprints appear in these miniatures, but compressed and clear. Listen, in the G-flat Impromptu especially, for the moments when the harmony shifts and the light changes, and in the F minor Moment Musical for the wistful major-minor coloring. The miniatures are an excellent place to hear the fingerprints cleanly, because there is less around them.

#### One-sentence background

This chapter anchors on Schubert's short solo-piano works of his last years: the Impromptus, D 899 (Op. 90) and D 935 (Op. 142), two sets of four pieces each composed in 1827, with focus on D 899 No. 3 in G-flat major (a serene song over a rippling accompaniment) and D 935 No. 2 in A-flat major (a gentle, dance-like piece); plus the Moments Musicaux, D 780, six short pieces published in 1828, with focus on No. 3 in F minor (a wistful miniature with a folk-dance lilt); and a brief look at the Fantasy in F minor for piano four hands, D 940 (1828); the **Primary Recording** for this chapter is Alfred Brendel (Philips) for the Impromptus and Moments Musicaux, chosen for clarity and lyrical warmth; Radu Lupu (Decca) and Mitsuko Uchida (Philips) are essential alternatives.

#### Search Hints

EN: "Schubert Impromptus D 899 D 935 Brendel" or "Schubert Moments Musicaux D 780 Lupu" ZH: "舒伯特 即兴曲 D899 D935 布伦德尔" 或 "舒伯特 音乐瞬间 D780 卢普" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

**Alternative recordings** within the recording-hallucination safeguard (≥10 years old OR ≥3 reviews):

* Radu Lupu (Decca, 1980s), a beloved reading of the Impromptus, warm, singing, and inward.
* Mitsuko Uchida (Philips), poetic and refined, especially fine in the more lyrical pieces.
* Krystian Zimerman (Deutsche Grammophon), a polished, beautifully voiced reading of the Impromptus.
* For the four-hand Fantasy in F minor, D 940: many distinguished duo recordings exist (for example by piano duos on major labels); look for pairings with multiple reviews.

### 11.2 The Listening Window

This Window treats the three short anchor pieces and gestures at the four-hand Fantasy. These are miniatures, so navigate by piece. Timings refer to the Brendel readings.

#### Impromptu in G-flat major, D 899 No. 3

**0:00 onward.** This is one of the most serenely beautiful pieces Schubert wrote. The left hand and inner texture set up a continuous, gently rippling accompaniment, a soft, flowing background, and over it the melody sings, long, calm, song-like, in the warm key of G-flat major. The whole piece is essentially one long, sustained melody floating over the rippling accompaniment, a "song without words" of great tenderness. Listen for the singing line and the rippling support (the texture of a Lied), and for the moments where the harmony shifts to shadow the serenity briefly before returning to peace. There is no drama, no development in the sonata sense; the piece simply unfolds its lovely melody, lets it deepen, and closes. The art is in the purity and completeness of a single beautiful idea. Notice how complete it feels despite its modest scale.

#### Impromptu in A-flat major, D 935 No. 2

**0:00 onward.** A gentler, more dance-like piece, in a moderate triple time with the lilt of a slow minuet or Ländler (an Austrian country dance). The main section is graceful and songful; a central section offers a contrast (a more flowing, lyrical middle) before the opening returns. Listen for the dance-like grace, the songful melody, and the simple, satisfying shape (opening section, contrasting middle, return), the basic architecture of the character piece. This is Schubert at his most charming and accessible, and the modest three-part form is the standard form of the miniature.

#### Moment Musical in F minor, D 780 No. 3

**0:00 onward.** A tiny piece, under two minutes, and one of Schubert's most famous miniatures. It has the lilt of a folk dance, light and a little wistful, in F minor but with a bittersweet, almost playful quality. The melody is simple and instantly memorable; the piece states it, varies it slightly, and is gone. Listen for the dance-lilt, the wistful major-minor coloring (the way the minor key is tinged with something almost cheerful), and the sheer economy, a complete, characterful piece in a tiny span. The Moments Musicaux show the miniature at its most concentrated: a single mood caught perfectly in the briefest space.

#### Fantasy in F minor for piano four hands, D 940 (brief)

The four-hand Fantasy is not a miniature but a large, serious work for two players at one piano, one of the masterpieces of the four-hand repertoire. It opens with a haunting, melancholy melody (one of Schubert's most beautiful) that recurs through the work, binding its sections cyclically (a relative of the _Wanderer_ Fantasy's principle). If you can hear it, listen for the recurring opening theme and for the richness two players can draw from one piano. The four-hand Fantasy is the bridge from the solo-piano world to the social music-making of the Schubertiade, which the chapter's Stories section takes up.

### 11.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### The art of the miniature: completeness without architecture

The central listening skill of this chapter is hearing how a miniature achieves completeness. A sonata movement is built on architecture: themes presented, developed (taken apart and recombined), and recapitulated, a large structure with internal drama and argument. A miniature does none of this. It presents a single idea or mood, explores it a little (often in a simple three-part shape: an opening section, a contrasting middle, a return), and rounds it off. There is no development in the sonata sense, no long argument, no large drama. And yet the best miniatures feel complete, as satisfying in their way as a sonata is in its.

How? By concentration and by perfection of a single thing. A miniature cannot say many things, so it says one thing perfectly: one melody, one mood, one image, fully realized in a small space. The G-flat Impromptu does not argue or develop; it sings one long, beautiful song and lets that be enough, and it is enough, because the song is perfect and complete in itself. The art is the art of the perfect short poem versus the novel: different scales, different pleasures, both complete. Learning to hear the miniature on its own terms, not to wish it were longer or more developed, but to receive the perfection of its single statement, is the skill this chapter adds. It is a different listening from the late sonatas, where the pleasure was in vast unfolding; here the pleasure is in concentration, in a complete world held in a few minutes. Both are central to Schubert, and the pianist who plays both the late sonatas and the Impromptus is showing the two poles of his keyboard art: the sublime vast structure and the perfect small statement.

#### Song without words: the Lied at the piano

These pieces are, in their texture and their melodic instinct, songs without words. The G-flat Impromptu is the clearest case: a singing melody over a flowing accompaniment, the exact texture of a Lied, but with both parts played by one pianist and no singer. This is Threading B (the song-instrumental interpenetration) in its quietest, most pervasive form. There is no borrowed song-tune here as there was in the _Wanderer_ Fantasy or the _Trout_ Quintet; rather, the song-composer's whole way of thinking, melody-over-accompaniment, the singing line as the heart of the music, shapes the piano writing.

This is worth dwelling on because it reveals how deeply the Lied penetrated all of Schubert's music. He was, before anything, a melodist and a song-composer, and even when writing for solo piano with no text in sight, he thought like a song-writer: he gave the piano a singing voice and a supporting accompaniment, he shaped melodies as if for a singer, he let the "song" be the substance of the piece. The Impromptus and Moments Musicaux are perhaps the purest demonstration that, for Schubert, the boundary between song and instrumental music was thin to the point of transparency. When you listen to the G-flat Impromptu, try hearing it as a wordless Lied: imagine a voice singing the melody, the pianist's other hand accompanying. That is, in essence, what it is. The skill of hearing the song inside the instrumental piece, launched with the _Wanderer_ Fantasy's borrowed tune, here becomes the subtler skill of hearing the song-composer's instinct shaping music that borrows no specific song at all. It is everywhere in Schubert once you listen for it.

#### The fingerprints in miniature

The Impromptus and Moments Musicaux are an excellent place to hear Schubert's harmonic fingerprints cleanly, because the small scale isolates them. In a vast sonata movement, the harmonic move by thirds or the major-minor switch is one event among many in a large structure; in a three-minute miniature, it stands out clearly. Listen, in the G-flat Impromptu, for the moments when the serene harmony shifts to a shadowed or distant key and the light changes, the harmonic move by thirds, the same device as in the late sonatas, now isolated in a small frame. Listen, in the F minor Moment Musical, for the major-minor coloring, the way the minor-key dance is tinged with brightness, the major-minor switch in miniature.

This is a useful exercise precisely because the miniatures are short and clear: having heard the fingerprints operate at vast scale and depth in the late sonatas (Chapter 10), you can now hear them in concentrated, legible form in the Impromptus, and the comparison sharpens your ear for both. The same harmonic instincts that make the D 960 first movement profound make the G-flat Impromptu lovely; the difference is scale and depth, not kind. Chapter 18 will collect these fingerprints across the whole catalogue, and the miniatures are among the clearest specimens, the fingerprints displayed, as it were, under glass, small and distinct.

#### The character piece and the Romantic future

A historical word, because these miniatures are not just lovely but pivotal. The "character piece," the short, self-contained piano work with a single mood, title, or image, became one of the defining forms of nineteenth-century music, and Schubert was among its founders. Before the 1820s, serious piano music meant mainly the sonata and the variation set; the short lyric piano piece existed (in dances, in Beethoven's Bagatelles) but was not yet a major serious form. Schubert's Impromptus and Moments Musicaux, along with a few contemporaries' work, helped establish the short lyric piece as a vehicle for serious musical expression, a complete artistic statement in miniature.

What followed is the history of Romantic piano music: Chopin's Nocturnes, Preludes, and Études; Schumann's character-piece collections (_Kinderszenen_ , _Carnaval_); Mendelssohn's _Songs Without Words_ (the very name confirms the song-without-words idea); Brahms's late Intermezzi and Klavierstücke; and on through Grieg, Debussy, and beyond. The whole tradition of the short, lyrical, single-mood piano piece, one of the richest veins in all of music, grows from roots Schubert helped plant. When you play or hear an Impromptu, you are at the source of this tradition, and the song-without-words instinct that animates it, the singing melody over accompaniment, is Schubert's signature contribution. The miniatures, modest as they seem beside the great sonatas, are thus among Schubert's most historically consequential piano works, the seed of a century of beloved music.

### 11.4 The Stories

#### The Schubertiade and the social piano

The Impromptus and Moments Musicaux belong to a world the book has mentioned repeatedly but can now describe fully: the Schubertiade, the musical evenings held by Schubert's circle of friends, at which his music was first played and sung. These were private gatherings in the homes of friends, the Spauns, the Sonnleithners, and others, where Schubert and his companions made music, read poetry, danced, talked, and drank, evenings of intimate sociability in the censored, inward Vienna of Metternich. Schubert often played the piano at these gatherings, accompanying his own songs (the singer Johann Michael Vogl was a frequent partner) and playing solo and four-hand piano music. The dances he wrote by the hundreds (waltzes, Ländler, ecossaises) were for actual dancing at these evenings; the four-hand piano music was for friends to play together; and the shorter solo pieces, the Impromptus and Moments Musicaux, belong to this world of music made among friends in a room.

This social context matters for how we hear the music. Much of Schubert's piano output, especially the dances and the four-hand works, was not "concert music" in the modern sense, written for a paying public in a hall, but "music for use," written for friends to play and dance to at home. This gives it a particular quality, intimate, unpretentious, companionable, that is part of its charm and that distinguishes Schubert from the public, heroic Beethoven. The Schubertiade was Schubert's true musical home: not the concert hall, which largely ignored him, but the private room full of friends. The Impromptus and Moments Musicaux, even as they became, after his death, concert and recital staples, carry the trace of that intimate origin, music made among friends, for the pleasure of playing and listening together. Understanding the Schubertiade is understanding the social soil from which most of Schubert's music grew, and it is the natural bridge to the chamber music of the next chapters, which was likewise, in large part, music for friends in a room.

#### The four-hand repertoire: music for friends

A special word for the four-hand piano music, because it is the most "Schubertiade" of all Schubert's piano output and the least known to general listeners. Schubert wrote a large and superb body of music for piano four hands, two players at one keyboard, including the great Fantasy in F minor, D 940 (1828), the _Grand Duo_ sonata, sets of variations, marches, polonaises, and many dances. Four-hand music was domestic music par excellence: it required two players to sit together at one instrument, sharing the keyboard, an inherently social, companionable form, perfect for friends making music together at home. Schubert, who often played four-hand music with friends and pupils, poured real seriousness into the medium, and the F minor Fantasy in particular is one of his late masterpieces, a large, cyclically organized work of haunting beauty that deserves to be far better known.

The four-hand music is a reminder that a great deal of Schubert's output was conceived for the intimate, social, amateur-and-friends context rather than the public concert hall, and that this is not a limitation but a defining quality. The F minor Fantasy is great music that happens also to be music for two friends at a piano, and its greatness and its sociability are not in tension. For the listener who has worked through the great sonatas and the songs, the four-hand music (and the dances, and the shorter pieces) opens another, gentler region of Schubert: the composer at home among friends, making music for the pleasure of making it. It is well worth exploring, and the F minor Fantasy is the place to start.

#### Recording history briefly

The recommended primary is Alfred Brendel, with Radu Lupu (especially beloved for its inward, singing quality) and Mitsuko Uchida as alternatives. Because these are miniatures, they reward a pianist's ability to characterize a mood quickly and completely, and a single disc of the Impromptus and Moments Musicaux by a fine Schubertian is one of the most rewarding and accessible entry points to his piano music, more immediately lovable than the great sonatas and just as deeply characteristic. (The four-hand music, and the fuller piano roster, are in Chapter 20.)

#### Cross-piece links you will hear later

This chapter connects across the volume.

To the chamber music (Part V, Chapters 12-14), through the Schubertiade context: the social, music-for-friends quality of the four-hand and shorter piano works is the same world that produced the _Trout_ Quintet and the quartets, chamber music made among friends in a room.

To the late sonatas (Chapter 10), as the miniature complement to the vast structure: the same harmonic fingerprints, the same song-without-words instinct, at opposite scales. The Impromptus are the clearest place to hear the fingerprints in concentrated form.

To _Close Listening: Chopin_ , for the Nocturnes and other character pieces that refined the form Schubert helped found; the historical kinship between Schubert's Impromptus and Chopin's miniatures is one of the threads connecting the single-composer volumes.

#### The 1827-1828 context (Thread A note)

The Impromptus come from 1827, the year of _Winterreise_ , and the Moments Musicaux and the four-hand Fantasy from 1828, the final year. They are, like the late sonatas, products of Schubert's last creative surge, but they show its other face: alongside the vast, profound late sonatas and the bleak late songs, Schubert was also writing these perfect, lyrical, often serene miniatures. The same final years that produced the abyss of "Der Doppelgänger" and the shadowed sublimity of D 960 produced the pure loveliness of the G-flat Impromptu and the wistful charm of the F minor Moment Musical. This is the fullness of the late Schubert: not only depth and darkness, but also serenity, charm, and the perfect small statement. The miniatures balance the picture, a reminder that the dying composer of 1827-1828 was, to the end, a melodist of irresistible warmth as well as an explorer of the depths. With the piano music complete, the book turns to the chamber music, where the Schubertiade's social music-making reaches its highest artistic form, beginning with the work that, more than any other, embodies the sunny, companionable Schubert: the _Trout_ Quintet, built on his own cheerful song "Die Forelle," and first played among friends on a summer holiday.

### Conversation Seeds

Listen to the G-flat Impromptu (D 899 No. 3). Does it feel as complete to you as a sonata movement, despite its modest scale? What is the particular pleasure of a perfect miniature, and how does it differ from the pleasure of a vast structure like the D 960 sonata?"_

* _"These piano pieces are, the chapter argues, 'songs without words', the singing-melody-over-accompaniment texture of a Lied, with no singer, revealing how deeply the song-composer's instinct shaped all of Schubert's music, even where no actual song is borrowed. Try hearing the G-flat Impromptu as a wordless Lied: imagine a voice singing the melody. Does this change how you hear it? Do you think instrumental music can be 'song-like' in a way you can actually hear, or is that just a metaphor?"_

The chapter argues this intimate, companionable, amateur-and-friends origin 'is not a limitation but a defining quality.' How does it change your sense of the music to know it was made for friends in a room rather than for a concert hall? Do you think music made for intimate, social settings has a different character from music made for public performance, and can you hear the difference?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find the Brendel, Lupu, or Uchida recordings (or the alternatives in §11.1). Attend to these things.

  1. **Completeness in the G-flat Impromptu (D 899 No. 3).** Listen for how the piece achieves wholeness without development or large architecture, simply by singing one long, perfect melody over a rippling accompaniment and rounding it off. Receive the perfection of the single statement rather than wishing for more.

  2. **Song without words.** In the G-flat Impromptu especially, hear the Lied texture: a singing melody (the "voice") over a flowing accompaniment (the "piano part"), both played by one pianist. Imagine a singer on the melody. This is the song-composer's instinct shaping the piano music (Threading B in its quietest form).

  3. **The fingerprints in miniature.** Listen for the harmonic move by thirds (the shift to a distant key, the change of light) in the G-flat Impromptu, and for the major-minor coloring in the F minor Moment Musical. The small scale isolates these fingerprints and makes them clear, the same devices that operate at vast scale in the late sonatas, here under glass.

  4. **The dance-lilt and economy of the Moment Musical (D 780 No. 3).** Attend to how much character is caught in under two minutes, the folk-dance lilt, the wistful coloring, the instantly memorable tune, and how complete the tiny piece feels. The Moments Musicaux are the miniature at its most concentrated.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** (the two scales): the G-flat Impromptu (D 899 No. 3) and the first movement of the B-flat Sonata D 960 (Chapter 10) in one sitting. Hear the two poles of Schubert's piano art, the perfect miniature and the sublime vast structure, sharing the same harmonic fingerprints and the same song-like instinct, differing in scale and depth, not in kind.

**Listen** (the four-hand world): the Fantasy in F minor for piano four hands, D 940, in any fine duo recording. One of Schubert's late masterpieces and the gateway to the rich four-hand repertoire, music for two friends at one piano. The haunting opening melody, which recurs cyclically, is among his most beautiful.

**Listen** (the Romantic descendants): a Chopin Nocturne after a Schubert Impromptu, to hear the character-piece tradition that grew from roots Schubert helped plant. The song-without-words instinct connects them across the generation.

**Read** : Charles Rosen, _The Romantic Generation_ , Harvard University Press, 1995, on the character piece and the short lyric forms of the early Romantic period. For the pianist's perspective, Alfred Brendel's essays on Schubert's piano music are illuminating on the Impromptus as well as the sonatas. The Schubertiade context is richly documented in the major biographies (Newbould, Gibbs, Byrne Bodley) and in Deutsch's documentary collection, which preserves friends' accounts of the musical evenings.
