## Chapter 12: The Trout Quintet D 667 (1819) + the Documented Schubertiade

You met the _Trout_ Quintet once, in _Close Listening: Classical Music_ , as one of the gateways to chamber music. This chapter brings it back into the full Schubert context and uses it to open Part V, the chamber music, the region where the Schubertiade's social music-making reaches its highest artistic form. The _Trout_ Quintet is the sunniest, most companionable major work Schubert wrote, music of pure summer pleasure, and it is also the clearest and most delightful demonstration of Threading B, the song-instrumental interpenetration, because its famous fourth movement is a set of variations on Schubert's own cheerful song "Die Forelle" ("The Trout").

The chapter does several things. It deepens the song-into-instrument listening skill you began with the _Wanderer_ Fantasy, now in its happiest form: a sunny song becoming a sunny chamber movement. It introduces the unusual scoring of the quintet and why it matters. And it uses the work as the occasion to describe, fully and concretely, the Schubertiade, the circle of friends and the world of music-among-friends that produced so much of Schubert's chamber music, because the _Trout_ Quintet, written on a summer holiday for a group of amateur music-lovers, is the Schubertiade spirit made into a masterpiece.

### 12.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote

Summer 1819. Schubert, twenty-two, in the happy early-maturity years before the 1822 illness, takes a holiday in the town of Steyr, in the beautiful Upper Austrian countryside, with his friend the singer Johann Michael Vogl. Steyr is Vogl's hometown, and the two spend a sunny summer among music-loving friends in idyllic surroundings; Schubert's letters from this trip are among the most cheerful he ever wrote, full of delight in the scenery and the company. Among the people he meets is a well-to-do amateur cellist named Sylvester Paumgartner, a music patron who hosts musical evenings at his home and who commissions Schubert to write a piano quintet for his circle to play. Paumgartner has a particular request: he loves Schubert's song "Die Forelle" ("The Trout") and asks that the quintet include a set of variations on it.

Schubert obliges, and the result is the _Trout_ Quintet, written that summer or shortly after, for Paumgartner's amateur group to play, with the song-variations fourth movement at its heart. The work is, in every way, a product of the holiday spirit: sunny, generous, full of pleasure, written for friends and amateurs to enjoy playing together, not for a concert hall or for posterity. And it has become one of the best-loved chamber works ever written, precisely because that holiday happiness shines through every bar. It is the great example of the Schubertiade ideal, music made among friends for the joy of it, raised to the level of a masterpiece.

The song behind it, "Die Forelle," composed around 1817, is itself a delight: a light, playful setting of a poem about a trout swimming happily in a clear brook, which a fisherman, after some effort, finally catches by muddying the water. The song has a famous, instantly memorable tune and a rippling piano accompaniment depicting the darting fish and the flowing water. (The poem has a faintly moralizing final verse, often omitted, warning young women against deceptive men, but the song's spirit is overwhelmingly one of bright, watery playfulness.) When Schubert built a quintet movement on this song, he imported its whole sunny, watery world into the chamber music, and the _Trout_ Quintet takes its nickname, and its character, from the fish.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen

  1. **The sunny, generous character throughout.** The whole quintet, not just the trout movement, radiates a warm, relaxed, holiday happiness. There is no tragedy here, no abyss, just the pleasure of music-making among friends in summer. Listen for the generosity and ease of it, the unhurried delight. This is the sunniest face of Schubert, the companionable major-key warmth that balances the depths of _Winterreise_ and the late sonatas.

  2. **The unusual scoring, and the singing cello.** The quintet is scored not for the usual piano quintet (piano plus string quartet) but for piano, violin, viola, cello, and double bass, replacing the second violin of a normal quartet with a double bass. This puts a deep bass instrument at the bottom, which frees the cello to sing in its warm tenor register rather than holding down the bass line. Listen for the rich, deep foundation (the double bass) and for the cello singing high and warm above it. The scoring gives the quintet its particular glowing, full sound.

  3. **The trout song and its variations (fourth movement).** The fourth movement presents the tune of "Die Forelle" and then varies it, each variation giving the melody to a different instrument and dressing it in different figuration, with the rippling "water" music returning near the end. Listen for the famous tune, then for how it is passed around and transformed, and for the return of the song's original watery accompaniment toward the close. This is Threading B at its most delightful and obvious: the song you can hum, turned into a chamber-music movement.

#### One-sentence background

The _Trout_ Quintet in A major, D 667, is a piano quintet composed in 1819 (during or just after a happy summer holiday in Steyr) for the amateur cellist Sylvester Paumgartner's circle, scored unusually for piano, violin, viola, cello, and double bass (rather than the standard piano-plus-quartet), in five movements, and famous above all for its fourth movement, a set of variations on Schubert's own song "Die Forelle" ("The Trout"), D 550 (c. 1817); the whole work radiates a sunny, companionable warmth and is one of the best-loved chamber works ever written; the **Primary Recording** for this chapter is the recording featuring the pianist Sir Clifford Curzon with members of the Vienna Octet (Decca, 1950s-style classic) or, for a modern reference, the recording by Alfred Brendel with the Cleveland Quartet members (Philips); for a celebrated historic option, the recording with Curzon is a touchstone.

#### Search Hints

EN: "Schubert Trout Quintet D 667 Curzon Vienna Octet" or "Schubert Trout Quintet Brendel" or "Schubert Die Forelle quintet" ZH: "舒伯特 鳟鱼五重奏 D667 柯曾 维也纳八重奏" 或 "舒伯特 鳟鱼钢琴五重奏 布伦德尔" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

**Alternative recordings** within the recording-hallucination safeguard (≥10 years old OR ≥3 reviews):

* Sir Clifford Curzon with members of the Vienna Octet (Decca), a classic, warm, idiomatic reading, a long-standing reference.
* Alfred Brendel with members of the Cleveland Quartet and a bassist (Philips), clear and lyrical.
* The recording with the pianist Sviatoslav Richter and the Borodin Quartet members (various labels), a powerful, characterful reading.
* The Schubert Ensemble, the Nash Ensemble, and many chamber groups have fine modern versions; look for those with multiple reviews.
* For the song "Die Forelle," D 550: Dietrich Fischer-Dieskau with Gerald Moore, or Elly Ameling, to hear the source.

### 12.2 The Listening Window

This Window walks through the quintet with focus on the fourth (trout) movement and the first movement. Timings refer to a typical reading; navigate by movement.

**First movement (Allegro vivace), 0:00.** The quintet opens with a bright, expansive movement in A major. After an introductory gesture, the main themes unfold, generous, songful, sunny. Listen for the rich, full sound the unusual scoring produces (the deep double bass at the bottom, the cello singing warmly above it) and for the relaxed, spacious unfolding, this is holiday music, in no hurry, delighting in its own melodies. Notice the glowing shifts to distant keys (the Schubert fingerprint), used here for warmth and color rather than drama.

**Second movement (Andante), about 9:00.** A lyrical slow movement, gentle and songful, moving through a sequence of warmly contrasting sections and keys. Listen for the singing melodies and the relaxed, contented mood; even the slow movement here is untroubled, a summer reverie rather than a lament.

**Third movement (Scherzo, Presto), about 18:00.** A vigorous, high-spirited scherzo with a gentler central trio. Listen for the rhythmic energy and rustic good humor, country-dance music, the Schubertiade at play.

**Fourth movement (Andantino, Theme and Variations), about 22:00.** The famous trout movement. It opens with the theme, the tune of "Die Forelle," presented simply by the strings. Then come the variations:

* The tune is given to the piano, decorated, while the strings accompany.
* It passes to the viola and cello, with the violin adding figuration above.
* A more dramatic, minor-tinged variation brings a brief shadow.
* The mood brightens again, the tune passed around.
* And crucially, near the end, the original rippling "water" accompaniment of the song returns, the darting-fish, flowing-brook figuration from "Die Forelle" itself, now in the strings and piano, so that the movement ends by recreating the actual texture of the song. Listen for this return of the song's watery accompaniment; it is the moment the variations reconnect, explicitly, with their source, the trout swimming again in its brook. The whole movement is a delighted play on the beloved tune, and its return to the song's original water-music at the close is one of the happiest strokes in all of chamber music.

**Fifth movement (Finale, Allegro giusto), about 30:00.** A bright, energetic finale, dance-like and good-humored, bringing the quintet to a joyful close. Listen for the sustained high spirits and the sense of a happy gathering coming to its satisfying end.

### 12.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### Song into chamber music: Threading B at its happiest

The _Trout_ Quintet's fourth movement is the most delightful and obvious example in the whole book of Threading B, the song-instrumental interpenetration. Schubert takes his own song "Die Forelle," tune and watery accompaniment and all, and builds a chamber-music movement on it, varying the melody and, at the close, recreating the song's original rippling water-texture. Here the thread is at its sunniest: where the _Wanderer_ Fantasy imported the homeless brooding of "Der Wanderer" into a piano epic, and "Death and the Maiden" (next chapter) will import a confrontation with death into a quartet, the _Trout_ Quintet imports the pure, bright, watery playfulness of "Die Forelle" into the chamber music.

The lesson is the same as before but in a major key: the borrowed song is not just a tune but a whole world of feeling, here a world of summer, clear water, and darting fish. The quintet does not merely use a catchy melody; it imports the song's entire sunny character, and the whole work takes its nickname and its spirit from the fish. The return of the song's actual water-accompaniment at the end of the variations is the key moment: Schubert reminds you, explicitly, that this chamber movement _is_ the song, the trout still swimming, the brook still flowing, now in five instruments instead of voice and piano. Hearing this is the chapter's central skill, and it is the most enjoyable demonstration of Threading B you will get. When you reach "Death and the Maiden" and the _Rosamunde_ Quartet (Chapter 13), you will apply the same listening, but to darker songs and darker transformations; here, learn it in its happiest form, where the song's world is pure sunlight.

#### The unusual scoring and the glowing sound

The _Trout_ Quintet's scoring, piano, violin, viola, cello, and double bass, is unusual and consequential, and it is worth understanding why, because it shapes the work's whole sound. The standard piano quintet of the later nineteenth century (Schumann's, Brahms's, Dvořák's) is piano plus string quartet (two violins, viola, cello). Schubert's quintet instead uses one each of violin, viola, and cello, and adds a double bass, the deep, low string instrument usually found at the bottom of an orchestra. (The model was probably a quintet by Hummel with the same scoring, written for the same kind of amateur ensemble.)

This change has two effects. First, the double bass provides a deep, solid foundation at the very bottom, giving the quintet a rich, full, almost orchestral sonority, a warmth and depth that the standard scoring lacks. Second, and more importantly for listening, the presence of the double bass frees the cello from its usual job of holding down the bass line, so the cello can sing in its warm, expressive tenor register, up where the melody lives, rather than anchoring the harmony below. The result is a glowing, song-rich texture: the bass instrument deep below, the cello singing warmly above it, the violin and viola in the middle, the piano weaving throughout. Listen for this, especially for the cello singing high and warm, freed by the double bass beneath it. The scoring is part of why the _Trout_ Quintet sounds so uniquely warm and full; it is a sound Schubert achieved nowhere else, and it suits the work's sunny, generous character perfectly. The unusual instrumentation is not a curiosity but a deliberate choice that gives the music its glowing voice.

#### The sunny Schubert: the major-key balance

The _Trout_ Quintet matters for the book's larger picture because it is the great example of the sunny, untroubled Schubert, and the reader who has come through _Winterreise_ , the late sonatas, and the late Heine songs needs this balance. It is easy, after the depths, to think of Schubert as essentially a composer of darkness and longing, the wanderer in the snow, the man before his ghost, the shadow beneath the beauty. But Schubert was also, and just as truly, a composer of pure happiness, warmth, and companionable pleasure, and the _Trout_ Quintet is the proof. There is no abyss here, no shadow beneath the beauty, no longing that cannot be satisfied, only summer, friendship, clear water, and the joy of music-making. The work is great not in spite of its happiness but because of it: it is a complete, generous, untroubled major-key masterpiece, and writing such a thing, sustained happiness without sentimentality or emptiness, is as rare and difficult as writing tragedy.

This sunny Schubert is not a lesser Schubert. The same composer who could face the void in "Der Doppelgänger" could also, with complete sincerity, write the holiday delight of the _Trout_ Quintet, and the wholeness of his genius lies in containing both. The book has insisted throughout that the "sweet, sentimental Schubert" reputation is a reduction; but the corrective is not to replace it with a "dark, tragic Schubert" that is equally reductive. The truth is that Schubert encompassed the full range, from the _Trout_ Quintet's sunlight to _Winterreise_ 's frost, and the _Trout_ Quintet is the indispensable evidence for the sunlit pole. Hold it against _Winterreise_ in your mind: the same composer, the summer brook and the winter snow, and you have the range of one of the most complete musical natures in history. The happiness of the _Trout_ Quintet is not naive; it is the achieved, generous happiness of a major artist who also knew the depths, and that is what makes it more than charming.

### 12.4 The Stories

#### The Steyr summer and the holiday spirit

The _Trout_ Quintet is inseparable from the happy circumstances of its creation, and they are worth knowing because they explain its character. In the summer of 1819 Schubert traveled to Steyr, in the lake-and-mountain country of Upper Austria, with the singer Johann Michael Vogl, an older man who had become one of Schubert's most important champions and performing partners. Vogl was from Steyr, and the trip was a homecoming for him and an idyll for Schubert; the young composer's surviving letters from the trip glow with happiness, delight in the beautiful scenery, the warm weather, the music-loving company, the good food and wine. It was, by all accounts, one of the happiest periods of Schubert's life, before the shadow of the 1822 illness fell.

Among the Steyr music-lovers was Sylvester Paumgartner, a well-off amateur cellist and music patron who hosted chamber-music evenings and commissioned the quintet, requesting the variations on "Die Forelle." The work was thus written for a specific group of amateur friends to play together at home, in the happiest of circumstances, and that holiday joy is audible in every bar. This is the Schubertiade ideal in its purest form: music made among friends, for the pleasure of playing it together, in a beautiful place, in a happy summer. The _Trout_ Quintet is the masterpiece that this ideal produced, and knowing its origins, an idyllic holiday, a commission from an amateur friend, a beloved song at its heart, deepens the listening: you are hearing the sound of one of the happiest summers in a short and often difficult life.

#### The Schubertiade, fully described

The _Trout_ Quintet is the right place to give the Schubertiade its full description, because it is the chamber-music masterpiece most fully born of that world. The Schubertiade was the name given to the musical evenings held by Schubert's circle of friends, gatherings in private homes at which Schubert's music, songs, piano music, chamber works, dances, was performed, often by the composer himself and his friends, for an audience of intimates. The circle included poets (Johann Mayrhofer, Franz von Schober), painters (Moritz von Schwind, Leopold Kupelwieser, who left famous drawings of the gatherings), singers (Vogl above all), and devoted music-loving friends (Josef von Spaun, the Sonnleithner family, and many others). The evenings combined music, poetry-reading, conversation, dancing, and conviviality; they were the social and artistic heart of Schubert's life.

The Schubertiade matters because it was, in effect, Schubert's substitute for the public musical career he never had. He held no salaried post, had no major patron, and was largely ignored by the public concert world; his music's true home was the private gathering of friends. This shaped what he wrote and how: a great deal of his output (the songs, the dances, the four-hand music, much of the chamber music) was conceived for performance among friends in a room, not for a paying public in a hall. This gives Schubert's music its particular intimate, companionable, unpretentious quality, so different from the public, heroic stance of Beethoven, and it is the social key to understanding him. The _Trout_ Quintet is the Schubertiade ideal raised to a masterpiece: music written for amateur friends to play together, full of the warmth and pleasure of that shared music-making, and great enough to have outlived the gathering it was made for by two centuries. When you listen to it, you are hearing what the Schubertiade was: friends, making music, for joy.

#### The song "Die Forelle" and its watery world

A word for the song itself, because it is the seed of the whole quintet. "Die Forelle" ("The Trout"), composed around 1817, sets a poem by Christian Friedrich Daniel Schubart (note: a different Schubart, no relation to the composer) about a trout darting happily in a clear brook, which a fisherman, frustrated at first by the clear water that lets the fish see and avoid the hook, finally catches by muddying the water. Schubert's setting is one of his most charming: a famous, instantly memorable tune, and a rippling, darting piano accompaniment that depicts the fish and the flowing water with delicious vividness. (The poem's last verse, often omitted in performance, draws a moralizing parallel, warning young women against deceptive men who "muddy the water," but the song's overwhelming character is bright, watery playfulness, not moral warning.)

The song's sunny, watery world is what Schubert imported into the quintet. When you hear the quintet's fourth movement, you are hearing the trout from the song, swimming again, varied and decorated and passed among five instruments, and at the close, when the song's original rippling water-accompaniment returns, the connection is made explicit: the brook is flowing again, the fish darting, the whole bright world of the song recreated in chamber music. Hearing the song first (recommended) makes the quintet movement land with full force; you recognize the tune, and you recognize, at the close, the return of the water, and the delight of the recognition is part of the pleasure Schubert designed.

#### Recording history briefly

For a first encounter, a warm, classic reading (Sir Clifford Curzon with members of the Vienna Octet) and a clear modern one (Alfred Brendel) make a good pair, and hearing the song "Die Forelle" alongside the quintet is essential to appreciating the fourth movement. Because the quintet is, in spirit, music for friends to play together, recordings by congenial ensembles often capture its companionable warmth best. (The fuller chamber roster is in Chapter 20.)

#### Cross-piece links you will hear later

The _Trout_ Quintet connects across the volume.

To "Death and the Maiden" and the _Rosamunde_ Quartet (Chapter 13), the next chamber works, which also build movements on Schubert's own songs (Threading B), but in a darker key: where the _Trout_ Quintet imports the sunny "Die Forelle," "Death and the Maiden" imports the death-confronting song of that name. The skill you build here, hearing a song's world transplanted into chamber music, applies directly, but the worlds transplanted there are tragic, not sunny.

To the String Quintet in C major (Chapter 14), the late chamber masterpiece, which shares the warmth and the rich scoring instinct (it too adds an extra low instrument, a second cello) but reaches a depth and sublimity far beyond the _Trout_ Quintet's holiday happiness. The two quintets, the sunny early one and the profound late one, frame Schubert's chamber-music achievement.

To the Impromptus and four-hand music (Chapter 11), through the shared Schubertiade context: the _Trout_ Quintet is the chamber-music expression of the same music-among-friends world that produced the social piano music.

#### The 1819 context (Thread A note)

The _Trout_ Quintet comes from 1819, in the heart of Schubert's happy first maturity, before the 1822 illness changed everything. He was twenty-two, recently freed from schoolteaching, living the precarious but joyful freelance life among his friends, and on holiday in beautiful Steyr in one of the happiest summers of his life. The quintet is a product and an expression of that happiness, and it stands, in the book's chronology, as the great sunlit work before the shadow fell. Heard against the late chamber music of 1824-1828 (the next two chapters), it marks the "before": before the illness, before _Winterreise_ , before the late sonatas, when Schubert could write pure, untroubled, holiday joy. The next chapter crosses into the late period and the darker chamber music: the string quartets of 1824-1826, above all "Death and the Maiden," where Schubert builds a quartet movement on a song not about a happy trout but about death itself, and where the song-instrumental interpenetration that here brought summer into the music brings, instead, a confrontation with mortality.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"The _Trout_ Quintet imports the whole sunny, watery world of the song 'Die Forelle' into the chamber music, and the chapter calls this 'the most enjoyable demonstration of Threading B', the borrowed song carrying not just a tune but a complete world of feeling, here 'pure sunlight.' Listen to the song first, then the quintet's fourth movement, especially the return of the song's water-accompaniment at the close. Can you hear the trout still swimming in the chamber music? What does it add to the quintet movement to know, and recognize, the song behind it?"_

* _"The chapter argues that the sunny _Trout_ Quintet is not a 'lesser Schubert' than the dark _Winterreise_ , that 'sustained happiness without sentimentality or emptiness is as rare and difficult as writing tragedy,' and that Schubert's greatness lies in containing both the summer brook and the winter snow. Do you agree that happy music can be as profound as sad music? Why do we often take darkness more seriously than joy in art? Hold the _Trout_ Quintet against _Winterreise_ in your mind, can you accept both as the work of one complete artist?"_

* _"The _Trout_ Quintet was written for amateur friends to play together at home, on a happy holiday, commissioned by an amateur cellist who wanted variations on a song he loved, the Schubertiade ideal of 'music made among friends, for the pleasure of playing it together.' The chapter argues this intimate, social origin is the key to understanding Schubert, whose music's true home was the private gathering, not the concert hall. How does it change the music for you to know it was made for friends to enjoy playing, rather than for a public to admire? Is there something we have lost in moving music from the living room to the concert hall?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find a warm classic reading (Curzon) and ideally a clear modern one (Brendel), plus the song "Die Forelle." Attend to these things.

  1. **The song in the fourth movement.** Listen for the tune of "Die Forelle" as the theme, then for how it is varied and passed among the instruments, and above all for the return of the song's original rippling water-accompaniment near the end. This is Threading B at its happiest and most explicit, the trout swimming again in chamber music.

  2. **The glowing sound and the singing cello.** Listen for the rich, full sonority the unusual scoring produces, the deep double bass at the bottom, and especially the cello singing warmly in its tenor register above it, freed from the bass line by the double bass beneath. This glowing texture is unique to the _Trout_ Quintet.

  3. **The sustained sunny character.** Across all five movements, attend to the warm, generous, untroubled happiness, holiday music, music-among-friends. There is no abyss here, no shadow beneath the beauty. Receive the sunlit pole of Schubert's range, and hold it against the depths of _Winterreise_ and the late sonatas.

  4. **The Schubert fingerprints in a major key.** Even in this sunny work, listen for the glowing shifts to distant keys (the harmonic move by thirds), used here for warmth and color rather than drama. The same fingerprints that create profundity in the late sonatas create radiance here.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** (the source song): "Die Forelle," D 550, in a Fischer-Dieskau / Moore or Ameling reading, before the quintet. Hearing the song's bright tune and darting water-music first makes the fourth movement's variations, and especially the return of the water at the close, land with full delight.

**Listen** (the two quintets): the _Trout_ Quintet and the late String Quintet in C major (Chapter 14) as the sunny and profound poles of Schubert's chamber music. Both enrich the texture with an extra low instrument; both glow; but the late Quintet reaches a sublimity far beyond the holiday happiness here. The pair frames the chamber achievement.

**Read** : the chapters on the Steyr summer and the chamber music in any of the major biographies (Newbould, Gibbs, Byrne Bodley); Schubert's own letters from the 1819 trip, collected in Deutsch's documentary biography, are among the happiest documents of his life and worth reading for the holiday spirit behind the quintet. For the Schubertiade context, the same sources, with Kupelwieser's and Schwind's drawings of the gatherings, bring the social world vividly to life.

**Read** (the analytical voice): Charles Rosen and other writers on Schubert's chamber music discuss the _Trout_ Quintet's scoring and its variation movement; the song-into-chamber-music technique (Threading B) is treated in the literature on Schubert's instrumental borrowings from his own songs.

## Chapter 13: The String Quartets — Death and the Maiden D 810 (1824) + Rosamunde D 804 (1824) + G major D 887 (1826)

The _Trout_ Quintet was the sunlit chamber Schubert of 1819. This chapter crosses into the late chamber music, the great string quartets of 1824-1826, written after the 1822 illness, and the temperature drops sharply. The String Quartet No. 14 in D minor, known as "Death and the Maiden," is one of the most powerful and harrowing works in the entire chamber repertoire, music of confrontation with mortality, and it is, like the _Trout_ Quintet, built on one of Schubert's own songs, but a song about death rather than a happy trout. This is Threading B in its dark key, and the contrast with the previous chapter is the whole point.

The chapter anchors on "Death and the Maiden" complete, with attention to its song-based second movement, and adds the _Rosamunde_ Quartet (which also borrows from Schubert's own music) and a glance at the harmonically extreme G major Quartet. It teaches the same song-into-chamber-music listening as Chapter 12, now applied to tragic material, and it shows Schubert mastering the string quartet, four equal voices, as a vehicle for his deepest and most dramatic instrumental thought. After the holiday of the _Trout_ Quintet, this is the chamber music of the wanderer's winter.

### 13.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote

Vienna, 1824. Schubert, twenty-seven, is in the depths of the years following his 1822-1823 illness, a period of physical suffering, financial difficulty, and dark moods. In a famous and much-quoted letter to a friend that year, he wrote words that are among the bleakest he set down: he described himself as the most unhappy and wretched man in the world, whose health would never be right again, who felt that his brightest hopes had come to nothing and that the happiness of love and friendship offered him nothing but pain. (The letter should be read with some care, written in a low moment, it may not represent his settled state, and Schubert's letters could be theatrical, but its darkness is real and documented.) It is in this period, and this state of mind, that Schubert turned to the string quartet, the most serious and demanding of chamber forms, and wrote the works that crown his quartet output: the _Rosamunde_ Quartet (D 804) and "Death and the Maiden" (D 810), both in 1824, and the great G major Quartet (D 887) in 1826.

"Death and the Maiden" takes its name from its second movement, which is a set of variations on Schubert's own song "Der Tod und das Mädchen" ("Death and the Maiden"), composed in 1817. That song sets a poem in which a frightened young woman begs Death to pass her by, and Death answers, not with menace but with a strange, dreadful gentleness, offering rest: "Give me your hand, you lovely, tender creature; I am a friend, and come not to punish. Be of good courage; I am not wild, you shall sleep softly in my arms." Death as a gentle comforter, sleep as the consolation, is the song's terrible idea, and when Schubert built a quartet movement on it, he imported that whole confrontation with death, and that strange gentleness, into the chamber music. The quartet as a whole is shadowed by death from its first violent bars to its relentless final dance, and the song-movement is its dark heart.

The contrast with the _Trout_ Quintet could not be sharper, and it is deliberate to place them in consecutive chapters. Both quintet and quartet are late-ish chamber works built on Schubert's own songs (Threading B). But the _Trout_ Quintet imports a happy song about a fish into a sunlit holiday work, while "Death and the Maiden" imports a song about death into a harrowing tragedy. Same technique, opposite worlds. The pairing shows the full range of what the song-instrumental interpenetration could carry: summer or death, depending on the song.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen

  1. **The violence of the opening.** "Death and the Maiden" begins not with a tune but with a violent gesture: a fierce, hammered chord and a stark rhythmic figure that bursts out and then subsides into something trembling and uneasy. This is not the genial Schubert of the _Trout_ Quintet; it is dramatic, dark, and urgent from the first second. Listen for the sheer force and tension of the opening, and notice that the whole quartet is shadowed by this violence.

  2. **The song in the slow movement.** The second movement opens with a solemn, hymn-like theme, this is the music of the song "Der Tod und das Mädchen," specifically the part where Death speaks his gentle, dreadful consolation. The movement is a set of variations on it. Listen for the solemn, chorale-like theme and for the variations that follow, by turns grieving, agitated, and finally serene. This is the quartet's dark heart, and Threading B at its most profound: a song about death becoming a meditation on death in four instruments.

  3. **The relentless final dance.** The quartet ends with a fast, driving tarantella (the frenzied dance also found in the late C minor sonata), in a dark minor, that races forward with obsessive, almost desperate energy to a headlong close. Listen for the relentless drive and the sense of a dance that cannot stop, a dance with death, many listeners hear it. After the grief of the slow movement, the finale is a kind of furious, doomed motion.

#### One-sentence background

This chapter anchors on the String Quartet No. 14 in D minor, "Death and the Maiden," D 810 (1824, ~38 minutes), one of the supreme works of the chamber repertoire, whose second movement is a set of variations on Schubert's own song "Der Tod und das Mädchen," D 531 (1817); plus the String Quartet No. 13 in A minor, "Rosamunde," D 804 (1824), whose Andante borrows a theme from Schubert's _Rosamunde_ incidental music, D 797; and a glance at the harmonically extreme String Quartet No. 15 in G major, D 887 (1826); the **Primary Recording** for this chapter is the Takács Quartet (Decca) for "Death and the Maiden," chosen for its intensity and clarity; the Alban Berg Quartet (EMI) and the Hagen Quartet (Deutsche Grammophon) are essential alternatives.

#### Search Hints

EN: "Schubert Death and the Maiden Quartet D 810 Takács" or "Schubert String Quartet D minor Death Maiden Alban Berg Quartet" ZH: "舒伯特 死与少女 四重奏 D810 塔卡奇四重奏" 或 "舒伯特 D小调弦乐四重奏 死神与少女 阿尔班·贝尔格四重奏" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

**Alternative recordings** within the recording-hallucination safeguard (≥10 years old OR ≥3 reviews):

* The Alban Berg Quartet (EMI), a powerful, polished, intense reading, long a reference.
* The Hagen Quartet (Deutsche Grammophon), refined and probing.
* The Amadeus Quartet (Deutsche Grammophon), a classic older reading, warm and weighty.
* The Quartetto Italiano, the Emerson Quartet, and many others have distinguished versions; look for those with multiple reviews.
* For the song "Der Tod und das Mädchen," D 531: a contralto or mezzo (the song suits a low female or a male voice) with piano, to hear the source; and for "Die Forelle"-style comparison, recall the _Trout_ Quintet's sunny song-movement from Chapter 12.

### 13.2 The Listening Window

This Window walks through "Death and the Maiden" complete (the anchor) with focus on the song-based second movement, and notes the _Rosamunde_ Andante. Timings refer to a typical reading; navigate by movement.

#### String Quartet in D minor, "Death and the Maiden," D 810

**First movement (Allegro), 0:00.** The quartet erupts with a violent gesture: a fierce, hammered chord and a stark triplet rhythm, fortissimo, that bursts out and subsides into trembling unease. This rhythmic figure (a hammering triplet) drives much of the movement. Listen for the alternation between violent outbursts and trembling, lyrical passages, the movement is a drama of force and fear, dark and urgent throughout. There are moments of aching lyricism (Schubert's song-instinct surfacing), but they are always shadowed by the surrounding violence. Notice the relentless rhythmic energy and the harmonic restlessness; this is Schubert's most dramatic chamber-music first movement.

**Second movement (Andante con moto), about 11:00.** The dark heart of the quartet. It opens with a solemn, slow, hymn-like theme in the strings, this is the music of the song "Der Tod und das Mädchen," the part where Death speaks his gentle consolation. The theme is grave, chorale-like, strangely peaceful, the peace of Death's terrible offer of rest. Then come the variations:

* A variation with the first violin singing an anguished, elaborate line over the theme.
* A more agitated, stormy variation, grief breaking out.
* A powerful, intense variation with driving rhythms.
* A turn toward consolation, the theme returning in a serene major, Death's gentleness.
* A final, hushed return to the grave peace of the opening. Listen for the solemn theme and for the emotional journey of the variations, from grief and agitation to a strange, dreadful serenity. The movement is a meditation on death built on the song's confrontation, and its final peace is the peace of Death's consolation in the song: not happy, but resigned, the maiden accepting Death's offer of rest. This is Threading B at its most profound, far from the _Trout_ Quintet's sunlight: a song about death becoming a quartet movement that meditates on death and ends in its terrible peace.

**Third movement (Scherzo, Allegro molto), about 24:00.** A fierce, driving scherzo in minor, hammering and urgent, with a gentler, songful central trio offering brief respite. Listen for the violence of the scherzo (it shares the first movement's dark energy) and the contrast of the lyrical trio, a moment of light before the finale's darkness.

**Fourth movement (Presto), about 30:00.** The relentless final tarantella. A fast, driving dance in D minor, racing forward with obsessive, desperate energy. Listen for the unflagging momentum, the sense of a dance that cannot stop, the dance with death many hear in it, and for the headlong drive to the fierce close. After the slow movement's meditation on death, the finale is furious, doomed motion, the quartet ending not in consolation but in a desperate, racing dance toward the end. The whole work, from the violent opening to this final dance, is shadowed by mortality, with the song-movement at its center.

#### String Quartet in A minor, "Rosamunde," D 804 — the Andante (brief)

The _Rosamunde_ Quartet's slow movement (Andante) borrows its main theme from Schubert's incidental music to the play _Rosamunde_ (D 797), a gentle, songful, slightly melancholy melody that Schubert clearly loved (he used it more than once). Listen for the lyrical, wistful theme and the quartet's warm, autumnal mood, gentler than "Death and the Maiden" but touched with the same late-period melancholy. The _Rosamunde_ Quartet is another instance of Threading B (Schubert building on his own pre-existing music), and its Andante is one of his loveliest slow movements.

### 13.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### Song into chamber music, in the dark key

"Death and the Maiden" is the dark counterpart to the _Trout_ Quintet, and the comparison is the chapter's central lesson. Both are late-ish chamber works built on Schubert's own songs (Threading B); both take a song's whole world and import it into the chamber music. But where the _Trout_ Quintet imported the sunny, watery playfulness of "Die Forelle," "Death and the Maiden" imports the death-confrontation of "Der Tod und das Mädchen", a song in which a terrified young woman begs Death to pass her by, and Death answers with a strange, dreadful gentleness, offering not punishment but rest, sleep, peace in his arms.

This dark song shadows the whole quartet, not just the movement built on it. The first movement's violence, the scherzo's fierce drive, the finale's desperate dance, all belong to the world the song opens: a world haunted by death, oscillating between terror (the maiden's fear) and a terrible consolation (Death's gentle offer of rest). The slow movement, built directly on the song, is the explicit meditation, and its journey from grief through agitation to a strange serene peace re-enacts the song's movement from the maiden's terror to Death's consoling answer. The quartet ends, in the finale, not with resolution but with a doomed, racing dance, the dance with death.

The listening skill is the one from Chapter 12, applied to tragic material: hear the song's world coloring the instrumental work. Knowing the song "Der Tod und das Mädchen", the maiden's plea, Death's gentle answer, deepens the quartet immeasurably, just as knowing "Die Forelle" deepened the _Trout_ Quintet. The borrowed song is, once again, not just a theme but a whole world of feeling, here a confrontation with mortality and a strange consolation, transplanted into the most serious of chamber forms. And the pairing of the two works, the sunny quintet and the deathly quartet, both built on Schubert songs, demonstrates the full expressive range of Threading B: the same technique can bring summer or death into the chamber music, depending on the song chosen. This is the chapter's gift to the synthesis (Chapter 18), where all the song-instrumental interpenetrations are collected: here you hear the technique's two poles, side by side.

#### The string quartet as a vehicle for tragedy

"Death and the Maiden" shows Schubert mastering the string quartet, four equal string voices (two violins, viola, cello), as a vehicle for his deepest and most dramatic instrumental thought. The string quartet was, by Schubert's time, the most serious and demanding of chamber forms, the form in which Haydn, Mozart, and the living Beethoven had done some of their profoundest work, four voices in intimate, intense dialogue, with nowhere to hide and no piano or orchestra to fill out the texture. Schubert had written quartets since childhood (for the family ensemble, Chapter 3), but the late quartets of 1824-1826 are where he turned the form to his deepest purposes.

What he found in the quartet, for "Death and the Maiden," was a vehicle for sustained tragic intensity. The four equal voices let him build drama through their interaction, the violent unison gestures, the trembling dialogues, the way grief passes from instrument to instrument in the slow movement's variations. The medium's intimacy, four players, no doubling, every line exposed, suits the work's concentrated darkness; there is no orchestral grandeur to soften it, only four voices confronting death together. Schubert proved, in this quartet, that he could write chamber music of tragic power equal to anything in the literature, that the song-composer and the sunny melodist was also a master of dark, dramatic, large-scale instrumental form. The string quartet, the most "abstract" and demanding chamber medium, became in his hands a vehicle for the same depth of feeling as the songs, and "Death and the Maiden" is the proof. When you reach the String Quintet (Chapter 14), you will hear him take the related medium of the string ensemble to an even greater sublimity; here, in the quartet, he masters tragic intensity in the most exposed of forms.

#### The major-minor switch and harmonic extremity in the quartets

The late quartets are full of the Schubert fingerprints, especially the major-minor switch and harmonic extremity, used here for tragic and unsettling effect. In "Death and the Maiden," listen for the way the music shifts between major and minor, the slow movement's turn from grieving minor to Death's serene major (the consolation), the lyrical major passages in the dark first movement that are always pulled back to minor. The major-minor switch (Threading B) is, here, the sound of consolation offered and the shadow returning, of light glimpsed within darkness, the same device that drove the deceptive consolations of _Winterreise_ (Chapter 7), now in instrumental form.

The G major Quartet (D 887) of 1826, the last and most harmonically extreme of the quartets, takes this even further: it opens with a famous gesture that switches violently between G major and G minor, the major-minor switch made into the very subject of the music, the two modes alternating starkly, neither winning. This harmonic instability, the refusal to settle between major and minor, between light and dark, is one of the most modern things in Schubert, and the G major Quartet is its most extreme instrumental expression. (The quartet is long and demanding, beyond this chapter's main anchor, but the listener who has absorbed "Death and the Maiden" should seek it out; its harmonic extremity points toward the late sonatas and the String Quintet.)

These harmonic fingerprints, heard throughout the songs and the piano music, operate in the quartets with a particular dramatic and tragic force, because the string quartet's exposed, intense medium foregrounds them. The shift from major to minor, in four naked string voices, is starker and more unsettling than in the fuller textures of the piano or orchestra. Chapter 18 collects the major-minor switch and harmonic extremity across the catalogue; the late quartets are among their most dramatic realizations, and "Death and the Maiden" and the G major Quartet are the places to hear them at their most tragic and most modern.

### 13.4 The Stories

#### The 1824 letter and the dark years

The late quartets come from the darkest period of Schubert's life, and the famous 1824 letter quoted at the chapter's opening is the documentary key. Following the 1822-1823 illness, Schubert entered years of physical suffering, recurring symptoms, treatments that were themselves debilitating, financial struggle, and dark moods. The 1824 letter, in which he called himself the most wretched man in the world and despaired of health, hope, and happiness, is the bleakest surviving expression of his state, and it dates from the very year of "Death and the Maiden" and the _Rosamunde_ Quartet.

It is tempting to read the quartets directly as expressions of this misery, and the book's usual caution applies: the works are not diary entries, and Schubert chose to build "Death and the Maiden" on a pre-existing 1817 song, not to write a fresh confession. But the conjunction is real and harder to dismiss than usual, because the letter and the quartets coincide so exactly in time and tone, and because the quartet's subject, death and its strange consolation, resonates so directly with a sick man's confrontation with mortality. The honest position, as with _Die schöne Müllerin_ and the late sonatas, is that the illness and the dark mood form the biographical backdrop and surely deepened Schubert's engagement with the death-haunted material, while the quartet remains a work of art shaped by craft and by the borrowed song, not a literal confession. What is certain is that "Death and the Maiden" is Schubert's most sustained confrontation with mortality in instrumental music, and that it comes from the period when mortality was most pressingly real to him. The reader can feel the connection without reducing the music to autobiography.

#### "Der Tod und das Mädchen": the gentle, dreadful Death

The song at the quartet's heart deserves full attention, because its idea is the quartet's idea. "Der Tod und das Mädchen" ("Death and the Maiden"), composed in 1817, sets a short poem (by Matthias Claudius) in two parts. First the Maiden speaks, in agitated, frightened music: she begs Death to pass her by, she is still young, she is not ready, go away. Then Death answers, and here is the song's terrible stroke: Death's music is not menacing but slow, gentle, hymn-like, almost tender. Death says, in effect: give me your hand, gentle creature; I am a friend, not a punisher; be of good courage; you shall sleep softly in my arms. Death as a gentle comforter, death as sleep, death as rest and consolation rather than terror, is the song's profound and disturbing idea, and it is the music of Death's gentle answer that becomes the theme of the quartet's slow movement.

This is why the quartet's slow movement is so strange and so moving: its grave, hymn-like, almost peaceful theme is the voice of Death offering rest, and the variations explore that terrible consolation, the peace that death offers to the suffering. The quartet is not simply "scary music about death"; it is a meditation on death's dreadful gentleness, on the consolation that the end offers to a sufferer, exactly the consolation a sick, despairing Schubert might have found himself contemplating. The serene final peace of the slow movement is the maiden's acceptance of Death's offer, the suffering soul accepting rest. Knowing the song, both the maiden's terror and Death's gentle answer, transforms the quartet from a generically dark work into a specific and profound meditation on mortality and consolation. Hearing the song first (recommended) is the key to the quartet's depth.

#### The Rosamunde music and Schubert's self-borrowing

The _Rosamunde_ Quartet (D 804) borrows its Andante theme from Schubert's incidental music to the play _Rosamunde_ (D 797, 1823), and this is worth a note because it illustrates a habit: Schubert often reused his own material, and the _Rosamunde_ theme was one he particularly loved, using it in the quartet, in the incidental music, and in a set of piano impromptus (D 935 No. 3). This self-borrowing is part of the song-instrumental interpenetration thread (Threading B) in a broader sense: Schubert's musical ideas flowed freely between genres, a theme from incidental music becoming a quartet movement and a piano variation-set. The _Rosamunde_ play itself was a flop (it closed after two performances), but Schubert's incidental music outlived it, partly through this recycling. The _Rosamunde_ Quartet's Andante, gentle, wistful, autumnal, shows the lyrical, song-like Schubert within the late quartets, a moment of warmth alongside the darkness of "Death and the Maiden," and a reminder that the late chamber music, though shadowed, is not uniformly tragic.

#### Recording history briefly

For a first encounter, a powerfully intense reading, the Takács Quartet (the recommended primary) or the Alban Berg Quartet, conveys the work's tragic force, and hearing the song "Der Tod und das Mädchen" alongside it is essential to understanding the slow movement. Because the quartet is so dramatic and so exposed, it rewards an ensemble's unanimity and intensity; the great readings make the four voices a single dramatic instrument. (The fuller quartet roster, and the _Rosamunde_ and G major quartets, are in Chapter 20.)

#### Cross-piece links you will hear later

The late quartets connect across the volume.

To the _Trout_ Quintet (Chapter 12), the sunny counterpart: both build on Schubert's own songs (Threading B), but the quintet imports summer and the quartet imports death. The pairing demonstrates the full range of the song-instrumental technique, its two poles in consecutive chapters.

To the String Quintet in C major (Chapter 14), the late chamber masterpiece that follows, which shares the late quartets' depth and harmonic extremity but reaches an even greater sublimity. The String Quintet's turbulent central eruptions are kin to the violence of "Death and the Maiden"; its serene outer music is kin to the consolation of the slow movement.

To _Winterreise_ (Chapter 7) and the late sonatas (Chapter 10), which share the late quartets' major-minor switching, harmonic extremity, and confrontation with darkness; the G major Quartet's violent major-minor alternation is the chamber sibling of the deceptive consolations of _Winterreise_ and the shadowed beauty of D 960.

#### The 1824-1826 context (Thread A note)

The late quartets come from the dark middle of Schubert's late period: "Death and the Maiden" and the _Rosamunde_ Quartet in 1824, the year of the despairing letter, in the suffering aftermath of the 1822-1823 illness; the G major Quartet in 1826, as Schubert moved toward the final astonishing years. They are the chamber music of the wanderer's winter, written when mortality was most pressingly real to Schubert, and "Death and the Maiden" in particular is his most sustained instrumental confrontation with death. Between these quartets and the late sonatas and String Quintet of 1828 lies the final flowering; the quartets are the great dark works of the period before that final surge. The next chapter reaches the summit of Schubert's chamber music and one of the summits of his whole output: the String Quintet in C major of 1828, written in the last months of his life, a work that takes the late style's depth, harmonic extremity, and the holding-together of beauty and darkness to a sublimity beyond even "Death and the Maiden", the chamber-music peak of the most extraordinary final year in musical history.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"'Death and the Maiden' and the _Trout_ Quintet are placed in consecutive chapters deliberately: both are chamber works built on Schubert's own songs (Threading B), but one imports a happy song about a fish into sunlit holiday music and the other imports a song about death into a harrowing tragedy. The chapter says this shows that 'the same technique can bring summer or death into the chamber music, depending on the song chosen.' Listen to both works' song-movements back to back. What does the pairing teach you about how much a borrowed melody carries with it, and about the range of one composer's art?"_

* _"The song at the quartet's heart presents Death not as a menacing terror but as a gentle comforter offering rest, 'I am a friend ... you shall sleep softly in my arms', and the quartet's slow movement is a meditation on this 'terrible consolation,' ending in a strange serene peace. Why is Death-as-gentle-comforter more disturbing, and more moving, than Death-as-terror? How might a sick, despairing Schubert have heard this idea? Does the consolation make the quartet more bearable or more painful for you?"_

* _"The chapter applies its usual caution to the link between the 1824 letter in which Schubert called himself 'the most wretched man in the world' and the death-haunted quartet of the same year, noting the works 'are not diary entries' while admitting 'the conjunction is real and harder to dismiss than usual.' How much should we let a composer's documented misery shape how we hear his music? When the biography and the music align so exactly in time and tone, is it dishonest to keep them apart, or essential? Where do you draw the line?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find an intense reading (Takács or Alban Berg) and the song "Der Tod und das Mädchen." Attend to these things.

  1. **The song in the slow movement.** Listen for the solemn, hymn-like theme of the second movement, the music of Death's gentle consolation from the song, and follow the variations from grief and agitation to the strange serene peace at the close. This is Threading B at its most profound, the dark counterpart to the _Trout_ Quintet's sunny song-movement.

  2. **The violence framing the work.** Hear how the quartet is shadowed by death from the violent opening gesture through the fierce scherzo to the relentless final tarantella, the "dance with death." The song-movement's meditation sits at the center of a work haunted throughout by mortality.

  3. **The major-minor switch as light within darkness.** Listen for the shifts between major and minor, especially the slow movement's turn from grieving minor to Death's serene major (the consolation). This is the same device as _Winterreise_ 's deceptive consolations, in instrumental form, light glimpsed within darkness.

  4. **The exposed intensity of the quartet medium.** Attend to how the four naked string voices, with no piano or orchestra to fill out the texture, make the drama starker and the harmonic shifts more unsettling. The string quartet's intimacy and exposure suit the work's concentrated darkness; this is Schubert mastering the most demanding chamber form for tragedy.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** (the source song): "Der Tod und das Mädchen," D 531, before the quartet, in a low-voiced reading (contralto, mezzo, or baritone). Hearing the maiden's terror and Death's gentle, hymn-like answer first transforms the quartet's slow movement from generically dark music into a specific meditation on death's consolation.

**Listen** (the two song-based chamber works): the _Trout_ Quintet's fourth movement (Chapter 12) and "Death and the Maiden"'s second movement, back to back. The sunny song-variation and the deathly song-variation, both built on Schubert's own songs, show the two poles of Threading B in the chamber music.

**Listen** (the harmonic extreme): the String Quartet No. 15 in G major, D 887 (1826), especially its opening, with the violent alternation of G major and G minor. This is the most harmonically extreme of the quartets and points toward the late sonatas and the String Quintet; it rewards the listener who has absorbed "Death and the Maiden."

**Read** : the chapters on the late quartets in the major biographies (Newbould, Gibbs, Byrne Bodley) and the analytical literature (Charles Rosen and others) on Schubert's chamber music. The 1824 letter and the documentary context of the dark years are in Deutsch's documentary biography. For the song-into-quartet technique, the literature on Schubert's self-borrowings treats both "Death and the Maiden" and the _Rosamunde_ music.
