## Chapter 14: String Quintet in C major D 956 (1828)

If a single work had to stand for Schubert's chamber music, and for many listeners for Schubert himself, it would be the String Quintet in C major, written in the summer or early autumn of 1828, in the last months of his life. It is, by a wide consensus that includes performers, scholars, and ordinary listeners, one of the supreme achievements in all of music, often named alongside Beethoven's late quartets as a peak of the entire chamber literature, and its slow movement is, for many, the most beautiful single stretch of music Schubert ever wrote, and perhaps that anyone ever wrote. This chapter is the summit of Part V and one of the summits of the book.

The Quintet brings together everything the late style has taught us: the spaciousness, the harmonic depth, the holding-together of beauty and darkness, the long-breathed melody, all at their fullest. It anchors on the sublime second-movement Adagio, with its serene outer sections framing a turbulent central eruption, and on the great first movement. And it has a particular sound, produced by an unusual scoring decision (a second cello rather than a second viola) that gives it a richness and depth unique in the literature. The String Quintet is where this book's listening skills converge on a single overwhelming experience; approach it with patience and an open ear, and give it the time it asks.

### 14.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote

The String Quintet was completed in the summer or early autumn of 1828, within weeks or a few months of Schubert's death on November 19. Like the late piano sonatas, the _Great_ C major Symphony, and the _Schwanengesang_ songs, it belongs to the astonishing final year, the most concentrated outpouring of masterpieces in musical history. Schubert mentioned the Quintet in a letter to a publisher in the autumn of 1828, offering it among his recent works; there is no evidence it was performed, or even rehearsed, in his lifetime. He wrote one of the greatest works in the chamber repertoire, and he never heard it played.

This is one of the most extraordinary facts in the history of music, and it bears dwelling on. The String Quintet was not performed publicly until 1850, twenty-two years after Schubert's death, and was not published until 1853. For a quarter-century after its composition, one of the supreme masterpieces of Western music sat unheard, unknown to the world, a manuscript among the papers of a composer the wider public still thought of, if at all, as a writer of pleasant songs. Its emergence into the repertoire was gradual, and its recognition as a peak of the literature is, like so much of Schubert, a later achievement. That a work of this magnitude could be written by a dying man, never heard by him, and then wait decades for the world to discover it, is a measure both of Schubert's neglect and of the depths that lay unrecognized in his catalogue.

A famous remark attaches to the Quintet, often quoted: that the violinist Arthur Rubinstein (or, in other tellings, various other figures) wished to have the slow movement played at his funeral, or that the work's Adagio is the music one would want to hear at the gates of heaven. A version of this is sometimes attributed to the poet Heinrich Heine, "if there is a heaven, Schubert's Quintet plays there", but this attribution is widely repeated and its provenance is uncertain; the book flags it accordingly and does not vouch for it. What is not uncertain is the universal testimony of listeners that the Quintet's slow movement reaches a beauty and a depth that few works in any art have equaled. Whoever did or did not say what about it, the music itself is the evidence.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen

  1. **The rich, deep, glowing sound.** The Quintet is scored for two violins, one viola, and _two cellos_ , the second cello added where most string quintets add a second viola. This puts two rich, deep, singing instruments at the bottom, giving the Quintet a uniquely warm, full, glowing sonority, darker and deeper than a normal quartet or quintet. Listen for this sound from the first bars: the depth and richness, the two cellos singing together, the glowing warmth of the texture. It is one of the most beautiful sounds in all of chamber music.

  2. **The sublime slow movement, and its eruption.** The second movement, the Adagio, is the heart of the work and one of the peaks of all music. Its outer sections are music of an almost unearthly stillness and serenity, time seems to stop, the music floating in a profound calm. But its central section erupts into turbulence and pain, a passionate, agitated storm, before the serene music returns, now shadowed by what has intervened. Listen for the suspended stillness of the outer sections and the turbulent eruption at the center. The movement holds the deepest peace and the deepest pain together.

  3. **The breadth and the holding-together of beauty and darkness.** Like the late sonatas, the Quintet unfolds at vast, unhurried length, and its beauty is everywhere shadowed: the radiant first movement has its dark turns, the sublime slow movement its central storm. Listen for the spaciousness and for the way beauty and darkness coexist throughout, the achieved depth of the late Schubert, where serenity is earned in full knowledge of darkness.

#### One-sentence background

The String Quintet in C major, D 956, was composed in the summer or early autumn of 1828, in the final months of Schubert's life, and is widely regarded as one of the supreme masterpieces of chamber music; it is scored unusually for two violins, viola, and two cellos (a second cello rather than the more common second viola), giving it a uniquely rich, deep, glowing sound; this chapter anchors on the sublime second-movement Adagio (~15 minutes, with serene outer sections framing a turbulent central eruption) and the great first movement; the Quintet was never performed in Schubert's lifetime, first played in 1850; the **Primary Recording** for this chapter is the recording featuring the cellist Mstislav Rostropovich with the Melos Quartet (Deutsche Grammophon) or the classic recording with Pablo Casals and the Prades Festival players; for a modern reference, the Takács Quartet with a second cellist, or the recording with the Pavel Haas Quartet and Danjulo Ishizaka.

#### Search Hints

EN: "Schubert String Quintet C major D 956 Rostropovich Melos" or "Schubert Cello Quintet D 956 Casals" or "Schubert String Quintet two cellos" ZH: "舒伯特 C大调弦乐五重奏 D956 罗斯特罗波维奇 梅洛斯四重奏" 或 "舒伯特 弦乐五重奏 双大提琴 卡萨尔斯" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

**Alternative recordings** within the recording-hallucination safeguard (≥10 years old OR ≥3 reviews):

* Pablo Casals with the Prades Festival players, Sony/Columbia, 1952, a historic, deeply felt reading, a touchstone despite its age.
* The Melos Quartet with Mstislav Rostropovich (Deutsche Grammophon), rich and intense.
* The Pavel Haas Quartet with Danjulo Ishizaka (Supraphon), a celebrated modern reading, intense and beautifully recorded.
* The Takács Quartet, the Alban Berg Quartet, the Hagen Quartet, and many others (each with an added cellist) have distinguished versions; look for those with multiple reviews.

### 14.2 The Listening Window

This Window focuses on the Adagio (the anchor) and the first movement. Listen to the complete Quintet eventually (about fifty minutes), but begin with these. Timings refer to a typical reading; navigate by movement.

#### First movement (Allegro ma non troppo)

**0:00. The glowing opening.** The Quintet opens with a sustained C major chord that swells and subsides, then darkens, harmonically, almost at once, a moment of shadow within the radiance, before the music gathers. Listen immediately for the rich, deep sound of the two-cello scoring and for this early darkening of the bright opening, the holding-together of light and shadow that runs through the whole work.

**About 2:00. The great second theme: the two cellos sing together.** The movement's most famous and most beautiful moment arrives when the two cellos sing a long, warm, songful melody together, in duet, one of the most glorious sounds in all chamber music, two rich tenor-baritone voices singing the same lyrical line in harmony. This is the moment the two-cello scoring exists for. Listen for the two cellos singing together; the melody is long-breathed, warm, and deeply Schubertian, and its sound, two cellos in duet, is unique to this work. It is one of the most-loved passages in the literature.

**The development and the journey.** The movement unfolds at vast, spacious length, the radiant and shadowed material developed, carried through Schubert's characteristic journey to distant glowing keys, and brought home. Listen for the spaciousness and for the constant coexistence of beauty and shadow, the bright C major always touched with darkness, the harmonic journeys to far regions. The first movement is one of Schubert's largest and richest, and it sets the work's tone: radiant, spacious, profound, shadowed.

#### Second movement (Adagio)

**0:00. The suspended stillness.** The Adagio opens in a state of profound, almost unearthly calm. The three middle instruments (second violin, viola, first cello) hold a slow, hushed, sustained chordal music, barely moving, while the first violin floats a long, serene line high above and the second cello adds gentle pizzicato (plucked) notes far below. The effect is of time stopping, of music suspended in a vast stillness, the whole texture floating, the harmony slow and glowing. Listen for this suspended stillness; it is one of the most extraordinary things in music, a calm so deep it seems outside of time. The music is in no hurry at all, and its serenity is the point. This outer section is, for many listeners, the most beautiful music Schubert wrote.

**About 5:00. The eruption.** Then, shockingly, the stillness breaks. The middle section erupts into turbulence, a passionate, agitated, anguished storm in a dark minor, the calm shattered by pain. The music becomes urgent, dramatic, almost violent, grief or terror breaking into the peace. Listen for the eruption, the sudden plunge from suspended serenity into storm. It is the same gesture as the central eruption of the D 959 Andantino (Chapter 10), the late Schubert's holding-together of deepest peace and deepest pain, and here it is at its most overwhelming. The serenity was not naive; it knew about this storm.

**About 9:00. The return, transfigured.** The storm subsides, and the suspended stillness returns, the serene opening music, but now it is shadowed by what has intervened. The first cello and other instruments add gentle, sighing figures, fragments of the storm's emotion, beneath the restored calm; the peace is the same and yet changed, deepened, made aware of the pain it contains. Listen for the return and for how the eruption has transformed the serenity, the calm now holding the memory of the storm. The movement ends in a peace that is profound precisely because it has passed through the storm and returned, a serenity earned in full knowledge of darkness. This is the late Schubert's deepest achievement, and the Adagio is its supreme realization.

#### Third and fourth movements (brief)

**Third movement (Scherzo, Presto).** A vigorous, robust scherzo, full of rustic energy and the rich two-cello sound, with a strange, dark, slow central trio (Andante sostenuto) that drops the energy into a hushed, mysterious stillness, an unexpected shadow at the heart of the dance. Listen for the contrast between the powerful scherzo and the eerie, slow trio.

**Fourth movement (Allegretto).** A finale that blends Hungarian/gypsy-flavored dance rhythms with Schubert's lyricism and harmonic adventure, energetic and warm but shadowed, like the rest of the work, by minor-key turns and harmonic restlessness, and ending with a final, ambiguous gesture. Listen for the dance energy and the persistent shadows.

### 14.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### The two-cello sound: depth and the singing duet

The String Quintet's most distinctive feature is its scoring: two violins, one viola, and two cellos. The standard string quintet (Mozart's, for example) adds a second viola to the string quartet; Schubert instead added a second cello, and the choice transforms the work's sound. (The precedent is Boccherini, who wrote many quintets with two cellos; Schubert may have known them, but his use of the scoring is entirely his own.)

The two-cello scoring does two things. First, it deepens and enriches the whole texture: with two cellos at the bottom, the Quintet has a darker, fuller, more resonant foundation than a normal quintet or quartet, a glowing depth that is audible from the first chord and that gives the work its uniquely warm, rich sonority. Second, and most gloriously, it lets the two cellos sing together in duet, as they do in the first movement's great second theme, two rich, warm, tenor-baritone voices in harmony, one of the most beautiful sounds in all of chamber music. A second viola could not do this; it would add a middle voice, not a deep singing one. By choosing a second cello, Schubert gained both the deep foundation and the singing low duet, and the Quintet's sound, dark, rich, glowing, with the two cellos singing together, is the result. It is a sound he achieved nowhere else, and it is inseparable from the work's character: the depth of the scoring matches the depth of the music. When you listen, attend especially to the two-cello duet in the first movement and to the rich low foundation throughout; the scoring is not a technicality but the source of the Quintet's unique voice.

#### The Adagio: suspended time and the eruption

The second-movement Adagio is the heart of the Quintet and, for many, the summit of Schubert's art, and understanding how it works is the chapter's central lesson. Its great achievement is the creation of suspended time. The outer sections do something almost no other music does: they create a sense of time stopping, of the music floating in a vast, motionless calm. This is achieved through the texture, the three middle instruments holding slow, sustained, barely-moving chords, the first violin floating a serene line above, the second cello adding gentle plucked notes below, so that nothing drives forward, nothing develops, the music simply _is_ , suspended, glowing, outside of time. The effect is of profound peace, of stillness so deep it seems eternal. This is the spaciousness of the late style (Chapter 10) taken to its absolute limit: not just unhurried but motionless, time itself suspended.

And then the eruption. The central section shatters the stillness with a passionate, agitated, anguished storm, and this is the crucial stroke, because it reveals that the serenity was not naive. The peace of the outer sections is not the peace of someone who has never known pain; it is a peace that contains, and has passed through, the storm at the center. When the serene music returns after the eruption, it is the same and yet transfigured, now shadowed by what intervened, the calm aware of the pain it holds, sighing figures of grief beneath the restored stillness. The movement's profundity lies exactly here: it holds the deepest peace and the deepest pain together, and the peace is earned, not given. This is the late Schubert's supreme achievement, the holding-together of beauty and darkness that we have traced through the D 960 trill and the D 959 eruption, here realized at its fullest and most overwhelming. The Adagio is not merely beautiful; it is the most complete expression of the late Schubert's hard-won, shadow-knowing serenity, the music of someone who has looked into the depths and found, on the other side, a peace that holds the darkness within it. Listen for the suspended stillness, the shattering eruption, and the transfigured return; this is the emotional and spiritual core of the whole book.

#### The convergence of the late style

The String Quintet is where this book's listening skills converge, and it is worth naming the convergence, because the Quintet rewards hearing everything at once. The spaciousness of the late sonatas is here, taken to the suspended stillness of the Adagio. The harmonic adventure, the move by thirds, the glowing distant keys, is here, in the first movement's journeys and the early darkening of the bright opening. The major-minor switch is here, in the constant shadowing of the radiant music. The long-breathed lyrical melody is here, supremely in the two-cello duet. The holding-together of beauty and darkness, the deepest theme of the late style, is here at its fullest, in the Adagio's peace-and-storm and in the whole work's shadowed radiance. And the rich-scoring instinct that warmed the _Trout_ Quintet is here, deepened, in the two-cello sonority.

The Quintet is thus a summa, a gathering of everything Schubert's instrumental art had become, in a single fifty-minute work of overwhelming depth and beauty. For the listener who has worked through the book, it is the place where all the threads come together and can be heard at once: the song-like melody, the harmonic journeys, the shadowed beauty, the spaciousness, the rich sound. This is why it stands as the summit of the chamber music and one of the summits of the whole output, and why it is the right work to end Part V. It is not a single new thing but the fullest realization of everything, the late Schubert's art complete. Chapter 18 will collect the fingerprints across the catalogue; the String Quintet is where you can hear nearly all of them converging in one supreme work, and hearing that convergence, the whole late style in a single piece, is the deepest reward of the listening the book has been teaching.

### 14.4 The Stories

#### Never heard by its composer

The fact that Schubert never heard the String Quintet performed is worth holding in mind as you listen, not as a sentimental detail but as a sobering reality about how genius and recognition can fail to meet. Schubert wrote the work in 1828, offered it to a publisher in a letter that autumn, and died in November; there is no record of any performance or rehearsal in his lifetime. The Quintet waited until 1850 for its first public performance and 1853 for publication. For twenty-two years, one of the supreme masterpieces of Western music existed only as a manuscript, unheard by the world, while the public continued to think of Schubert, when it thought of him at all, as a composer of charming songs.

This is the extreme case of the neglect that shadowed Schubert's whole posthumous reputation. The String Quintet's silence, written by a dying man, never heard by him, unknown to the world for a quarter-century, is the starkest single instance. It should make the listener marvel at two things: at the depth that lay unrecognized in Schubert's catalogue for so long, and at the strangeness of a creative act so complete, so perfect, that needed no audience to be what it is. Schubert wrote the Quintet for no occasion, no performance, no public, possibly knowing he might not live to hear it; he wrote it because it was in him to write, and it is among the greatest things any human being has made. That it sat silent for twenty-two years does not diminish it; it only reminds us how much can lie unheard, and how slowly the world sometimes comes to its treasures.

#### The uncertain "heaven" attribution

A word on the famous quotation, because it illustrates the book's documentary discipline. It is often said, in program notes and popular writing, that someone, frequently Heinrich Heine, declared that "if there is a heaven, Schubert's Quintet plays there," or words to that effect, and the slow movement is routinely described as the music one would want at the gates of heaven or at one's funeral. These attributions are widely repeated, and the sentiment they express, that the Quintet's Adagio reaches a beauty close to the divine, is shared by countless listeners. But the specific attributions are of uncertain provenance; the Heine version in particular cannot be reliably sourced, and the book flags it as a probably-apocryphal saying rather than a documented quotation.

This matters not because the attribution is important in itself, but because it is a small example of the kind of legend that accretes around great works and great composers, and that this book consistently declines to pass off as fact. The Quintet does not need a celebrity endorsement to establish its greatness; the music is the evidence, and the universal testimony of listeners across two centuries is the witness. When you read, as you often will, that "Heine said" or "Rubinstein wanted" this or that about the Quintet, treat the specific claim with caution while accepting the underlying truth it gestures at: that this music has struck generations of listeners as reaching a beauty and a depth that few works in any art attain. The legend is uncertain; the greatness is not.

#### Recording history briefly

For a first encounter, a deeply felt classic (Pablo Casals's 1952 Prades recording) and a beautifully recorded modern reading (the Pavel Haas Quartet with Danjulo Ishizaka) make a good pair. The choice of second cellist matters, since the two-cello duet in the first movement is central; and the Adagio especially rewards hearing in more than one interpretation, since its handling of suspended time and the eruption varies significantly between ensembles. (The fuller chamber roster is in Chapter 20.)

#### Cross-piece links you will hear later

The String Quintet connects across the volume.

To the late piano sonatas (Chapter 10), also of 1828, with which it shares the whole late style: the spaciousness, the harmonic depth, the holding-together of beauty and darkness. The Quintet's Adagio is the chamber sibling of D 960's first movement (the suspended, shadowed beauty) and its central eruption the sibling of D 959's (the storm within the calm).

To the _Trout_ Quintet (Chapter 12), the sunny early quintet, with which it shares the rich-scoring instinct (both add an extra low instrument) but which it utterly transcends in depth; the two quintets, sunny and sublime, frame Schubert's chamber achievement.

To "Death and the Maiden" (Chapter 13), whose tragic intensity the Quintet shares and deepens; the Quintet's eruptions are kin to the quartet's violence, its lyricism kin to the quartet's slow-movement consolation.

#### The 1828 context (Thread A note)

The String Quintet belongs to the final months of 1828, the climax of the most extraordinary year in musical history, alongside the late piano sonatas, the _Great_ C major Symphony, and the _Schwanengesang_ songs. Schubert was thirty-one, his health failing through the autumn; he completed the Quintet, offered it to a publisher, and died in November without hearing it. It is the chamber-music summit of that final surge, and one of the summits of his whole life's work, the fullest realization of the late style, written by a dying man who never heard it and whom the world would not fully recognize for decades. With the chamber music complete at its peak, the book turns to the last great region of Schubert's output, the symphonies, and especially the two late ones, the _Unfinished_ and the _Great_ C major, that, like the Quintet, belong to the late style and, like so much of Schubert, waited long for recognition. The next chapter begins with the early symphonies before the two late masterpieces; but the String Quintet stands here, at the close of Part V, as perhaps the single work that most completely embodies what Schubert became, and what the world so nearly lost.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"The String Quintet's Adagio creates a sense of 'suspended time', music floating in a vast, motionless calm, then shatters it with a turbulent central eruption, then returns to a serenity 'transfigured' by the storm it has passed through. The chapter calls this 'the late Schubert's deepest achievement': a peace 'earned, not given,' that 'holds the deepest peace and the deepest pain together.' Listen to the Adagio. Can you feel time stop in the outer sections? What does the central eruption do to the serenity that returns? Why might a peace that has passed through a storm be more profound than one that never knew it?"_

* _"Schubert wrote one of the greatest works in all of music and never heard it performed; the Quintet waited twenty-two years after his death for its first performance, while the world still thought of him as a writer of pleasant songs. The chapter marvels at 'a creative act so complete ... that needed no audience to be what it is.' What do you make of a masterpiece written for no occasion and no audience, possibly by a man who knew he might not live to hear it? Does it change how you hear the Quintet to know its composer never did?"_

* _"The chapter flags the famous saying that 'if there is a heaven, Schubert's Quintet plays there' (often attributed to Heine) as 'of uncertain provenance', a probably-apocryphal legend, while accepting 'the underlying truth it gestures at.' Why does the book bother to question a harmless, flattering quotation? What is the value of distinguishing the documented from the legendary, even when the legend expresses something true? Does the Quintet need the 'heaven' quote, or does the music speak for itself?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find a deeply felt reading (Casals or the Pavel Haas Quartet). Use this pass to hear the whole late style converging in one work, rather than the Adagio and first movement as separate items.

  1. **The two-cello sound and the singing duet.** From the first bars, hear the rich, deep, glowing sonority the two cellos produce, and listen especially for the first movement's great second theme, where the two cellos sing a long, warm melody together in duet, one of the most beautiful sounds in chamber music, unique to this work.

  2. **The suspended stillness of the Adagio's outer sections.** Let yourself float in the motionless calm, the held middle voices, the floating violin line above, the plucked cello below. Feel time stop. This is, for many listeners, the most beautiful music Schubert wrote, and its serenity is the point; do not wait for it to "go somewhere."

  3. **The eruption and the transfigured return (Adagio center).** Listen for the shattering of the stillness into a passionate, anguished storm, and then for the return of the serene music, now shadowed by what intervened. Hear how the eruption reveals that the serenity was never naive, the peace holds the storm within it. This is the late Schubert's deepest achievement.

  4. **The convergence of the whole late style.** Across the work, hear everything at once: the spaciousness, the harmonic journeys to distant keys, the major-minor shadowing of the radiant music, the long-breathed melody, the holding-together of beauty and darkness. The Quintet is the fullest realization of everything Schubert's instrumental art became; hearing the convergence is the deepest reward of the book's listening.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** (the late-style siblings): the String Quintet's Adagio and the first movement of the B-flat Sonata D 960 (Chapter 10), back to back. Both are summits of the late style, sharing the suspended, shadowed beauty and (in D 960's case via the trill, in the Quintet via the eruption) the holding-together of peace and darkness. Hearing them together reveals the unity of the late style across chamber and piano.

**Listen** (the two quintets): the _Trout_ Quintet (Chapter 12) and the String Quintet, the sunny early quintet and the sublime late one. Both enrich the texture with an extra low instrument; the contrast between the holiday happiness of 1819 and the profound depth of 1828 measures Schubert's whole journey.

**Listen** (multiple Adagios): the Quintet's slow movement in two or three different recordings, attending to how each handles the suspended time of the outer sections and the violence of the eruption. The Adagio varies significantly between ensembles, and comparing readings deepens your sense of what the music holds.

**Read** : the chapters on the String Quintet in the major biographies (Newbould, Gibbs, Byrne Bodley) and the analytical literature (Charles Rosen and others) on the late style. The documentary context of 1828, the offer to the publisher, the silence after Schubert's death, is in Deutsch's documentary biography. The Quintet is so widely revered that much has been written about it; the listener who falls in love with the Adagio (as many do) will find a rich literature waiting.

## Chapter 15: The Early Symphonies 1-6 (1813-1818)

Part VI turns to the orchestra, and like the piano sonatas, Schubert's symphonies tell a story of a melodist learning to build large structures, and of a reputation long overshadowed by Beethoven. Schubert wrote his first six symphonies between 1813 and 1818, all by the age of twenty-one, and they are charming, fluent, classical works, the apprenticeship of a symphonist. This chapter covers them, anchoring on the C minor "Tragic" Symphony No. 4 and glancing at the Mozartean Symphony No. 5, and it sets up the two late symphonies, the _Unfinished_ and the _Great_ C major, that are the real summits of his orchestral output and the subjects of the next two chapters.

The early symphonies matter for the book the way the First Symphony (Chapter 3) and the early sonatas (Chapter 9) mattered: as a baseline. They show Schubert's symphonic starting point, the bright classical inheritance of Haydn and Mozart, with Beethoven's example beginning to pull at him, so that the distance to the late symphonies can be measured. They are also genuinely enjoyable music, underplayed and underrated, well worth hearing for their own youthful freshness. And the "Tragic" Symphony in particular shows the young Schubert reaching, for the first time in the orchestra, toward something darker and more dramatic, the C-minor world he associated with Beethoven.

### 15.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote

Between 1813 and 1818, the years from age sixteen to twenty-one, Schubert wrote six symphonies. He wrote them for the amateur and semi-amateur orchestras of his world, the Stadtkonvikt student orchestra in which he had played, and later a private orchestra that grew out of the family quartet and met in friends' homes, the same Schubertiade-adjacent world of music-among-friends that produced so much of his output. These were not works written for the great public concert halls or for posterity; they were written to be played by the musicians around him, for the pleasure of playing, in the classical idiom those amateur players knew and loved.

This is the first thing to understand about the early symphonies: they belong to the domestic, amateur, classical world, not to the public, heroic, Beethovenian one. Beethoven, in these same years, was writing symphonies for the great Viennese concert public, vast, ambitious, revolutionary works that redefined what a symphony could be. The teenage Schubert, writing for his friends' orchestra, was working in an older, gentler, more intimate tradition, the classical symphony of Haydn and Mozart, written with fluency and charm rather than revolutionary ambition. The early symphonies are sometimes faulted for not being more Beethovenian, but this is to misunderstand them: they were not trying to be. They are classical symphonies, written for friends, by a young master of the classical idiom.

And yet Beethoven's shadow falls across them increasingly, especially in the Fourth, the "Tragic," of 1816. Here, for the first time, Schubert reached for the orchestra toward something darker and more dramatic, choosing the key of C minor, the key Beethoven had made his own for works of tragic struggle (the Fifth Symphony, the _Pathétique_ Sonata), and giving the symphony the title "Tragic" himself. The Fourth is the young Schubert testing whether he could write a "serious," dramatic, Beethovenian symphony, and the result is fascinating: not yet the real thing (the drama is more gestured-at than achieved), but a clear sign of the ambition that would, years later, produce the _Unfinished_ and the _Great_ C major.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen

  1. **The classical brightness and fluency.** The early symphonies (the Fifth especially) are bright, clean, fluent classical works, full of good tunes and easy energy, in the manner of Haydn and Mozart. Listen for the classical clarity, the singable melodies, the bright orchestral sound, the same world as the First Symphony (Chapter 3), now more assured.

  2. **The "Tragic" reaching for darkness (Symphony No. 4).** The Fourth opens with a slow, dark introduction and is in the dramatic key of C minor; it reaches for a Beethovenian seriousness and weight. Listen for the darker, more dramatic ambition, the slow brooding introduction, the C-minor urgency, and notice both the genuine reach and the ways it falls a little short of the real drama the late symphonies will achieve. The Fourth is the young Schubert trying on tragedy.

  3. **The melodist within the symphonist.** As in all of Schubert, listen for the quality of the tunes, especially in the slow movements. Even when reaching for Beethovenian drama, Schubert thinks in song; the lyrical melodies are the most personal and most "Schubertian" element, and they point toward the lyrical greatness of the late symphonies.

#### One-sentence background

Schubert composed his first six symphonies between 1813 and 1818, all by age twenty-one, for the amateur and semi-amateur orchestras of his circle, in the bright classical idiom of Haydn and Mozart; this chapter anchors on the Symphony No. 4 in C minor, "Tragic," D 417 (1816, ~30 minutes), in which Schubert reaches toward Beethovenian darkness and drama (and which he titled himself), and glances at the Mozartean Symphony No. 5 in B-flat major, D 485 (1816, ~26 minutes); the **Primary Recording** for this chapter is the Chamber Orchestra of Europe conducted by Claudio Abbado (Deutsche Grammophon, complete Schubert symphonies) or the Berlin Philharmonic under Karl Böhm (Deutsche Grammophon); for a lighter, period-style sound, a historically informed cycle.

EN: "Schubert Symphony No. 4 Tragic D 417 Abbado" or "Schubert Symphony 5 B-flat D 485 Böhm" ZH: "舒伯特 第四交响曲 悲剧 D417 阿巴多" 或 "舒伯特 第五交响曲 降B大调 D485 贝姆" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

**Alternative recordings** within the recording-hallucination safeguard (≥10 years old OR ≥3 reviews):

* The Chamber Orchestra of Europe under Claudio Abbado (Deutsche Grammophon), lithe and transparent, excellent in the early symphonies.
* The Berlin Philharmonic under Karl Böhm (Deutsche Grammophon), warmer and fuller, a classic complete cycle.
* The Academy of St Martin in the Fields under Sir Neville Marriner (Philips), chamber-scaled and clear.

### 15.2 The Listening Window

This Window focuses on the "Tragic" Symphony No. 4 (the anchor) and notes the Fifth. Timings refer to a typical reading; navigate by movement.

#### Symphony No. 4 in C minor, "Tragic," D 417

**First movement (Adagio molto — Allegro vivace), 0:00.** The symphony opens with a slow, dark, brooding introduction in C minor, full of dramatic dotted rhythms and harmonic tension, a clear reach for Beethovenian seriousness (compare the slow introductions of Haydn and the dramatic minor-key gestures of Beethoven). Then the fast main movement launches, urgent and energetic, in C minor. Listen for the contrast between the dark introduction and the driving Allegro, and for the genuine dramatic ambition, this is the young Schubert trying to write a "tragic," serious symphony. Notice, too, that the drama is somewhat conventional; the C-minor urgency is more a matter of gesture and key than of the genuine, hard-won struggle Beethoven (or the later Schubert) achieves. But the reach is real and impressive for a nineteen-year-old.

**Second movement (Andante), about 10:00.** A lyrical slow movement, and here the real Schubert shines: a gentle, songful melody of genuine beauty, the melodist within the symphonist. Listen for the singing tune and the warm, expressive writing; this is the movement where the Fourth most reveals Schubert's personal voice, and it points toward the lyrical slow movements of the late symphonies.

**Third movement (Menuetto, Allegro vivace), about 18:00.** A vigorous minuet with a gentler trio, fast and energetic, with a darker, more driving character than a typical classical minuet (the C-minor seriousness carrying over). Listen for the energy and the slightly stormy quality.

**Fourth movement (Allegro), about 23:00.** A fast, energetic finale, driving and dramatic, that works through its C-minor material and ends, characteristically for the period, by turning to C major for a brighter close (the "darkness to light" trajectory Beethoven made famous). Listen for the energetic drive and the turn to major at the end, the young Schubert following the Beethovenian model of tragedy resolving into triumph, though more by convention than by the genuine struggle the model implies.

#### Symphony No. 5 in B-flat major, D 485 (brief)

The Fifth, also from 1816, is the opposite of the Fourth: bright, sunny, Mozartean, scored for a small classical orchestra (no trumpets, drums, or clarinets, the lightest of Schubert's symphony orchestras). It is the most purely classical and most immediately charming of the early symphonies, often compared to Mozart for its grace and clarity. Listen, if you sample it, for the Mozartean lightness, the elegant tunes, and the sheer charm; the Fifth is the early symphony most often played, and it shows Schubert's mastery of the classical idiom at its most delightful, the sunny counterpart to the Fourth's reach for darkness.

### 15.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### The classical symphony, written for friends

The early symphonies are classical symphonies in the Haydn-Mozart tradition, and understanding what that means is the key to hearing them rightly. The classical symphony, as Haydn and Mozart perfected it, is a four-movement orchestral work, a fast first movement (often with a slow introduction), a lyrical slow movement, a minuet, and a fast finale, built on clear, balanced forms, bright orchestral colors, and singable themes, for the pleasure and edification of an audience. It is an art of clarity, balance, grace, and good humor, not of revolutionary struggle or vast ambition. Schubert was trained in this tradition (Chapter 3), wrote his early symphonies in it, and wrote them for the amateur orchestras of his world to play.

This is why the early symphonies should not be measured against Beethoven's. Beethoven, in these same years, was transforming the symphony into a vehicle for heroic struggle and revolutionary ambition, vast, dramatic, public works. The teenage Schubert, writing for friends in the classical idiom, was doing something different and more modest: writing fluent, charming, classical symphonies for amateur players, in the tradition he had inherited. Judged as classical symphonies, they succeed: they are well-made, tuneful, energetic, and enjoyable, the work of a young master of the idiom. Judged as failed Beethoven symphonies, they disappoint, but that is the wrong judgment, because they were not trying to be Beethoven symphonies. The listening lesson is the one from the sonatas (Chapter 9): hear the early symphonies on their own terms, as classical symphonies written for friends, and enjoy their fluency and charm, rather than wishing they were more ambitious than they meant to be.

#### The "Tragic" and the reach toward Beethoven

The Fourth Symphony is the most interesting of the six precisely because it does reach toward Beethoven, and hearing both the reach and its limits is instructive. With the "Tragic," Schubert stepped outside the bright classical idiom of the others and tried to write a serious, dramatic, weighty symphony in the Beethovenian manner: he chose C minor (Beethoven's tragic key), wrote a dark slow introduction, gave the symphony a "tragic" title, and aimed for drama and struggle rather than classical grace. This is the young Schubert testing his ambition, trying on the role of the serious, dramatic symphonist.

The result is fascinating and a little uneven. The ambition is real and impressive for a nineteen-year-old; the dark introduction, the C-minor urgency, the dramatic gestures all show Schubert reaching for something beyond charm. But the drama is more gestured-at than achieved: the C-minor seriousness is partly a matter of key and convention rather than the genuine, hard-won, motivically argued struggle that Beethoven (and, later, the _Unfinished_ Schubert) accomplishes. The young Schubert can put on the costume of tragedy, the dark key, the brooding introduction, the dramatic energy, but he cannot yet fully inhabit it; the symphony reaches for Beethovenian weight without quite earning it. This is not a criticism so much as an observation about an apprenticeship: the Fourth shows the ambition that will, years later, be fully realized in the late symphonies, before the technique to realize it has fully arrived. It is the document of a young composer reaching beyond his current grasp, and the reach itself is the point. When you hear the _Unfinished_ (Chapter 16), with its genuine, organic darkness, you will hear how far Schubert traveled from the "Tragic"'s gestured drama to the real thing.

#### The melodist within the symphonist

Across the early symphonies, the most personal and most "Schubertian" element is the quality of the tunes, especially in the slow movements, and this is worth attending to because it is the seed of the late symphonies' greatness. Schubert, as the whole book has shown, was before anything a melodist, and even in these classical apprentice symphonies, his melodic gift is the thing that most distinguishes them from generic classical works. The slow movement of the Fourth, the graceful tunes of the Fifth, the lyrical themes throughout, these are the moments where the young Schubert's own voice comes through the inherited idiom, and they are consistently the most beautiful and most memorable parts of the early symphonies.

This melodic gift is also, as in the sonatas, the thing Schubert had to learn to deploy in large symphonic structures. A symphony, especially a Beethovenian one, is partly an argument, a working-out of conflict through the development and transformation of themes, and pure melody does not argue. The early symphonies often pour out lovely tunes within classical forms without quite knowing how to build a large dramatic structure from them; the development sections (where themes are taken apart and recombined) are competent but not yet profound. The story of Schubert's symphonic maturity, which the next two chapters tell, is the story of how he learned to build large symphonic structures that suit a melodist, the _Unfinished_ , where lyrical themes generate genuine drama, and the _Great_ C major, where melody and large-scale structure achieve a "heavenly length" that is the melodist's own kind of symphonic greatness, different from Beethoven's argument but equally valid. The early symphonies are where you hear the melodic gift in its classical apprenticeship, before Schubert learned to make symphonies that were true to his lyrical nature. Listen for the tunes; they are the future.

### 15.4 The Stories

#### The amateur-orchestra world

The early symphonies belong to a specific social world worth knowing: the amateur and semi-amateur orchestras of Schubert's Vienna. Schubert wrote them not for professional orchestras or public concerts but for the music-making circles around him, first the Stadtkonvikt student orchestra in which he had played as a boy, and later a private orchestra that grew out of the Schubert family string quartet, expanding as more friends and musicians joined, and meeting in private homes to play for their own pleasure. This was the same world of music-among-friends that produced the chamber music and the Schubertiade, extended to the orchestra: amateurs and semi-professionals gathering to play symphonies in someone's house or hall, for the joy of playing.

This context shapes the music. The early symphonies are scaled for these forces, modest classical orchestras of the kind an amateur group could muster, and written in an idiom those players knew and could manage. They are companionable, unpretentious music, like the chamber works, made for participation rather than for awe. This is part of why they are bright, fluent, and unintimidating: they were meant to be played by friends, not to overwhelm a paying public. It also explains why Schubert, unlike Beethoven, did not write symphonies as grand public statements in these years, he had no access to the great public orchestras and concerts, and his musical home was the private gathering. The early symphonies are the orchestral expression of the Schubertiade world, and hearing them as such, music for friends to play, not monuments for the concert hall, is the key to their modest, companionable charm.

#### The neglect of the symphonies

Like the sonatas and so much else, Schubert's symphonies were long neglected, and the early ones still are, relatively. In Schubert's lifetime, none of his symphonies received a major public performance; they were played, if at all, by the amateur orchestras for whom they were written. After his death, the two late symphonies (the _Unfinished_ and the _Great_ C major) were gradually discovered and entered the repertoire (the stories are in the next two chapters), but the early six remained obscure for generations, dismissed as classical apprentice work overshadowed by Beethoven and by Schubert's own late masterpieces. Only in the twentieth century, with complete recorded cycles, did the early symphonies become widely available and begin to be appreciated for their own youthful charm.

They are still the least-performed part of Schubert's major output, rarely programmed in concerts, known mostly through recordings. This is a pity, because they are genuinely enjoyable, fluent, tuneful, energetic classical symphonies, the Fifth especially is a delight, and they show the symphonic apprenticeship of a great composer. The neglect is the same pattern as with the sonatas (Chapter 9): music overshadowed by Beethoven and by the composer's own later masterpieces. The listener who has come this far in the book, and who has learned to hear Schubert's classical inheritance (Chapter 3) and his lyrical gift, will find the early symphonies a pleasant and instructive region, the orchestral counterpart to the early sonatas, and the baseline against which the late symphonies' greatness can be measured.

#### Recording history briefly

The early symphonies live almost entirely in complete cycles, since they are rarely programmed individually (the "Tragic" and the popular Fifth are the ones most likely to be found alone). For them, a chamber-scaled or period reading (Abbado's Chamber Orchestra of Europe cycle, or a historically informed one) tends to suit the music best, since their classical scale and brightness can be weighed down by a large modern orchestra. (The full symphony lineage is in Chapter 20.)

#### Cross-piece links you will hear later

The early symphonies connect across the volume.

To the First Symphony (Chapter 3), the starting point of the symphonic journey, of which the early six are the continuation; the bright classical idiom is the same, developing in assurance.

To the _Unfinished_ Symphony (Chapter 16) and the _Great_ C major (Chapter 17), the late masterpieces, against which the early symphonies serve as baseline. The "Tragic"'s reach toward Beethovenian drama, gestured but not yet achieved, makes the genuine, organic darkness of the _Unfinished_ legible as the fulfillment of an ambition first attempted here; the early symphonies' lyrical tunes point toward the late symphonies' melodic greatness.

To _Close Listening: Beethoven_ , for the C-minor signature: the Fourth's choice of C minor and its "darkness to light" trajectory consciously echo Beethoven's tragic-key works (the Fifth Symphony above all). Schubert, in the "Tragic," is explicitly working in Beethoven's shadow, and the cross-reference illuminates both.

To the early sonatas (Chapter 9), the keyboard counterpart of the early symphonies: both are the apprentice work of a melodist learning to build large structures, both long neglected, both rewarding on their own terms.

#### The 1813-1818 context (Thread A note)

The early symphonies span the years from the end of the Stadtkonvikt (1813, the First Symphony) through the unhappy schoolteacher years (Chapter 4) to the threshold of the first freelance maturity (1818). The "Tragic" and the Fifth both come from 1816, the heart of the schoolteacher period, the same astonishing years that produced the flood of songs. They are the orchestral output of Schubert's youth, written for friends, in the classical idiom, before the illness and the late period. After the Sixth (1818), Schubert's symphonic path becomes complicated: he began several symphonies that he left unfinished (including, eventually, the _Unfinished_ of 1822), and there is a gap in completed symphonies until the _Great_ C major. The early six stand as the complete, classical, youthful phase of his symphonic work, the baseline. The next chapter leaps forward to 1822 and the work that transformed Schubert's symphonic art and remains one of the most beloved symphonies ever written: the _Unfinished_ , where the gestured drama of the "Tragic" becomes genuine, organic, unforgettable darkness, and where Schubert, for the first time in the orchestra, achieves the real thing.

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find a chamber-scaled or period reading (Abbado, or a HIP cycle). Listen to the "Tragic" Symphony and sample the Fifth, attending to these things.

  1. **The classical brightness and fluency.** In the Fifth especially, hear the bright, clean, Mozartean classical idiom, the singable tunes, the elegant clarity, the same world as the First Symphony, now more assured. Enjoy the youthful charm on its own terms.

  2. **The reach toward darkness in the "Tragic" (Symphony No. 4).** Listen for the dark slow introduction, the C-minor urgency, the dramatic gestures, the young Schubert trying on Beethovenian tragedy. Notice both the genuine ambition and the ways the drama is more gestured than achieved; this is the apprentice reaching beyond his current grasp.

  3. **The melodist in the slow movements.** Attend to the lyrical tunes, especially the Andante of the Fourth, where Schubert's personal voice comes through the classical idiom most clearly. The melodic gift is the most "Schubertian" element and the seed of the late symphonies' greatness.

  4. **The baseline for the late symphonies.** Hold the early symphonies' bright classical idiom and gestured drama in memory. When you reach the _Unfinished_ and the _Great_ C major, the distance traveled, from classical charm and reached-for tragedy to genuine, organic symphonic greatness, is one of the book's central stories.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** (the charmer): the Symphony No. 5 in B-flat, D 485, complete, the most delightful and most-played of the early symphonies. Its Mozartean grace and clarity make it the best single entry point to the early symphonic Schubert, and a pure pleasure on its own terms.

**Listen** (the Beethoven model): Beethoven's Symphony No. 5 in C minor, or the _Pathétique_ Sonata, to hear the C-minor "tragedy resolving into triumph" model the young Schubert was reaching for in the "Tragic." Hearing Beethoven's genuine, hard-won struggle makes both Schubert's ambition and its limits in the Fourth legible (cross-reference to _Close Listening: Beethoven_).

**Listen** (the leap ahead): the _Unfinished_ Symphony (Chapter 16), to hear what Schubert's symphonic art became. The contrast with the early symphonies' classical charm and gestured drama measures the distance to genuine symphonic greatness.

**Read** : Brian Newbould, _Schubert: The Music and the Man_ (1997), the source-of-record for the symphonic Schubert; Newbould is a leading scholar of Schubert's symphonies (including his completions of the unfinished ones) and is especially good on the early symphonies and the symphonic development. The documentary context of the amateur-orchestra world is in Deutsch's documentary biography and the major biographies.

## Chapter 16: Symphony No. 8 in B minor "Unfinished" D 759 (1822)

The early symphonies showed a melodist learning his craft in the classical idiom and reaching, in the "Tragic," toward a Beethovenian drama he could not yet achieve. The _Unfinished_ Symphony, written in 1822, is where Schubert achieves it, and more: it is one of the most beloved and most perfect symphonies ever written, and it is, famously, incomplete, just two finished movements where a symphony should have four. The paradox at the heart of this chapter is that an unfinished work should feel so complete, so satisfying, so monumental, that the absence of the missing movements is felt by almost no one as a lack.

The chapter anchors on the two surviving movements, the dark, dramatic first movement and the serene-yet-troubled second, and it takes up the famous mysteries: why Schubert left the symphony unfinished (no one knows), how the manuscript survived and finally reached the world (decades after his death), and the tangle of its numbering. But above all it attends to the music, because the _Unfinished_ is where Schubert's symphonic genius first fully arrives: where the gestured drama of the "Tragic" becomes genuine, organic, unforgettable darkness, and where lyrical melody and symphonic drama fuse into something new in the history of the form, the Romantic symphony being born.

### 16.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote

In October and November 1822, Schubert, twenty-five, began a symphony in B minor. He wrote two complete movements and began a third, a scherzo, sketching it in some detail and orchestrating the opening before stopping. And then he set it aside. He never returned to it; he lived another six years, wrote the _Great_ C major Symphony and much else, and left the B minor symphony with two finished movements and an abandoned scherzo. Why he stopped is one of the great mysteries of music, and the honest answer, which this book gives, is that no one knows.

The circumstances deepen the mystery. In 1823 Schubert gave the manuscript (or a related score) to his friend Anselm Hüttenbrenner, apparently as a gift or in connection with an honorary diploma Schubert had received from a music society in Graz, Hüttenbrenner's city. And Hüttenbrenner kept it, for decades, telling almost no one, through Schubert's death in 1828 and for nearly forty years after. Not until 1865, forty-three years after the symphony was written and thirty-seven years after Schubert's death, did the conductor Johann von Herbeck finally extract the manuscript from the aged Hüttenbrenner and give the _Unfinished_ its first performance, in Vienna. One of the most beloved symphonies ever written sat in a drawer for over four decades while the world had no idea it existed.

The work was an immediate sensation at its 1865 premiere and has been among the most popular symphonies in the repertoire ever since. The combination of its haunting beauty, its perfect-feeling incompleteness, and the romance of its long-hidden manuscript made it irresistible, and it remains, for many listeners, the gateway to Schubert's orchestral music and one of the best-loved symphonies of all. That it is "unfinished" has become part of its identity and its mystique, but the deeper truth is that the two movements Schubert completed are so perfect, and feel so complete, that the symphony stands as one of the supreme achievements of the form despite, or even because of, its incompleteness.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen

  1. **The dark, hushed opening from the depths.** The symphony begins not with a grand gesture but with a low, dark, mysterious theme in the cellos and basses alone, quiet, brooding, rising from the depths of the orchestra. This opening, dark, soft, ominous, is utterly unlike the bright classical openings of the early symphonies; it is the sound of the Romantic symphony being born. Listen for it: the music emerging from darkness and silence, the cellos and basses murmuring alone before the rest of the orchestra enters. It sets the whole work's mood of beautiful, troubled darkness.

  2. **The famous lyrical second theme, and what interrupts it.** Soon a gorgeous, song-like melody appears in the cellos (one of Schubert's most beloved tunes), warm and consoling over a gently rocking accompaniment. But listen for what happens to it: the lyrical flow is suddenly, violently interrupted by a crashing orchestral outburst, and the music plunges into drama and turbulence. This interruption of beauty by violence is the heart of the first movement's drama, and it is the genuine, organic darkness the "Tragic" only gestured at.

  3. **The serene-yet-troubled second movement.** The slow movement (the second and last completed movement) is more peaceful, a flowing, lyrical Andante, but its serenity is repeatedly shadowed by darker, more troubled passages. Listen for the beautiful flowing main music and for the moments where it darkens, the same holding-together of beauty and shadow you have heard throughout the late Schubert. The movement ends quietly, peacefully, and the symphony simply stops there, with no scherzo, no finale, and yet it feels, somehow, complete.

#### One-sentence background

The Symphony No. 8 in B minor, "Unfinished," D 759, was composed in 1822; Schubert completed two movements (a dark, dramatic first movement and a serene-yet-troubled Andante) and abandoned a third (a scherzo, sketched but not orchestrated), and never returned to it, for reasons unknown; the manuscript was given to Anselm Hüttenbrenner, who kept it hidden for over forty years until its first performance in 1865; it is among the most beloved symphonies ever written and feels complete despite its two movements; the **Primary Recording** for this chapter is the Vienna Philharmonic under Carlos Kleiber (Deutsche Grammophon, coupled with the Beethoven Fifth) or under Karl Böhm; for a HIP alternative, John Eliot Gardiner or Roger Norrington.

#### Search Hints

EN: "Schubert Unfinished Symphony B minor D 759 Carlos Kleiber Vienna" or "Schubert Symphony 8 Unfinished Böhm" ZH: "舒伯特 未完成交响曲 B小调 D759 卡洛斯·克莱伯 维也纳爱乐" 或 "舒伯特 第八交响曲 未完成 贝姆" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

**Alternative recordings** within the recording-hallucination safeguard (≥10 years old OR ≥3 reviews):

* The Vienna Philharmonic under Carlos Kleiber (Deutsche Grammophon), an electrifying, intensely dramatic reading, a modern reference.
* The Berlin or Vienna Philharmonic under Karl Böhm (Deutsche Grammophon), warm, weighty, and idiomatic.
* The Vienna Philharmonic under Wilhelm Furtwängler (historic), a deeply expressive, monumental reading.
* John Eliot Gardiner with the Orchestre Révolutionnaire et Romantique or Roger Norrington (period-instrument), for the lighter, more transparent, faster HIP approach.

### 16.2 The Listening Window

This Window walks through the two completed movements in some detail; they are the whole anchor. Timings refer to a typical reading; navigate by movement.

#### First movement (Allegro moderato)

**0:00. The opening from the depths.** The symphony begins with a low, dark, quiet theme in the cellos and basses alone, brooding, mysterious, rising from the orchestra's depths. This is one of the most arresting openings in symphonic music, and it announces a new world: not the bright classical symphony but a dark, Romantic, dramatic one. Fix this opening in your ear; it returns at crucial moments and gives the whole movement its troubled, searching character.

**About 0:30. The first theme proper.** Over a soft, agitated accompaniment in the strings (a quiet, shivering figure) and a plaintive theme in the oboe and clarinet, the movement's main material unfolds, restless, melancholy, beautiful. Listen for the shivering string accompaniment and the wistful wind theme; the mood is one of beautiful unease.

**About 1:40. The famous lyrical second theme.** The cellos sing a gorgeous, warm, consoling melody (one of Schubert's most beloved tunes), over a gently rocking accompaniment, a moment of lyrical beauty and warmth. Listen for this glorious cello melody; it is the emotional heart of the movement's lyrical pole, song-like and tender.

**About 2:40. The interruption.** And then, crucially, the lyrical flow is shattered: a sudden, violent orchestral outburst (a crashing chord, a plunge into turbulence) interrupts the beautiful melody, and the music erupts into drama and conflict. Listen for this interruption, the violence breaking into the beauty; it is the central dramatic stroke of the movement, and it recurs. This is the genuine, organic darkness, beauty and violence in dramatic conflict, that the "Tragic" reached for but could not achieve. Here it is real.

**The development and recapitulation.** The movement works through its material in a genuine symphonic drama: the dark opening theme and the lyrical and violent elements developed, intensified, and brought into conflict, the dark opening from the depths returning to dominate the development. Listen for the genuine dramatic working-out, the lyrical and dark elements in real conflict, not gestured but achieved, and for the return of the themes in the recapitulation. The movement ends with the dark opening theme, fortissimo, and a powerful, tragic close in B minor. This is Schubert's first fully achieved symphonic drama, the melodist's lyrical gift and a genuine dramatic structure fused.

#### Second movement (Andante con moto)

**0:00. The flowing, serene main music.** The slow movement opens with a flowing, peaceful, lyrical theme in a warm major (E major), gentle and consoling, over a calm accompaniment, descending pizzicato in the basses, smooth strings, serene winds. Listen for the flowing serenity, the beautiful, song-like main music; after the first movement's drama, this is a turn toward peace.

**About 2:00. The shadowed second theme.** A second theme appears, introduced by the clarinet (and then oboe), a wistful, slightly melancholy melody that is one of the movement's loveliest moments, but already touched with shadow. Listen for the plaintive wind melody and the way the serenity is tinged with sadness.

**Throughout. Serenity repeatedly shadowed.** The movement alternates between its flowing serene music and darker, more troubled, sometimes turbulent passages, the peace repeatedly disturbed by shadow and drama before returning. Listen for this alternation, the holding-together of beauty and darkness that runs through all the late Schubert, here in symphonic form. The serenity is never naive; it is always aware of the shadow.

**The quiet end.** The movement, and the symphony, ends quietly, peacefully, the serene music returning and fading to a gentle close. There is no scherzo, no finale; the symphony simply ends here, after two movements. And yet, remarkably, it feels complete: the two movements, the dramatic first and the serene-yet-troubled second, form a satisfying whole, and the absence of the missing movements is felt by almost no listener as a lack. Listen to how the symphony ends, and ask yourself whether it feels unfinished. For most listeners, it does not.

### 16.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### The Romantic symphony is born

The _Unfinished_ is a landmark in the history of the symphony, and understanding why is the chapter's central point. The early symphonies (Chapter 15) were classical symphonies in the Haydn-Mozart tradition, bright, balanced, fluent. The _Unfinished_ is something new: a Romantic symphony, dark, dramatic, lyrical, emotionally direct, built on a new kind of relationship between melody and drama. From its very first bars, the dark theme rising from the cellos and basses, it announces a different world from the classical symphony: not brightness and balance but darkness, mystery, and emotional depth.

What makes it Romantic, and new, is the fusion of Schubert's lyrical gift with genuine symphonic drama. The classical symphony built drama through the development of short motifs (Beethoven's way); Schubert, the melodist, found a different path: he built drama through the conflict between lyrical beauty and violent darkness, the gorgeous cello melody interrupted by the crashing outburst, the serene slow movement shadowed by turbulence. The drama is not the development of motifs but the collision of moods — beauty against violence, serenity against shadow — and this is a new, Romantic, lyrical kind of symphonic drama, suited to a melodist's gifts. The _Unfinished_ is where Schubert solved the problem the early symphonies posed: how to build genuine symphonic drama while remaining true to his lyrical nature. The solution, drama through the conflict of lyrical and dark elements, opened the way for the whole Romantic symphony that followed (Bruckner, who revered Schubert, and many others built on it). The _Unfinished_ is thus not just a beloved work but a pivotal one, the birth of the Romantic symphony, and hearing it as such, as the moment the symphony turned from classical balance to Romantic drama and lyricism, is the key to its historical importance.

#### Beauty interrupted: the genuine darkness

The first movement's central dramatic stroke, the gorgeous lyrical second theme shattered by the violent orchestral outburst, is worth dwelling on, because it is where Schubert achieves the genuine, organic darkness that the "Tragic" Symphony (Chapter 15) only gestured at. In the "Tragic," the drama was a matter of key and convention, the costume of tragedy; in the _Unfinished_ , the drama is real, arising organically from the music itself. The beauty (the cello melody) and the violence (the interrupting outburst) are not gestures but genuine, opposed forces, and their conflict is the movement's true subject. The interruption shocks because the melody is so beautiful and the violence so sudden; the listener feels the beauty broken, the consolation shattered, and this felt conflict is the genuine drama the early Schubert could not achieve.

This is the same gesture, the interruption of beauty by darkness, that you have heard throughout the late Schubert: the trill beneath D 960's serenity, the eruption in the D 959 Andantino and the String Quintet's Adagio, the deceptive consolations of _Winterreise_. In the _Unfinished_ , it operates at symphonic scale, and it is the work's emotional core. The genuine darkness, beauty and violence in real conflict, is what makes the _Unfinished_ profound rather than merely lovely, and it is what distinguishes it from the gestured drama of the "Tragic." The melodist who, in the early symphonies, could write lovely tunes but not genuine drama, has learned, in the _Unfinished_ , to set his lyrical beauty in genuine, organic conflict with darkness, and the result is his first symphonic masterpiece. Listen for the interruption and for the conflict it opens; it is the moment Schubert's symphonic drama becomes real.

#### The completeness of the incomplete

The deepest puzzle of the _Unfinished_ is why it feels complete, and engaging with this puzzle is part of the chapter's work. A symphony, by classical convention, has four movements; the _Unfinished_ has two. By the rules, it is radically incomplete, missing half its movements. And yet almost no listener feels it as incomplete; the two movements form a satisfying, complete-feeling whole, and the _Unfinished_ stands as one of the most beloved and most perfect-seeming symphonies ever written. Why?

Several things contribute. The two movements are emotionally complementary, the dark, dramatic first movement and the serene-yet-troubled second, so that together they trace a complete emotional arc, from darkness and conflict to a shadowed peace. They are both lyrical in character (the first movement is marked Allegro moderato, not a fast Allegro), so they balance each other without needing the contrast a scherzo and fast finale would add. And they are both so perfectly realized, so complete in themselves, that they satisfy fully; there is no sense of a question left hanging, of a drama unresolved. The second movement's quiet, peaceful ending feels like an ending, not a pause. The result is that the _Unfinished_ , by some happy accident or deep instinct, achieves wholeness with two movements, and the missing scherzo and finale are not missed.

This raises a genuine question, which the book leaves open (the Conversation Seeds return to it): is the _Unfinished_ better unfinished? Some have argued that the two movements are so perfect together that a scherzo and finale could only have diminished them, that the work is complete as it is, and that Schubert's abandonment, whatever its cause, left us with something more perfect than a four-movement symphony would have been. Others find this romantic nonsense, a four-movement _Unfinished_ would simply be a different and possibly greater work, and the incompleteness is a loss, not a gain. (Scholars including Brian Newbould have made completions, using the scherzo sketches and other material; they are interesting but have not displaced the two-movement work in listeners' affections.) The book does not adjudicate. What it observes is the remarkable fact that an unfinished work feels complete, and invites you to sit with the puzzle: a symphony missing half its movements that no one wishes were longer. It is one of the strangest and most beautiful accidents in the history of music, if it was an accident at all.

### 16.4 The Stories

#### Why unfinished? The mystery

The central mystery, why Schubert abandoned the symphony, has no solution, and the book's honest answer is that no one knows. This is worth stating plainly, because the gap has attracted a great deal of speculation, much of it presented with more confidence than the evidence warrants. The documented facts are sparse: Schubert wrote two movements and part of a third in late 1822, then stopped, and never returned to the work in the six years of life remaining. No letter or document survives in which he explains the abandonment.

The hypotheses are several, and all are speculative. One connects the abandonment to the 1822 illness, which struck around the time the symphony was set aside: perhaps the illness interrupted the work, and Schubert never picked it up again. Another suggests he simply got absorbed in other projects (he was extraordinarily productive) and let the symphony lapse, as he let other works lapse. Another, more romantic, holds that he felt the two movements were complete in themselves and deliberately stopped, but there is no evidence for this, and it seems unlikely (the sketched scherzo suggests he meant to continue). Yet another notes that both completed movements share a triple meter and a certain similarity, and speculates that Schubert may have felt he could not write a sufficiently contrasting scherzo and finale, but this too is guesswork. The truth is that we do not know why Schubert stopped, and the book declines to pretend otherwise. The abandonment is a genuine mystery, and the honest response is to acknowledge it as such rather than to choose a favorite speculation and present it as fact. What we have is two perfect movements and an open question; the question has no answer, and the music does not need one.

#### The forty years in a drawer

The story of the manuscript's survival is almost as remarkable as the music. In 1823, Schubert gave the score (in connection with an honorary diploma from a Graz music society) to his friend Anselm Hüttenbrenner, himself a composer, who lived in Graz. And Hüttenbrenner kept it, privately, for over four decades. Through Schubert's death in 1828, through the gradual posthumous discovery of Schubert's other works, through the rise of his reputation, Hüttenbrenner held the _Unfinished_ manuscript and told almost no one. Why he sat on it for so long is itself something of a mystery, possibly inertia, possibly a sense of personal possession, possibly forgetfulness.

Not until 1865 did the conductor Johann von Herbeck, having heard rumors of an unknown Schubert symphony in Hüttenbrenner's possession, travel to Graz, charm or cajole the aged Hüttenbrenner into parting with the manuscript, and give the _Unfinished_ its first performance in Vienna, forty-three years after it was written and thirty-seven years after Schubert's death. The premiere was a triumph, and the symphony entered the repertoire at once, where it has remained among the most-loved of all symphonies. The story is the extreme case of the neglect that shadowed Schubert's posthumous reputation: not just unperformed but unknown, hidden in a drawer for four decades, while the world had no idea one of the great symphonies existed. It is a sobering reminder of how nearly the _Unfinished_ was lost entirely (had Hüttenbrenner died without revealing it, or had the manuscript been destroyed, the world would never have known), and of how much of Schubert's greatness reached the world only by luck and the persistence of a few advocates, decades after his death.

#### The numbering tangle

A brief note on the numbering, because it causes confusion. The _Unfinished_ is usually called Symphony No. 8, but it is sometimes called No. 7, and the numbering of Schubert's late symphonies is genuinely contested. The complication arises because Schubert left several symphonic projects in various states of completion, and different cataloguing traditions have counted them differently. The "No. 7" designation has sometimes referred to a different, largely unfinished symphony (D 729); the _Great_ C major has been called both No. 7 and No. 9; and the _Unfinished_ has been No. 7 or No. 8 depending on the system. The book follows the most common modern convention, the _Unfinished_ as No. 8 and the _Great_ C major as No. 9, but uses the D-numbers (D 759 for the _Unfinished_ , D 944 for the _Great_) as the unambiguous identifiers, since the numbering is contested and the D-numbers are not. When you encounter "Schubert's Seventh" or "Schubert's Ninth" in different sources, check the D-number to be sure which work is meant; the numbering is a tangle, the D-numbers are clear. This is a small instance of the book's general preference for the documented and unambiguous over the conventional and contested.

#### Recording history briefly

For a first encounter, an intensely dramatic modern reading (Carlos Kleiber with the Vienna Philharmonic) and a historically informed one (John Eliot Gardiner) make an instructive pair, the former emphasizing the work's Romantic drama, the latter its classical roots and transparency. Because the work is short (about twenty-five minutes for the two movements), it is easy to hear in multiple interpretations. (The full conductor lineage is in Chapter 20.)

#### Cross-piece links you will hear later

The _Unfinished_ connects across the volume.

To the "Tragic" Symphony No. 4 (Chapter 15), whose gestured reach toward Beethovenian drama the _Unfinished_ fulfills with genuine, organic darkness. The distance from the "Tragic"'s costume of tragedy to the _Unfinished_ 's real thing measures Schubert's symphonic maturation.

To the _Great_ C major Symphony (Chapter 17), the other late symphony, with which the _Unfinished_ forms the summit of Schubert's orchestral output. Where the _Unfinished_ is dark, dramatic, and concentrated (two movements), the _Great_ C major is expansive, radiant, and vast (four large movements); the two late symphonies are complementary peaks.

To the late chamber and piano music (the String Quintet, the late sonatas), which share the _Unfinished_ 's holding-together of beauty and darkness; the interruption of the lyrical second theme by violence is the symphonic sibling of the eruptions in the String Quintet's Adagio and the D 959 sonata.

#### The 1822 context (Thread A note)

The _Unfinished_ comes from 1822, the pivotal year: the year Schubert wrote the _Wanderer_ Fantasy (Chapter 9), the year just before the illness that would change everything, the threshold of the late period. It was written, and abandoned, around the time the 1822-1823 illness struck, and one hypothesis (unprovable) connects the abandonment to that illness. Whatever the cause, the _Unfinished_ stands at the hinge of Schubert's life, his first fully achieved symphonic masterpiece, written as the shadow was about to fall, and then mysteriously set aside, to wait forty years for the world. It is the symphonic counterpart to the great deepening that the illness opened in Schubert's art, the dark, dramatic, lyrical symphony that announces the Romantic symphony and the late Schubert at once. The next chapter takes up the other late symphony, the _Great_ C major, Schubert's symphonic summit, a vast, radiant, "heavenly length" work that, like the _Unfinished_ and so much else, waited for posthumous rescue, this time by Schumann and Mendelssohn, and that crowns Schubert's orchestral achievement with expansiveness and light where the _Unfinished_ gave darkness and concentration.
