## Chapter 20: Recording History — the Documented Schubert Performance Tradition 1900-2026

Schubert wrote much of his greatest music for a world that barely heard it, and a great deal of it he never heard performed at all. The String Quintet, the late sonatas, the _Great_ C major, the symphonies, all reached the public after his death, some decades after. The Schubert we know is, to an unusual degree, a Schubert constructed by performers, the singers, pianists, conductors, and ensembles who, over the past century and more, discovered, championed, and recorded his music, building the performance traditions through which we now hear him. This chapter surveys those traditions, lineage by lineage, so that you can navigate the vast recorded legacy and find your way to the performances that will become your own.

The chapter is organized by the four great regions of Schubert's output, the Lieder, the symphonies, the chamber music, and the piano music, because each has its own performance tradition, its own great names, its own history. It names the principal singers, accompanists, conductors, ensembles, and pianists of each, so that the consolidated starter list of first recordings to live with, gathered in Chapter 21 (§21.1), has the lineages behind it mapped here. This is not a complete discography, an impossibility, but a map of the terrain, a guide to the major lineages and the central figures, so that you can explore the recorded Schubert with some sense of where the landmarks lie. The recordings named throughout the book's individual chapters are gathered and contextualized here.

### 20.1 The Lieder Tradition

The Lieder are the heart of Schubert's output, and their performance tradition is the richest and most central. Because a Lied requires two artists, a singer and a pianist, the tradition has two intertwined lineages, and the great recordings are partnerships.

**The singers.** The dominant figure of the modern Lieder tradition is the German baritone Dietrich Fischer-Dieskau (1925-2012), who recorded essentially the entire body of Schubert songs for male voice (in a vast Deutsche Grammophon project) and whose readings, marked by clarity of word, subtlety of characterization, and intelligence of interpretation, became the reference point against which all others are measured. Fischer-Dieskau more than any other artist shaped how the postwar world hears Schubert's songs, and his recordings of the cycles (_Die schöne Müllerin_ , _Winterreise_ , _Schwanengesang_) and the individual songs remain central. Around and after him stand many great Lieder singers: the bass-baritone Hans Hotter (1909-2003), whose dark, weighty _Winterreise_ is for many the most overwhelming on record; the tenor Peter Schreier; the radiant tenor Fritz Wunderlich (1930-1966), whose _Die schöne Müllerin_ , made shortly before his early death, is a classic; and, among the modern generation, the baritones Matthias Goerne, Christian Gerhaher, and Thomas Quasthoff, and the tenor Ian Bostridge (the performer-scholar whose _Winterreise_ book the book drew on in Chapter 7). The female Lieder tradition, essential for the many songs better suited to or written for women's voices, includes the great sopranos and mezzos: Elisabeth Schwarzkopf, Elly Ameling, Janet Baker, Christa Ludwig, Brigitte Fassbaender (whose _Winterreise_ is celebrated), and Anne Sofie von Otter, among many others.

**The accompanists.** The pianist in a Lied is a full partner, not an accompanist in the subordinate sense, and the great Lieder pianists are central to the tradition. Gerald Moore (1899-1987) was the supreme accompanist of the mid-twentieth century, the partner of Fischer-Dieskau in the complete-songs project and of countless other singers, and his command of the songs' piano parts, the brook, the spinning wheel, the winter wind, set a standard for the art of accompaniment. Other distinguished Lieder pianists include Helmut Deutsch and, of special importance, Graham Johnson, who directed the Hyperion Schubert Edition (1988-2005), a recording of every Schubert song with a rotating cast of singers and extensive scholarly commentary, the single most complete and best-annotated recorded survey of the Lieder and an indispensable resource for the serious listener.

### 20.2 The Symphony Tradition

The symphonies, especially the two late ones, are repertoire staples, and their performance tradition turns largely on the question of scale, how to handle the classical brightness of the early symphonies and the "heavenly length" of the _Great_ C major.

**The conductors and orchestras.** The modern-orchestra tradition is represented by the great mid-twentieth-century conductors: Wilhelm Furtwängler, whose monumental, intense readings of the _Great_ C major and the _Unfinished_ are classics of the grand manner; Karl Böhm, whose warm, weighty, idiomatic complete cycle (with the Berlin Philharmonic) is a benchmark; Otto Klemperer, whose readings are broad and powerful; and, for the _Unfinished_ especially, Carlos Kleiber, whose electrifying Vienna Philharmonic recording is a modern reference. A clear, energetic, well-structured approach is exemplified by George Szell (Cleveland) in the _Great_ C major. The chamber-scaled tradition, lighter and more transparent, includes Claudio Abbado with the Chamber Orchestra of Europe (an outstanding modern complete cycle), Sir Neville Marriner with the Academy of St Martin in the Fields, and Sir Charles Mackerras. And the historically informed (period-instrument) tradition, John Eliot Gardiner, Roger Norrington, and others, takes the symphonies faster and lighter, with period or period-style instruments, revealing inner detail and bringing the sound closer to Schubert's own orchestras, an approach that particularly suits the early symphonies and offers a bracing alternative in the late ones.

### 20.3 The Chamber Music Tradition

The chamber music, the _Trout_ Quintet, the late quartets, the String Quintet, is performed by the great string quartets and chamber ensembles, often with guest artists for the quintets.

**The ensembles.** The string quartet tradition runs through the great quartet ensembles of the twentieth and twenty-first centuries: the Amadeus Quartet (warm, weighty, a classic older tradition); the Alban Berg Quartet (intense, polished, a modern reference, especially in "Death and the Maiden"); the Hagen Quartet (refined and probing); the Takács Quartet (intense and clear); the Quartetto Italiano; the Emerson Quartet; and, among recent ensembles, the Pavel Haas Quartet (whose String Quintet, with a guest cellist, is celebrated). For the _Trout_ Quintet, the great pianists are central (the piano part is prominent): Sir Clifford Curzon (with members of the Vienna Octet) and Sviatoslav Richter (with the Borodin Quartet members) made classic readings, and Alfred Brendel and many chamber groups have fine versions. For the String Quintet (with its two cellos), the historic Pablo Casals Prades recording of 1952 is a touchstone, alongside the great quartets with guest cellists (the Melos Quartet with Rostropovich, the Pavel Haas Quartet with Ishizaka, and others).

### 20.4 The Piano Tradition

The piano music, the sonatas (especially the late three), the Impromptus and Moments Musicaux, the _Wanderer_ Fantasy, was long neglected and is, in its modern recognition, largely the achievement of a handful of great pianists who made the case for it.

**The pianists.** The rehabilitation of the sonatas owes much to Artur Schnabel (an early champion) and, above all, to Sviatoslav Richter, whose spacious, intense, sometimes extreme readings (the famously slow, repeat-observing D 960 first movement) revealed the late sonatas' scale and made the case for their greatness; and to Alfred Brendel, who recorded the sonatas and the shorter works across a long career, combining structural clarity with lyrical warmth, and who wrote illuminatingly about the music, the central modern Schubert pianist for many. Other great Schubert pianists include Wilhelm Kempff (a warm, singing complete sonata cycle); Mitsuko Uchida (poetic and refined); Radu Lupu (warm and inward, a beloved D 960 and Impromptus); Maurizio Pollini and Murray Perahia (distinguished in the _Wanderer_ Fantasy and the late sonatas); Imogen Cooper (a lyrical intelligence); and Paul Lewis, whose complete late-sonatas cycle is a celebrated modern benchmark. Maria João Pires, Krystian Zimerman, and many others have left distinguished readings.

### 20.5 How to Use the Recorded Legacy

A few principles for navigating this vast legacy, drawn from the book's practice throughout.

**Hear more than one reading of the great works.** The deepest Schubert works, the cycles, the late sonatas, the String Quintet, the late symphonies, are so rich and so open to interpretation that no single recording exhausts them, and comparing readings is one of the great pleasures and deepest lessons of close listening. The book has repeatedly recommended pairs: a baritone and a tenor _Die schöne Müllerin_ ; a slow and a flowing D 960; a monumental and a HIP _Great_ C major; Fischer-Dieskau and Hotter in _Winterreise_. These comparisons teach you how much of what you hear is the performer's reading of an inexhaustible text, and they deepen your understanding of the music itself.

**The voice-type and the instrument matter.** In the Lieder especially, the choice of voice (baritone, tenor, soprano, mezzo) profoundly shapes a song or cycle, and there is rarely a single "correct" choice; exploring different voice-types in the same work is illuminating. Similarly, in the symphonies and chamber music, the choice between modern-orchestra grandeur and historically informed lightness, or between different ensembles' conceptions, opens different facets of the music.

**Use the great scholar-performers and annotated editions.** For the Lieder, Graham Johnson's Hyperion Schubert Edition (complete songs with commentary) is an unmatched resource for the listener who wants to explore in depth; Ian Bostridge's _Winterreise_ book (Chapter 7) is a model performer-scholar companion; Alfred Brendel's writings illuminate the piano music. These resources turn listening into understanding.

**Build outward from the anchors.** This book covered about fifty works; the recorded legacy contains hundreds more. Having learned to hear Schubert's voice (Chapter 18), use the recordings to explore beyond the anchors, the many songs the book did not cover, the early symphonies, the piano trios, the Masses, the dances, the four-hand music, with the listening skills you have built. The recorded legacy is the door to the whole catalogue, and the next and final chapter points the way into it.

### Chapter Summary

The Schubert we know is, to an unusual degree, a Schubert constructed by performers, since so much of his music reached the public only after his death, and some he never heard at all. This chapter surveyed the performance traditions through which the modern world has come to know him, organized by the four great regions of his output. The Lieder tradition centers on the singer-pianist partnership: Dietrich Fischer-Dieskau (the reference baritone) and Gerald Moore (the supreme accompanist) above all, with a rich tradition of singers (Hotter, Wunderlich, Schreier, Goerne, Gerhaher, Bostridge; Schwarzkopf, Ameling, Baker, Ludwig, Fassbaender, von Otter) and the indispensable Hyperion Schubert Edition (Graham Johnson). The symphony tradition turns on the handling of scale, from the monumental Furtwängler and Böhm and the electrifying Kleiber (_Unfinished_) to the chamber-scaled Abbado and the historically informed Gardiner. The chamber tradition runs through the great quartets (Amadeus, Alban Berg, Hagen, Takács, Pavel Haas) and, for the quintets, classic pianists and cellists (Curzon, Richter, Casals). The piano tradition is largely the achievement of the pianists who rehabilitated the long-neglected sonatas, Schnabel, Richter, Brendel above all, with Kempff, Uchida, Lupu, Lewis, and many others. Beyond the lineages, the chapter offered principles for navigating the legacy: hear more than one reading of the great works, attend to voice-type and instrument, use the scholar-performers and annotated editions, and build outward from the book's anchors into the whole catalogue (the consolidated starter list of first recordings to live with is in Chapter 21). The final chapter completes the book by pointing the way into that wider listening life, with a reading list, that first-recordings list, and the journey onward.

* * *

---

## Chapter 21: Where to Go Next — and the Schubert Recordings to Live With

You came to this book because you had met Schubert once, in _Close Listening: Classical Music_ , through two songs from _Winterreise_ and the _Trout_ Quintet, and sensed there was much more. There was. Across twenty chapters you have listened closely to about fifty works spanning Schubert's whole life, from the teenage "Erlkönig" to the late String Quintet, and you have learned to hear the voice that runs through all of them, the move by thirds, the major-minor switch, the long melody, the song-instrumental interpenetration, the heavenly length, the piano as co-narrator, the holding-together of beauty and darkness. And the six habits you arrived with, that Schubert is sentimental, that he is minor, that the catalogue is small, that he was unknown, that the Lieder all sound alike, that there is nowhere to start, you have dismantled one by one along the way, not because the book told you to but because you listened past them. This final chapter is the handoff: it points you onward, into the hundreds of works the book did not cover, with a curated reading list, a first-recordings-to-live-with list, and a sense of where the journey goes from here.

The book has covered fifty works; Schubert wrote nearly a thousand. What you have now is not a finished knowledge but a key, the listening skills and the recognition of the voice that let you explore the rest on your own. This chapter is about using that key: about which recordings to make your own, which books to read if you want to go deeper, and which directions to walk in the vast catalogue that still awaits you. It closes, too, by placing Schubert in the larger _Close Listening_ journey, looking back to the composers you have met and forward to those still to come.

### 21.1 The First Recordings to Live With

If you take nothing else from this book, take a handful of recordings and live with them, return to them, let them become part of your inner life. Here is a starter set, drawn from the book's recommendations, organized as a foundation for a Schubert listening life (the fuller performance lineages behind these names are mapped in Chapter 20). These are not "the best" recordings in any absolute sense; they are excellent, central, and a sound place to begin, and you will add and revise as you explore.

**The Lieder (the heart):**

* _Winterreise_ , Dietrich Fischer-Dieskau / Gerald Moore (Deutsche Grammophon), the central reference; and, when you want a darker, weightier reading, Hans Hotter / Gerald Moore.
* _Die schöne Müllerin_ , Fritz Wunderlich / Hubert Giesen (the radiant tenor classic) or Fischer-Dieskau / Moore (the baritone narrative).
* _Schwanengesang_ and a recital of individual songs, Fischer-Dieskau / Moore, or explore the Hyperion Schubert Edition (Graham Johnson) for the individual songs with commentary.

**The piano music:**

* The late sonatas (D 958, D 959, D 960), Alfred Brendel, with Sviatoslav Richter (the monumental D 960) or Paul Lewis (the modern cycle) as a contrast.
* The Impromptus and Moments Musicaux, Brendel, Radu Lupu, or Mitsuko Uchida.
* The _Wanderer_ Fantasy, Pollini, Richter, or Perahia.

**The chamber music:**

* The String Quintet in C major, Pablo Casals (Prades) or the Pavel Haas Quartet with a guest cellist, the work many love above all others.
* "Death and the Maiden" Quartet, the Takács or Alban Berg Quartet.
* The _Trout_ Quintet, Curzon (classic) or Brendel (clear), the sunny one.

**The symphonies:**

* The _Unfinished_ , Carlos Kleiber / Vienna Philharmonic (electrifying).
* The _Great_ C major, Furtwängler or Böhm (monumental) and a HIP reading (Gardiner) for contrast.
* A complete cycle for the early symphonies, Abbado / Chamber Orchestra of Europe or Böhm.

This is a year's worth of deep listening, or a lifetime's. Start with whichever region drew you most, the Lieder if the songs moved you, the late sonatas or the String Quintet if the late style did, and let it lead you outward.

### 21.2 Where to Go in the Catalogue

The book covered about fifty works; here is a map of the rich territory it could not, for the listener ready to explore with the skills now in hand.

**More Lieder.** This is the largest unexplored region, around six hundred songs, of which the book covered a few dozen. Beyond the cycles and the anchors, explore: the Mignon and Harper songs from Goethe's _Wilhelm Meister_ (dark, searching late-Goethe settings); the Mayrhofer settings (Schubert set many poems by his friend Johann Mayrhofer); the great individual songs the book could not fit, "Du bist die Ruh," "Nacht und Träume," "An die Musik" (Schubert's hymn to music itself), "Der Tod und das Mädchen" (the song behind the quartet), "Im Frühling," "Auf dem Wasser zu singen," and hundreds more. The Hyperion Schubert Edition is the great guide; or simply follow your ear from song to song. The Lieder are inexhaustible, and the listening skills you have built, the piano as co-narrator, the form-to-poem match, the major-minor switch, apply directly to all of them.

**More piano music.** Beyond the late sonatas and the Impromptus: the earlier and middle sonatas (the D 850, the lovely D 894); the dances (hundreds of waltzes, Ländler, and ecossaises, the social music of the Schubertiade); and the four-hand music (the great F minor Fantasy D 940, the _Grand Duo_ , and much else), a whole rich repertoire for two players.

**More chamber music.** Beyond the anchors: the other late quartets and the early ones; the two piano trios (the Op. 99 in B-flat, D 898, and the Op. 100 in E-flat, D 929, both great late works the book could only mention, the slow movement of the E-flat trio is among Schubert's most famous); the _Arpeggione_ Sonata; and the octet (a large, genial, Schubertiade-spirited chamber work).

**More orchestral and choral music.** The early symphonies (Chapter 15) reward fuller exploration; and beyond the symphonies lie the Masses (the Mass in A-flat D 678 and the Mass in E-flat D 950 are the great ones), the part-songs, and the incidental music to _Rosamunde_. The operas and Singspiele, mostly unsuccessful and rarely performed, are the one region even enthusiasts often skip, but they contain fine music for the curious.

Wherever you go, the key is the same: listen for the voice. The Schubert of an unfamiliar song or trio or Mass is the same Schubert whose fingerprints you learned in Chapter 18, and recognizing them is the pleasure and the orientation that the book has given you.

### 21.3 The Reading List

For the listener who wants to go deeper into Schubert's life and music, here are the books the volume has drawn on, the voice DNA of this very book, organized by what each offers.

**Biography:**

* Brian Newbould, _Schubert: The Music and the Man_ (1997), the source-of-record for the music-and-life balance, especially strong on the instrumental music and the symphonies (Newbould is also the scholar who completed several of Schubert's unfinished symphonic works).
* Christopher H. Gibbs, _The Life of Schubert_ , Cambridge University Press, 2000, the best concise modern biography, reliable and good at separating documented fact from legend.
* Lorraine Byrne Bodley, _Schubert: A Musical Wayfarer_ , Yale University Press, 2024, the contemporary biographical synthesis, incorporating the latest scholarship.
* Maurice J. E. Brown, _Schubert: A Critical Biography_ (1958), the older classic, still valuable.

**The Lieder:**

* Susan Youens's trilogy, _Schubert: Die schöne Müllerin_ (1992), _Retracing a Winter's Journey: Schubert's Winterreise_ (1991), and _Schubert's Late Lieder_ (2002), the scholarly source-of-record for the song cycles and the late songs, combining poetic and musical insight for serious listeners.
* Ian Bostridge, _Schubert's Winter Journey: Anatomy of an Obsession_ , Knopf, 2015, the great performer-scholar's song-by-song meditation on _Winterreise_ , the ideal companion for living with the cycle.

**The instrumental music:**

* Charles Rosen, _The Romantic Generation_ , Harvard University Press, 1995, the analytical voice for the piano and chamber music, on Schubert's harmonic language and large-scale forms.
* Alfred Brendel's essays on Schubert (in his collected musical writings), the pianist's-eye view from the artist who did most to establish the sonatas' greatness.

**The primary sources:**

* Otto Erich Deutsch, _Schubert: A Documentary Biography_ (English edition translated by Eric Blom), the great compendium of the surviving documents, the letters, the friends' memoirs, the contemporary notices, in their own words. To read the documents themselves, rather than a biographer's paraphrase, is to feel how much of what we "know" about Schubert rests on a handful of fond and partial recollections, and to practice, for yourself, the documentary discipline this book has tried to model.

For the cause-of-death question (Chapter 19), the medical-historical reassessment is found in the work of Anton Neumayr and others on the illnesses of composers. And the documentary films, the BBC Schubert programs, and Ian Bostridge's filmed _Winterreise_ , are worth seeking out for the listener who wants the music brought to life on screen.

### 21.4 The Close Listening Journey

This is the sixth single-composer volume in the _Close Listening_ series, and it is worth placing Schubert in the larger journey, looking back and forward.

You came to Schubert, most likely, through _Close Listening: Classical Music_ , the introductory volume on classical music as a whole, and perhaps through some of the single-composer volumes that preceded this one: _Beethoven_ , _Mozart_ , _Chopin_ , _Bach_. Each gave you a composer in depth, and each connects to Schubert. Beethoven was the overwhelming presence of Schubert's Vienna, the master he idolized, was buried near, and measured himself against (the C-minor "Tragic" Symphony, the reach for symphonic drama); the contrast between Beethoven's motivic argument and Schubert's lyrical expansiveness has run through this book. Mozart was the classical master on whose symphonies and operas Schubert was raised, and whose grace echoes in the early symphonies and the Fifth especially. Chopin was the younger contemporary whose character pieces refined the short lyric form Schubert helped found (the Impromptus and the Nocturnes are cousins). And Bach was the great contrapuntal master whose study Schubert sought out at the very end of his life, wanting to strengthen his fugue. Schubert sits among these masters as the great melodist and song-composer, the hinge between the Classical and the Romantic, the composer who made the German song a major art and let song flow into all his music.

Looking forward, the series continues with further single-composer volumes, Brahms and Tchaikovsky among those planned. Brahms in particular is a direct heir of Schubert: he revered Schubert, collected and edited his manuscripts, and his own songs, chamber music, and late piano pieces grow from Schubertian roots; the listener who has absorbed this volume will hear Schubert in Brahms everywhere. Tchaikovsky, too, the great melodist of the Russian Romantic tradition, shares Schubert's gift for the long, singable, emotionally direct tune. The journey through the _Close Listening_ series is a journey through the central tradition of Western music, composer by composer, and Schubert is one of its essential figures, the great song-composer, the melodist's melodist, the young master who, in thirty-one years, made music of a depth and beauty that the world is still discovering.

### 21.5 A Closing Word

A final reflection, to close the book. Schubert died at thirty-one, having written nearly a thousand works, much of it unperformed and unrecognized in his lifetime, some of it (the String Quintet, the _Great_ C major) unheard even by him. The world came to know him slowly, over the century after his death, through the advocacy of those who recognized what had been lost, Schumann, Mendelssohn, Liszt, Brahms, and the performers and scholars who followed. We are the inheritors of that long recognition, and the music that took a century to be valued is now among the most beloved in the world.

What you have gained from this book, if it has done its work, is not just knowledge of fifty works but a relationship with a composer, the ability to hear his voice, to recognize his hand, to be moved by his beauty and his darkness held together, to follow him from the galloping terror of "Erlkönig" through the frozen journey of _Winterreise_ to the suspended serenity of the String Quintet's Adagio. That relationship is the real gift, and it does not end with the book. Schubert's catalogue is vast and you have only begun; the recordings await; the songs and sonatas and quartets you have not yet heard will reward you for the rest of your life. Go and listen. Put on the _Winterreise_ , or the String Quintet, or the B-flat sonata, and listen closely, the way this book has tried to teach you, and let the music of a man who died at thirty-one, in mortality's shadow, having made beauty that holds darkness within it, become part of your own life. That is what close listening is for, and Schubert, of all composers, rewards it. The friend who walked you through his life and music now steps back; the music remains, and it is yours.

### Chapter Summary

This closing chapter handed the reader off to a Schubert listening life of their own. It offered a first-recordings-to-live-with list across the four regions of the output, the Lieder (Fischer-Dieskau / Moore, Wunderlich, the Hyperion Edition), the piano music (Brendel, Richter, Lewis, Lupu), the chamber music (Casals and the great quartets), and the symphonies (Kleiber, Furtwängler, Abbado, Gardiner), as a foundation to build on. It mapped the vast territory the book could not cover, the hundreds of further Lieder, the piano trios and other chamber music, the dances and four-hand music, the Masses, so the reader can explore with the listening skills now in hand, listening always for the voice collected in Chapter 18. It gave a reading list, the biographies (Newbould, Gibbs, Byrne Bodley, Brown), the Lieder studies (Youens, Bostridge), the analytical writing (Rosen, Brendel), and the primary-source compendium (Deutsch), the voice DNA of this book. And it placed Schubert in the larger _Close Listening_ journey, looking back to Beethoven, Mozart, Chopin, and Bach, and forward to Brahms and Tchaikovsky, situating Schubert as the great melodist and song-composer, the hinge between Classical and Romantic. The book closes by reflecting that what the reader has gained is a relationship with a composer, the ability to hear his voice and be moved by his beauty and darkness held together, and by sending the reader onward into a lifetime of close listening to the music of a man who died at thirty-one and whose vast catalogue the world is still discovering.
